The computational cost will be m times larger than the cost with a scalar-valued function. The advantage of the forward mode is that the computer memory storing 

(xj , D vxj ) can be released once the computations at all of the child nodes of 

xj are completed. This can be beneficial in practice since the computational graph is often very large. The drawback is that, if the full gradient or Ja-cobian is needed, then we have to set v to each of ei’s for i ∈ [n]. The total computational cost will be n times larger than the cost of directional deriva-tive (or Jacobian-vector product) above, which is expensive when n is very large. We will show next that the reverse mode of automatic differentiation can address this issue. 

Reverse Mode to Compute Gradient and Jacobian 

We begin by directly computing the gradient ∇f (x) of a scalar-valued func-tion f : Rn → R. (The directional derivative Dvf (x) = ⟨∇ f (x), v ⟩ can be easily computed by vector inner product afterwards for any given v.) The main difference between the reverse mode and the forward mode is that the reverse mode does not compute anything other than the values of 2.3. AUTOMATIC DIFFERENTIATION 69

The result is ∇f (x) = ( e2, e −2, 0) .The advantage of the reverse mode is that the computational cost of the full gradient is only a small multiple (often about 4 to 5) of what needed to evaluate a scalar-valued function f : Rn → R. In contrast, the forward mode requires O(n) times of the computational cost for f . Note that the so-called back-propagation in deep learning community is a special case of the reverse mode automatic differentiation. For vector-valued function f :

Rn → Rm, the computational costs to evaluate the full Jacobian ∇f (x) using the forward and reverse modes become similar as m increases though. One issue with the reverse mode is that all the values computed in the forward sweep need to be stored, and the computer memory storing yj cannot be released until it has been used by all the parent nodes of xj in the reverse sweep. Therefore, the memory requirement for reverse mode of automatic differentiation may be higher in practice.

# 2.4 Deterministic Optimization Algorithms 

In this section, we present several representative algorithms for deterministic optimization, namely, the objective function has an analytic form without any randomness or expectation, and its derivatives at all orders can be accu-rately evaluated, say by automatic differentiation. This may be more clear by comparing the deterministic optimization (2.44) and stochastic optimiza-tion (2.101) below. The general form of deterministic optimization is written as 

min  

> x∈Ω

f (x), (2.44) where Ω is a closed subset of Rn and the objective function f : Rn → R is possibly non-convex. We assume that f is differentiable and its gradient ∇f

is Lipschitz continuous with some Lipschitz constant L > 0 (often referred to as “smooth” in the optimization community). It is worth noting that we usually assume f is globally Lipschitz in Rn with L, but this can be often relaxed to being Lipschitz on a bounded domain of interest, as we will explain in the derivations later. This assumption is mild and held in the majority of problems we encounter in deep learning. With the smoothness assumption, we have the following important in-equality which is frequently used in convergence proofs of optimization al-gorithms. 

Proposition 2.4.1. Suppose f : Rn → R is continuously differentiable and 

∇f is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant L > 0. Then 

f (y) ≤ f (x) + ⟨∇ f (x), y − x⟩ + L

2 |y − x|2 (2.45) 

for all x, y ∈ Rn.Proof. For any x, y ∈ Rn, we define ϕ : R → R by 

ϕ(t) = f ((1 − t)x + ty ).

Since f is differentiable, we have 

ϕ(1) = ϕ(0) +  

> ∫10

ϕ′(t) dt, 2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 75 which implies that 

f (y) = f (x) +  

> ∫10

⟨∇ f ((1 − t)x + ty ), y − x⟩ dt. 

Subtracting f (x) + ⟨f (x), y − x⟩ on both sides, we obtain 

f (y) − f (x) − ⟨∇ f (x), y − x⟩ = 

> ∫10

where the second inequality is because ∇f is Lipschitz continuous. 

2.4.1 Algorithms for Unconstrained Optimization 

If the optimization problem (2.44) is unconstrained, namely, Ω = Rn, our major task is to find a critical point of f according to the first-order necessary condition. To this end, we consider the following iterative scheme: 

xk+1 = xk + αkvk, (2.46) where k = 0 , 1, 2, . . . is called the iteration counter , αk > 0 the step size (or 

learning rate which is commonly used in the deep learning community), and 

vk is a descent direction of f at xk in the kth iteration. Here by descent di-rection we meant that ⟨∇ f (xk), v k⟩ < 0 (we will discuss descent direction in detail later in this section). In the developments of optimization algorithms like (2.46), the key is to determine proper vk and αk such that the sequence 

{xk} generated by (2.46) satisfies |∇ f (xk)| → 0 as k → ∞ .

Gradient Descent Method and Line Search 

We first show the construction of the gradient descent method, with an automated step size selection strategy called backtracking line search, to illustrate the ideal features and potential issues of an optimization algorithm. In the gradient descent method, the easiest step is to set up the descent direction vk. We temporarily assume that vk is a unit vector since our goal is to determine a direction for the moment. In particular, we want to find vk

such that the directional derivative Dvk f (xk) can be negative and as small as possible, so we can attain fastest decay of f along this direction. This 76 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

easily renders the following conclusion: 

vk = arg min 

> |v|=1

Dvf (xk)= arg min 

> |v|=1

⟨∇ f (xk), v ⟩

= −∇ f (xk)/|∇ f (xk)|.

Therefore, we can just use vk = −∇ f (xk) and let the step size αk be re-sponsible of the scaling as we show in detail later. Since we use the negative gradient as the descent direction, we have the iterative scheme 

xk+1 = xk − αk∇f (xk), (2.47) and call this iterative scheme (2.47) the gradient descent method .We remark that the choice vk = −∇ f (xk) in the gradient descent method (2.47) just ensures vk to be a descent direction, which merely simplifies our discussion below. In fact, we can prescribe any c > 0 and choose any vk as long as ∇f (xk)vk ≤ − c|∇ f (xk)|2 for all k, our derivations below still work. Determining a suitable step size αk in (2.46) and (2.47) is particularly important, as it affects the convergence and stability of the algorithm in practice. Large step sizes result in faster progress but may cause the se-quence {xk} in (2.47) to diverge or highly fluctuate. Small step sizes make the sequence {xk} progress slowly and the algorithm work very inefficiently. To find the proper step size at iteration k, it is intuitive to look for the α

that minimizes f along the ray xk + αv k for α ≥ 0. To this end, we can set the line search function ϕk : R → R as ϕk(α) := f (xk + αv k) and solve this one-dimensional optimization problem to obtain αk = arg min α≥0 ϕk(α). A gradient descent method with such chosen step sizes is called steepest descent method . However, despite ϕk is a univariate function, finding its minimizer involves evaluations of f and ∇f , and the minimization problem is not easy to solve. Another common approach is using a constant step size α > 0 such that αk = α for all k yields convergence. In fact, we can show that for any α ∈ (0 , 2/L ), where L is the Lipschitz constant of ∇f , the sequence 

{xk} generated by (2.47) satisfies |∇ f (xk)| → 0. However, the value of L

is often unknown and difficult to estimate, and we cannot determine the proper interval for α in practice. There are a number of different strategies for the algorithm to determine the step sizes automatically without much knowledge about the objective function f . We consider one of the widely used strategies called backtracking line search to find αk. The goal of this line search strategy is to find a step size αk such that it satisfies the following Armijo condition for some prescribed c ∈ (0 , 1) :

f (xk + αkvk) ≤ f (xk) + cα k∇f (xk)vk. (2.48) 2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 77 In the gradient descent method (2.47), we have vk = −∇ f (xk) and hence the last term in (2.48) reduces to −cα k|vk|2.In general, as long as vk is a descent direction, we know ∇f (xk)vk < 0,and (2.48) implies that f (xk +αkvk) ≤ f (xk). Hence, (2.48) is also called the 

sufficient descent condition . It is useful to interpret the Armijo condition (2.48) in the following way: By using the line search function ϕk(α) = 

f (xk + αv k) again, we can see that the Armijo condition (2.48) effectively requires 

ϕk(α) ≤ ϕk(0) + cαϕ ′

> k

(0) . (2.49) The right-hand side of (2.49) is a straight line passing through (0 , ϕ k(0)) with slope cϕ ′

> k

(0) < 0 (note that the tangent line of f at (xk, f (xk)) corresponds to the straight line passing through (0 , ϕ ′

> k

(0)) with more steep slope ϕ′

> k

(0) < 0). Since f (x) is bounded below by f ∗, this line ϕk(0) + cαϕ ′

> k

(0) must intercept with ϕ(α) at some ˆα > 0, and the step size αk should be between 0 and this 

ˆα. Therefore, the Armijo condition (2.48) ensures decreasing the objective function value and meanwhile prevents the step size αk from being too large. From (2.49), we can see that it holds true as long as α is sufficiently small. However, the step size αk should not be too small in order to just satisfy (2.49). This is because that small step sizes cause slow improvements of the iterates xk’s and the algorithm needs many more iterations to converge, reducing the overall computational cost efficiency. Therefore, a line search strategy may also include a curvature condition such as 

ϕ′

> k

(α) ≥ ˆcϕ ′

> k

(0) (2.50) for some prescribed ˆc ∈ (c, 1) . This condition prevents αk from being too small. As a consequence, a line search strategy also need an implementable procedure to find a step size αk that satisfies both (2.49) and (2.50). The combination of the Armijo condition (2.48) and the curvature condi-tion (2.50) is called the Wolfe condition , which is one of the most commonly used conditions for line search. Next, we show how backtracking line search works to automatically find such step sizes. In backtracking line search, we manually set an upper bound ¯α > 0 of step sizes in the gradient descent method and the backtracking rate ρ ∈ (0 , 1) 

arbitrarily. Then, at any iteration k, we set αk = ¯ αρ lk where 

lk = arg min 

> {

f (xk + αkvk) ≤ f (xk) − cα k|vk|2. (2.52) If (2.52) is satisfied, we decide to use this αk; otherwise, we replace αk with 

ρα k, commonly written as αk ← ρα k in pseudo code, and try (2.52) again. 78 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

This process is repeated until (2.52) is satisfied and we will accept that αk

as our step size at iteration k. The gradient descent algorithm with this backtracking line search strategy is summarized in Algorithm 2.4.1.

O(1 /k ). This result is often considered the best convergence result for gen-eral smooth non-convex optimization problems. Meanwhile, the algorithm itself does not require much knowledge about the problem: We do not need to struggle with the choice of step sizes because the line search can find them automatically. Moreover, the convergence is guaranteed regardless of the hyperparameter (the parameters that need manually selected in an algorithm) values as long as they are in the specified ranges, which are independent of the problem. By contrast, recall that if we use a constant step size αk ≡ α ∈ (0 , 2/L ), then we need to know the Lipschitz constant L of ∇f , which is difficult to estimate and varies with different f .Despite the aforementioned promising properties of Algorithm 2.4.1, we are often more interested in the practical efficiency of an optimization algo-rithm. The reason is that, from practical perspective, it is more appealing to have a significantly faster empirical convergence (as this reduces time, resources, and power to obtain a solution) at the cost of some freedom in hyperparameter setting or convergence rate guarantee. Gradient descent method is known to converge extremely slowly with ill-conditioned objec-tive function f , even if f is strongly convex (in which case the ratio L/μ ,where μ is the strong convexity parameter of f , is large). Therefore, exten-sive works have been devoted to build more efficient optimization algorithms, particularly for large-scale optimization problems. Next, we consider several optimization methods to mitigate the ill-conditioning issue of optimization problems by exploiting second-order information of objective functions. 

Newton’s Method and Quasi-Newton Methods 

The gradient descent method is easy to implement as the descent direction 

vk is simply chosen to be the negative gradient −∇ f (xk) at each iterate 

xk. However, the gradient descent method can be very slow in convergence even if the step sizes are chosen as in the steepest descent method. This is mostly due to the ill-conditioning of the Hessian of the objective function. We now consider its opposite extreme, the Newton’s method , which is more 2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 81 structurally complicated than most descent methods but is one of the fastest optimization methods in terms of convergence rate. Newton’s method proceeds with the typical form descent methods as 

xk+1 = xk + αkvk, (2.59) where the key is in the selection of the Newton’s direction , which we denote by: 

vNk := −(∇2f (xk)) −1∇f (xk). (2.60) The idea of the Newton’s direction is that the update xk+1 should be the minimizer of a second-order approximation to the original objective function at xk:

f (x) ≈ ˜fk(x) := f (xk) + ∇f (xk)( x − xk) + 12 (x − xk)⊤∇2f (xk)( x − xk).

Taking the gradient of ˜fk above with respect to x, we can see that xk+1 

given in (2.59) is indeed the stationary point of ˜fk, provided that ∇2f (xk)

is invertible. As the Hessian provides the second-order information of the objective function near xk, it is expected that the Newton’s direction vk

in (2.60) is more effective than only using the first-order information as in gradient descent methods. As we can see, the Newton’s direction (2.60) involves the inverse of the Hessian ∇2f (xk). Therefore, if ∇2f (xk) is singular, the Newton’s method cannot work. Furthermore, if ∇2f (xk) is not positive definite, then it is possible that vNk defined in (2.60) is not a descent direction. These issues can often be remedied by using line search strategy of step sizes, trust-region technique, Gauss–Newton approximation, and etc. As our goal here is to motivate the idea and demonstrate the convergence of the Newton’s method and its important variant later, we simply assume that vNk exists and is a descent direction. We also notice that the Newton’s step vNk in (2.60) can be very expensive to compute as it requires the inverse of the Hessian matrix, as the computa-tional cost is typically cubic in n. We will address this issue later and just temporarily assume that we have vNk for each iteration. Now we consider the local convergence behavior of the Newton’s method with vNk and constant step size αk. By local convergence, we meant that for a local minimizer x∗ satisfying the sufficient optimality condition (i.e., 

∇f (x∗) = 0 and ∇2f (x∗) ≻ 0), we can show that the Newton’s method converges if the initial guess is close enough to x∗. We admit that these conditions seem strong and not practical, however, they often appear to be the best convergence guarantee that have been established for non-convex problems. This specific convergence result of the Newton’s method is given as follows. 82 2. NETWORK TRAINING

If x0 is sufficiently close to x∗, then xk → x∗ as k → ∞ at a quadratic rate (to be specified in the proof). 2. Moreover, |∇ f (xk)| → 0 as k → ∞ at a quadratic rate. Proof. We notice that

For large-scale problems, i.e., n is at millions or higher, it is in-tractable to find the inverse of ∇2f (xk) as this requires a computational cost of O(n3). Instead of directly finding the inverse, we can just compute 

vNk by solving 

∇2f (xk)v = −∇ f (xk). (2.66) This is in the form of a standard linear system

where Q is symmetric positive definite (when we assume ∇2f (xk) in (2.66) is positive definite, which is true when xk is sufficiently close to x∗ where 

∇2f (x∗) ≻ 0). There have been a substantial amount of numerical methods developed to solve large linear system like (2.67). Here we only present the method called conjugate gradient (CG), which is one of the most classic and efficient methods for solving (2.67). The standard CG method for solving linear systems is given in Algorithm 2.4.2. Note that the CG method converges in

In practice, we often get very close to the true solution of Qx = b in √n

iterations, depending on the condition number of Q (the ratio of the largest and smallest eigenvalues of Q). If the condition number is too large, there are also many pre-conditioning modifications to the CG method such that the ill-conditioning issue can be mitigated [105]. If the Hessian is sparse, the computational cost can be further reduced. Hence, CG is a practically viable approach to estimate vNk by solving (2.66). In summary, while the Newton’s method is computationally intractable in its original form for large-scale problems, there are many workarounds that produce inexact Newton’s methods which are efficient for such problems. The example using CG to estimate the Newton step above, which is called the Newton–CG method, is one of such methods. We remark that, these inexact Newton’s methods generally do not compute or store the Hessian matrix, but only some Hessian-vector multiplications. Therefore, they are often termed as Hessian-free methods despite they use second-order deriva-2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 85 tives of f .There is another class of Hessian-free methods known as quasi-Newton method , which are often considered as the first choice for solving smooth non-convex optimization problems of small to moderate sizes. The main idea of quasi-Newton methods is as follows. Suppose the iterate xk+1 is close to xk, then we have 

∇f (xk) = ∇f (xk+1 ) + ∇2f (xk+1 )( xk − xk+1 ) + o(|xk − xk+1 |)

≈ ∇ f (xk+1 ) + ∇2f (xk+1 )( xk − xk+1 ).

Rearranging this relation and assume the approximation is accurate, we obtain 

∇2f (xk+1 )( xk+1 − xk) = ∇f (xk+1 ) − ∇ f (xk). (2.69) If we could find a matrix Bk ∈ Rn×n to mimic the role of the Hessian 

∇2f (xk) for every k, then we can rewrite (2.69) as

A drawback of the BFGS method (and all quasi-Newton methods) is that they need to store a possibly dense n×n matrix Hk (even if the true Hessian is sparse), which hinders their application to large-scale problems. There is a variant called limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) that uses curvature information from only the most recent iterations (between 3 and 20) to construct the approximation of Hk. We refer interested readers to [105] for more details. When an iterate xk is sufficiently close to a local minimizer x∗

with ∇2f (x∗) ≻ 0, the BFGS method is shown to converge at a superlinear rate, i.e.,

lim

for some constant γ ∈ (0 , 1) [105]. 

2.4.2 Algorithms for Constrained Optimization 

In this subsection, we present several algorithms for constrained optimiza-tion. We remark that they are rarely used in network training because we often avoid to form such large-scale optimization problems as constrained ones which are much more challenging. However, these algorithms provide inspiring ideas of leveraging unconstrained optimization algorithms for solv-ing constrained ones. They can be useful when we want to enforce additional constraint and regularization on network parameters or design adaptive net-work architectures. A major class of approaches to solving constrained optimization prob-lems is replacing the problem with a sequence of unconstrained optimization problems. These unconstrained problems are designed such that their so-lutions converge to a solution of the constrained one. We first present a commonly used algorithm in this case. 2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 87 

Quadratic Penalty Method 

We consider optimization problems with equality constraints (2.17), which we recall below: 

min  

> x∈Rn

f (x), (2.74a) s.t. h(x) = 0 . (2.74b) The first algorithm we introduce is called the quadratic penalty method .This method modifies the constrained problem (2.74) as an unconstrained problem 

min  

> x∈Rn

Qγ (x), (2.75) where the quadratic penalty function Qγ is defined by 

Qγ (x) := f (x) + 12γ |h(x)|2 (2.76) and γ > 0 is called the penalty parameter . This penalty parameter γ is used to penalize the violation of the constraint h(x) = 0 : The smaller γ is, the heavier the penalty is. Therefore, it makes sense to set a sequence of penalty parameters {γk} such that γk → 0+ as k → ∞ . Meanwhile, for each 

k, we solve the unconstrained minimization problem (2.75) with γ = γk and some initial guess to obtain a solution xk, and expect that the sequence of solutions {xk} converges to a solution of the original constrained problem (2.74). If we choose γk and an initial guess wisely, it is possible that the unconstrained problem min x Qγk can be solved quickly. Now we describe the convergence property of the aforementioned quadratic penalty method.

Remarks. We have several remarks about Algorithm 2.4.3. First, it is practical that xk in line 2 of Algorithm 2.4.3 can be found provided certain smoothness conditions on f and h. More specifically, notice that γk > 0 and 

∇Qγk (x) = ∇f (x) + 1

γk

h(x)⊤∇h(x).

If both f and h⊤∇h are Lipschitz continuous, then so is Qγk . Thus, there exists an algorithm, such as the gradient descent method with backtracking line search (Algorithm 2.4.1), that can find xk in finitely many steps from any initial guess xk, 0. Note that, however, h⊤∇h(x) may not be Lipschitz continuous in many cases, e.g., h(x) = x2. It is Lipschitz if h is an affine function though. Second, we often choose γk to be decreasing and ϵk non-increasing, but eventually need γk → 0+ and ϵk → 0+ as k → ∞ . A commonly used method is to scale γk and ϵk by some prescribed constants in (0 , 1) .Third, a typical choice of the initial guess is xk+1 ,0 = xk. But better choices may exist. Last but not the least, we notice that Algorithm 2.4.3 is terminated when xk is “satisfactory” which sounds very subjective. In practice, we terminate when all of γk, |h(xk)|, and |∇ Qγk (xk)| (all these quantities can be evaluated) are sufficiently small. Lagrangian Method 

We consider an alternative method for equality constrained optimization problems based on the Lagrangian function: 

l(x, λ ) = f (x) + λh (x), (2.80) where f, h ∈ C2. The Lagrangian method is given by 

xk+1 = xk − αk(∇f (xk) + λk∇h(xk)) ⊤, (2.81a) 

λk+1 = λk + βkh(xk)⊤, (2.81b) 92 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

where k is the iteration counter and αk, β k > 0 are step sizes. The transposes  

> ⊤

are to make vector shapes consistent to our convention, i.e., x ∈ Rn is a column vector and λ ∈ Rm is a row vector. Before we discuss the property of the Lagrangian method (2.81), we show an intuitive way to memorize this method as follows. For any x ∈ Rn, we notice that 

max  

> λ∈Rm

max

l(x, λ ).

Therefore, the Lagrangian method (2.81) can be thought of as gradient descent for minimizing l(x, λ ) with respect to x and gradient ascent for maximizing it with respect to λ. Since only gradients and Jacobians are needed in (2.81), we call it a first-order Lagrangian method. Now we want to examine the convergence property of the Lagrangian method (2.81). We simplify the analysis by assuming constant step sizes 

αk = α and βk = β for some α, β > 0. By rescaling λ, we can also assume 

β = α without loss of generality. In this case, the Lagrangian method (2.81) can be thought of as a fixed point iteration of form zk+1 = F (zk), where 

zk := ( xk, λ k) ∈ Rn+m is a column vector that concatenates x and λ, and

Lemma 2.4.7. A point z∗ = ( x∗, λ ∗) satisfies the Lagrange conditions 

∇f (x∗) + λ∗∇h(x∗) = 0 , (2.83a)

if and only if z∗ is a fixed point of F .

|zk+1 − z∗| ≤ κ|zk − z∗| ≤ r

and |M (zk+1 , z ∗)| ≤ κ ∈ (0 , 1) . By induction, we know zk converges to z∗

at a linear rate. 

Augmented Lagrangian Method 

In Section 2.2.3, we introduced a variant of the Lagrange function, called the augmented Lagrangian function , to derive the second-order sufficient conditions of constrained optimization problems. There is also an associ-ated method, called the augmented Lagrangian method , to solve constrained optimization, which we discuss next. It is worth noting that the augmented Lagrangian method is also related to the quadratic penalty method. The difference is that, with the Lagrange multiplier, the augmented Lagrangian method can effectively mitigate the ill-conditioning issue of the quadratic penalty method. 2.4. DETERMINISTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 95 To gain some inspiration about the construction of the augmented La-grangian method, we first recall the equality constrained optimization prob-lem: 

min  

> x∈Rn

f (x), (2.88a) s.t. h(x) = 0 . (2.88b) We also recall that, in Theorem 2.4.6 where we showed the convergence of the quadratic penalty method, the accumulation point of the sequence 

{h(xk)⊤/γ k} is in fact the Lagrange multiplier λ∗ corresponding to the accumulation point x∗ of the sequence {xk}. Hence, we would expect 

h(xk) ≈ γkλ∗ for k sufficiently large. In the quadratic penalty method, we need γk → 0+ (in order to obtain h(xk) → 0) which causes the ill-conditioning of ∇2Qγk . In the augmented Lagrangian method, this issue can be mitigated so that γk does not need to approach to 0 very fast while still ensuring that h(xk) tends to 0 quickly. To see this, we recall that the augmented Lagrangian function is defined by the following “hybrid” of the Lagrangian function and the quadratic penalty function corresponding to the problem (2.88): 

lA(x, λ ; γ) := f (x) + λh (x) + 12γ |h(x)|2. (2.89) Taking gradient of lA with respect to x, we have 

∇xlA(x, λ ; γ) = ∇f (x) + λ∇h(x) + 1

γ h(x)⊤∇h(x)= ∇f (x) + 

> (

γ h(x)⊤)

∇h(x).

Suppose k is sufficiently large, and we use xk to denote the minimizer of 

lA(x, λ k; γk), then we would expect to get 

λk + h(xk)⊤

γk

≈ λ∗,

or equivalently 

h(xk)⊤ ≈ γk(λ∗ − λk),

when (xk, λ k) is close to (x∗, λ ∗). If λk → λ∗, then λ∗ −λk → 0, and therefore we may not need γk → 0 as fast as in the quadratic penalty method. This also suggests that we make the following update 

λk+1 = λk + h(xk)⊤

γk

If we keep the setting of the quadratic penalty method, i.e., γk → 0+,and assume that {λk} is bounded, then we can prove that the augmented Lagrangian method also ensures convergence, as shown below. 

Proposition 2.4.9. Let (¯ x, ¯λ) be any accumulation point of {(xk, λ k)} gen-erated by (2.90) and (2.91) with γk → 0. Then ¯x is a global minimizer of 

(2.88) .Proof. Denote f ∗ := min {f (x) : h(x) = 0 , x ∈ Rn}. Then by (2.91) for every k, we have 

lA(xk, λ k; γk) ≤ lA(x, λ k; γk)

for any x. Taking the infimum of the right-hand side over all x satisfying

While the convergence result given in Proposition 2.4.9, we notice that (2.91) is difficult to implement in practice since lA(·, λ k; γk) can be non-convex and we are generally unable to obtain its global minimizer xk. # 2.5 Stochastic Optimization Algorithms 

Deep learning often requires large scale of data samples, and as a conse-quence the objective functions are usually formulated in distinctive settings where stochastic optimization algorithms become more adaptive than con-ventional deterministic ones. In this section, we present consider stochastic optimization algorithms, including their formulations, motivations, advan-tages and drawbacks, and several widely used algorithms in network training nowadays. In particular, we focus on their applications in solving non-convex unconstrained optimization problems which are the prevalent formulation in training neural networks. 

2.5.1 Fundamentals of Stochastic Optimization 

Recall that, in the standard supervised learning problem, we are often given a dataset D = {(xi, y i) ∈ Rd × Rm : i ∈ [N ]} consisting of N sample pairs, where N can be very large like 10 6 or greater. Then our goal is to find the mapping u : Rd → Rm, parameterized as a deep neural network uθ

with parameter θ ∈ Rn, such that uθ(xi) is approximately equal to yi for all i ∈ [N ]. Suppose for simplicity we set the loss function ℓ : Rn → R of 

θ as the averaged squared differences between uθ(xi) and yi, and find the optimal θ by solving 

min  

> θ∈Rn

ℓ(θ) := 1

N

> N∑
> i=1

fi(θ), where fi(θ) = 12 |uθ(xi) − yi|2. (2.99) Since the gradient ∇θfi(θ) and the Hessian ∇2

> θ

fi(θ) can be calculated by automatic differentiation as we explained in Section 2.3, we know how to compute the gradient and Hessian of ℓ(θ). Therefore, in theory, we may ap-ply any of the unconstrained optimization algorithms introduced in Section 2.4.1, such as the gradient descent method, the Newton’s method, and the 2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 101 quasi-Newton methods, to find a (local) minimizer of ℓ. However, it turns out to be infeasible, or at least inefficient empirically, to employ a determin-istic optimization algorithm with a loss function of form (2.99). Let us dive into this and see the reasons next. To be consistent with the notations used throughout this chapter, we still denote by x the variable to be optimized (instead of θ) and by FN the loss function to indicate that it is the average of N terms, and write the optimization problem (2.99) as 

min  

> x∈Rn

FN (x) := 1

N

> N∑
> i=1

fi(x). (2.100) We remark that FN is also called the finite-sum objective, which is a kind of objective function often appears in deep learning problems. It is a special case of the general form of (unconstrained) stochastic optimization problem, which is typically of form 

min  

> x∈Rn

F (x) := Eξ[f (x; ξ)] , (2.101) where ξ is a random variable whose sample (realization) may be one or a subset of samples from D consisting of N samples independently drawn from some (possibly unknown) data probability distribution. For example, if we have a dataset D = {zi : i ∈ [N ]} called a batch ,where each sample zi renders a function fi of x, and ξ is set to be a sample drawn from D with uniform distribution, then (2.101) reduces to (2.100). One can also interpret (2.100) as an approximation of (2.101) by Monte Carlo integration (see Appendix A.1) with dataset D. The loss functions F

and FN are often called the expected loss and empirical loss , respectively. We generally focus on minimizing FN as (2.100) in the remainder of this section, and draw some connections to the problem of minimizing F as in (2.101) when needed. If we consider solving (2.100) as a deterministic optimization problem, then the standard gradient descent method proceeds as follows in the kth iteration: 

xk+1 = xk − αk

N

> N∑
> i=1

∇fi(xk), (2.102) where αk > 0 is the step size. We call (2.102) the batch gradient (or full gradient ) method. This is effectively applying the gradient descent method to (2.100). To obtain insights about stochastic optimization, we consider the basic stochastic update for solving (2.100) as follows in the kth iteration: 

xk+1 = xk − αk∇fik (x), (2.103) 102 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

where ik is an index randomly drawn from {1, . . . , N } and independent of any other ij ’s for all j < k . We call (2.103) the basic stochastic gradient 

method hereafter. Note that, xk in (2.103) depends not only on FN , the initial guess x0, and the step sizes αk’s, but also the random sequence {ij :

j < k }. Indeed, {xk : k ≥ 1} can be thought of as a stochastic process. By comparing the batch gradient (2.102) and the basic stochastic gra-dient (2.103), we may have the feeling that the former, deterministic opti-mization approach is more appealing. This is understandable as there are many sophisticated deterministic optimization algorithms available, and we can expect to obtain satisfactory convergence rate and solution quality by employing these algorithms. Moreover, the structure of FN in (2.100) allows us to leverage parallel computing technique which can significantly reduce per-iteration computational time in practice. However, the stochastic gradient method (2.103) has proven to be more efficient in deep learning applications from multiple perspectives [20]. For example, we consider a network training problem where the given dataset D

is poorly constructed: D consists of ten identical copies of a small dataset 

Dsub . (Although we often try to avoid such situation, it often occurs that the data samples collected are similar and repetitive in practice. Neverthe-less, we use this example just to provide an intuitive motivation that the batch gradient method may not be as effective as the basic stochastic gra-dient method.) In this case, the batch gradient method generates the same iterates by just using Dsub , but it literally takes 10 times of computational cost because it is using D in practice. By contrast, the computational cost and convergence behavior remain the same for the basic stochastic gradient method. This suggests that using all samples from D as in the batch gra-dient method (2.102) can be less efficient than the basic stochastic gradient method (2.103). Indeed, there have been numerous reports on the comparison of the basic stochastic gradient method and advanced deterministic optimization method in the literature. For example, some results posted by these reports show that the basic stochastic gradient method with a properly tuned constant step size dramatically outperforms the limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method, one of the most efficient and widely adopted optimization algorithm as a variant of the BFGS method (Section 2.4.1). More specifically, the basic stochastic gradient method is significantly faster than L-BFGS and attains satisfactory result in the first few epochs (the two methods are compared using objective function value versus epoch number—one epoch means the method uses all samples in D in expectation—which is a fair comparison that takes into account the difference of the per-iteration computational costs using the two methods) in a binary classification problem with a logistic loss function and the RCV1 dataset, while L-BFGS catches up and surpasses the basic stochastic gradient method a lot later [20]. This result appears to be surprising and counter-intuitive. However, the 2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 103

which has a unique minimum value at x = 0 .If we apply the batch gradient descent method (2.102) or more advanced deterministic optimization algorithm like L-BFGS to (2.106) starting from an initial guess x0 far away from the minimizer 0 (see Figure 2.6), they are effectively minimizing (2.107) and can generate a sequence of iterates {xk}

that stably converges to the minimizer x = 0 . By contrast, if we use the basic stochastic gradient method (2.103) with this initial x0, we can expect that the generated sequence of iterates {xk} still converge to 0 rapidly at in the first few epochs but at a much lower per-iteration computational cost: The batch gradient descent method computes the gradients of all fi’s, and it is N times of the cost by the basic stochastic gradient descent method which only computes the gradient of a randomly selected fi in each iteration. This example also suggests that the basic stochastic gradient method may only ensure convergence to a neighborhood of the minimizer if a con-stant step size is used. In other words, to ensure convergence to the mini-mizer, a diminishing step size policy may have to be employed. From theoretical perspective, we can also provide an estimate on the upper bounds of the total computational costs required by batch gradient descent and basic stochastic gradient for minimizing FN . Notice that FN is a strongly convex smooth function, the batch gradient descent is effectively the standard gradient descent method and known to have a linear convergence rate. More precisely, one can show that the batch gradient method converges at a linear rate: 

FN (xk) − F ∗ 

> N

≤ O(γk),

where F ∗ 

> N

> ∞ is the (possibly unknown) minimum value of FN over R and 

γ ∈ (0 , 1) depends on the ratio L/μ ≥ 1. Here L is the Lipschitz constant of ∇FN and μ is the strong convexity parameter of FN , i.e., 

FN (ˆ x) ≥ FN (x) + ∇FN (x)(ˆ x − x) + μ

2 |ˆx − x|2

for all x, ˆx ∈ Rn. We know that FN is more ill-conditioned and γ is closer to 1 if L/μ is larger. (In this particular example with FN defined in (2.107), 

L = μ = 1 . However, we remark that the ratio L/μ can be very large even if FN is strongly convex in real-world applications.) Due to the linear convergence rate above, one can easily deduce that to obtain an ϵ-optimal solution xk of FN , namely, 

FN (xk) − F ∗ 

> N

≤ ϵ

for any user-selected error tolerance ϵ > 0, the iteration number k is at the order O(log(1 /ϵ )) . As the per-iteration computational cost for the batch gradient descent method is N (as it needs to compute the gradients of 

f1, . . . , f N to get the gradient of FN ), the total computational cost to obtain an ϵ-optimal solution is proportional to N log(1 /ϵ ).2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 105 On the other hand, we can check the computational cost of the basic stochastic gradient descent method (2.103) when applied to minimize FN

given in (2.106). As FN is strongly convex, one can show that the basic stochastic gradient method converges at a sublinear rate: 

E[FN (xk)] − F ∗ 

> N

= O(1 /k ),

where xk is the kth iterate generated by the basic stochastic gradient descent method. The convergence rate above is only sublinear, which is much slower than linear asymptotically . Nevertheless, the sublinear rate above does not depend on the sample size N . As a result, the number of iterations needed to achieve an ϵ-optimal solution is O(1 /ϵ ). In terms of per-iteration cost, we know it is 1 as the basic stochastic gradient descent method only needs to compute the gradient of a randomly selected function among f1, . . . , f N .In summary, the total computational cost is proportional to 1/ϵ , which is again independent of N .Now we see that computational costs needed by the batch gradient de-scent (2.102) and the basic stochastic gradient descent (2.103) are propor-tional to N log(1 /ϵ ) and 1/ϵ , respectively. For fixed N , we know N log(1 /ϵ )

is much smaller than 1/ϵ asymptotically as ϵ → 0. However, the practical situation is that ϵ > 0 is often set to a sufficiently small number in advance, and the sample size N plays an important role in the convergence rate, es-pecially at the early stage of the optimization process. This explains why the basic stochastic gradient descent can be even faster than L-BFGS in convergence in the first few epochs. We also remark that an additional advantage of the stochastic opti-mization algorithms, such as the basic stochastic gradient descent method (2.103), is their applicability even when minimizing an expected loss func-tion F as in (2.101). By contrast, deterministic optimization algorithms, such as the batch gradient algorithm 2.102, become infeasible since we can-not compute the exact gradient of F in practice. These advantages are the reasons why stochastic optimization algorithms have become prevalent in deep learning research and applications nowadays. Next, we consider the convergence properties and computation complex-ities of stochastic gradient methods. Comparing the batch gradient method (2.102) and the basic stochastic gradient method (2.103), we see that their per-iteration computational costs are N versus 1, because (2.102) computes 

N gradients whereas (2.103) only computes one. As a convention in deep learning, we say a method completes one epoch when it uses up all N sam-ples in the given dataset D on expectation or equivalent. Therefore, the batch gradient method (2.102) completes one epoch in one iteration, whereas the basic stochastic gradient method (2.103) completes one epoch in every 

N iterations. We will also see the so-called mini-batch stochastic gradient 106 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

method which uses Nk samples of D (called a mini-batch) in the kth iter-ation where Nk is between 1 and N (for simplicity we assume Nk does not change in k), and therefore it completes one epoch every N/N k iterations. 

Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Method 

Now we investigate the convergence behavior of stochastic gradient method. As we pointed out earlier, stochastic gradient methods, such as (2.103), can be applied to find minimizers of either empirical loss (2.100) or expected loss (2.101). For the latter case, we denote by pξ the probability distribution of ξ.Therefore, we use F : Rn → R to denote the loss function that can be either of them in the remainder of this section. The general form of stochastic gradient (SG) method is given in Algorithm 2.5.1. We will provide more details about the steps in this algorithm next. 

Algorithm 2.5.1 Stochastic Gradient Method for Solving (2.100) or (2.101) 

Input: Initial guess x1 ∈ Rn. 

> 1:

for k = 1 , 2, . . . do  

> 2:

Generate a realization of ξk. 

> 3:

Compute g(xk, ξ k). 

> 4:

Choose a step size αk > 0. 

> 5:

Set xk+1 ← xk − αkg(xk, ξ k). 

> 6:

end for 

We first explain how Lines 2 and 3 of Algorithm 2.5.1 are implemented. In particular, we consider three scenarios in the implementation of SG method in either the expected loss case or the empirical loss case: The basic SG, the mini-batch SG, and the mini-batch SG with second-order in-formation (we called it second-order mini-batch SG for short hereafter). We remark that, in either of the three scenarios, {ξk : k ≥ 1} is a sequence of jointly independent random variables. Once ξk is determined, the vector 

g(xk, ξ k) is defined according to the specified SG method: 

g(xk, ξ k) =

ik ∈ { 1, . . . , N }, then we have ξk = ik in Line 2 and g(xk, ξ k) = ∇fik (xk)2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 107 in Line 3 of Algorithm 2.5.1. If the basic SG is applied to the expected loss F defined in (2.101), then ξk is a sample drawn from pξ and we set 

g(xk, ξ k) = ∇xf (xk, ξ k).In the mini-batch SG scenario, ξk refers to Nk indices ( Nk is called the mini-batch size which is user-selected in advance) drawn from {1, . . . , N }

uniformly for the empirical loss case, and g(xk, ξ k) is the average of the gradients of fi at the selected indices in ξk. See (2.108) for the explicit form. Similarly, in the expected loss case, ξk is a set of Nk samples indepen-dently drawn from pξ, and g(xk, ξ k) is the averaged gradients on the samples specified by ξk.The second-order mini-batch SG scenario is similar to the previous one except for an additional symmetric positive definite scaling matrix Hk. This matrix is computed based on different strategies of incorporating second-order information. A general rule on the selection of Hk is that −g(xk, ξ k)

should be a descent direction under expectation over ξk. We will provide more details on the construction of Hk later. Now we list a few assumptions required to establish the convergence of the stochastic gradient descent method in Algorithm 2.5.1. 

Assumption 2.5.2. Suppose the loss function F : Rn → R is continuously differentiable and the following assumptions hold. 1. F ∗ := inf x∈Rn F (x) is finite. 2. The gradient ∇F is L-Lipschitz continuous, namely, 

|∇ F (ˆ x) − ∇ F (x)| ≤ L|ˆx − x|, ∀ x, ˆx ∈ Rn.

3. There exist scalars μ and μG with 0 < μ ≤ μG such that for all k

∇F (xk) · Eξk [g(xk, ξ k)] ≥ μ|∇ F (xk)|2, (2.109) 

|Eξk [g(xk, ξ k)] | ≤ μG|∇ F (xk)|. (2.110) 4. There exist scalars M, M G ≥ 0 such that for all k

Eξk [|g(xk, ξ k)|2] ≤ M + MG|∇ F (xk)|2. (2.111) The first two conditions in Assumption 2.5.2 are mild and often satisfied in real-world applications. The condition (2.109) requires that, in expec-tation on ξk, the angle between g(xk, ξ k) and ∇F (xk) should be less than 

π/ 2. This is a typical requirement for −g(xk, ξ k) to be a descent direction in expectation. The condition (2.110) effectively requires the expectation (the first moment) of g(xk, ξ k) is at order O(|∇ F (xk)|) for every k. Combining with (2.109), we see that |g(xk, ξ k)| in expectation on ξk should be propor-tional to |∇ F (xk)|. The condition (2.111) requires the second moment of 108 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

g(xk, ξ k) to be upper bounded by some constant M > 0 plus O(|∇ F (xk)|2)

for every k.Note that, if g(xk, ξ k) is an unbiased estimate of F (xk), namely, 

Eξk [g(xk, ξ k)] = ∇F (xk),

then both of the conditions (2.109) and (2.110) hold with μ = μG = 1 . In the standard settings of many SG methods, such as the basic SG method, it can be shown that g(xk, ξ k) is indeed an unbiased estimate of ∇F (xk).Under Assumption 2.5.2, we have a simple bound on the difference be-tween Eξk [F (xk+1 )] and F (xk) at the kth iteration, as shown in the following lemma.

Second-order Stochastic Optimization Methods 

As pointed out in [20], second-order information (also called curvature in-formation) can be very helpful to eliminate some critical issues (such as ill-conditioning) with SG methods and significantly improve their perfor-mance. This is inspired by the substantial successes of second-order meth-ods, such as the class of quasi-Newton methods, in solving a broad range of deterministic optimization problems. We remark again that, by using second-order information, it does not mean that one needs to compute or store the Hessian of F , which can be infeasible, or at least extremely expen-sive in computation, for deep learning problems where the dimension n is at millions or even billions. We use a variant of the inexact Newton method to illustrate the use of second-order information in stochastic optimization algorithms. In particu-lar, we consider the subsampled Hessian-free inexact Newton method as an example. This method is summarized in Algorithm 2.5.2. 112 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

To implement this method, we note that in addition to ξk of size Nk for 

g(xk, ξ k) as in (2.108), we need to sample another realization ξHk of size N Hk

which yields a stochastic Hessian estimate 

H(xk; ξHk ) := 1

N HkN Hk∑

> i=1

∇2f (xk; ξHk,i ). (2.123) It was found that this method appears not sensitive to the noise in the esti-mate H(xk; ξHk ) as to g(xk; ξk). Therefore, N Hk is often chosen to be smaller than Nk, making the computational cost lower. Note that, H(xk; ξHk ) is not explicitly computed and stored here. Instead, the method employs the con-jugate gradient (CG) algorithm (Algorithm 2.4.2) to solve for sk ∈ Rn from the linear system (2.120), which only requires a number of matrix-vector multiplications (see Section 2.3 for details of computing such multiplica-tions by automatic differentiation). This is the reason that the method is Hessian-free. While the CG algorithm is proved to achieve the exact solution of (2.120) in finite steps provided H(xk; ξHk ) is positive definite, one usually terminates CG early once a prescribed maximum iteration number KCG is reached or the difference between the two sides of (2.120) is sufficiently small as specified by (2.121). The solution sk obtained by the CG algorithm for (2.120) plays the role of a descent direction, and a standard backtracking line search strategy is applied to determine the step size αk and consequently the next iterate xk+1 with ξk fixed. 

2.5.2 Practical Stochastic Optimization Algorithms 

While the standard stochastic gradient descent methods introduced above remain the fundamental approach, their limitations have motivated the de-velopment of adaptive optimization methods. In this subsection, we present several widely used adaptive optimizers in deep learning with some com-ments on their motivations and properties. However, at the time of writing, these methods do not have complete convergence guarantee when solving stochastic optimization problems with smooth non-convex objective func-tions under mild regularity assumptions. The investigation of their conver-gence behaviors is an active area of research nowadays. Nevertheless, these method appear to be faster and more efficient (and also stable) than the standard basic/mini-batch SG methods empirically. Therefore, they are often the first choice for training networks in deep learn-ing research and industrial applications. Meanwhile, it is very likely that more efficient and stable stochastic optimization algorithms will be invented continuously in the future. 2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 113 

Momentum Method 

For notation simplicity, we denote by gk the stochastic descent direction such as those in (2.108): 

gk := g(xk; ξk).

One of the two critical techniques to accelerate convergence is using the momentum. The idea of momentum is to incorporate a weighted moving average of past gradients to improve gk. This helps smoothing out the trajectory of the optimization, allowing the algorithm to converge faster by reducing oscillations. A basic stochastic gradient descent method with 

momentum is given by 

mk+1 = β1mk + (1 − β1)gk, (2.124a) 

xk+1 = xk − αkmk+1 , (2.124b) where mk ∈ Rn is the momentum at the kth iteration, and αk is the step size (learning rate). The momentum m1 initialized as 0, and the weight parameter β1 is often set to 0.9 in practice. Despite the speed improvement of (2.124), the step sizes αk still depend on the Lipschitz constant of the gradient of the objective function to be minimized, and they need to follow a diminishing policy for convergence. These bring additional trouble for practitioners to tune these step sizes for optimal performance. The adaptive methods that we present below have the feature of cancelling the Lipschitz constant and can often work properly with a fixed step size. These adaptive methods are effectively using a diagonal scaling matrix Hk, cf. (2.108), to incorporate second-order information of the objective function. 

Adaptive Gradient (AdaGrad) 

AdaGrad is one of the earliest adaptive methods proposed for large-scale and sparse optimization problems [33]. It introduces a raw second-order moment estimate in the form of a vector vk ∈ Rn in each iteration. Then the components of vk are used to scale their corresponding ones in gk. The AdaGrad scheme is given by 

vk+1 = vk + gk ⊙ gk, (2.125a) 

xk+1 = xk − αk

gk

√vk+1 + ϵ , (2.125b) where ⊙ is the componentwise product of two vectors, which results in a vector of the same dimension, and the parameter ϵ > 0 is usually set to 

10 −8 to bound the denominator away from 0. The square root and division in (2.125b) are also applied componentwisely. Therefore, the components of gk are scaled individually in (2.125b), and this ratio is invariant to the 114 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

scaling of the objective function by any positive constant (which changes the Lipschitz constant of the objective function). As a result, the step sizes αk

are not affected by such scaling and hence can remain fixed during iterations. AdaGrad is particularly effective in settings with sparse features, such as natural language processing, since infrequent parameters receive relatively larger updates. However, its main drawback is the denominator monoton-ically increases, which makes the ratio in (2.125b) tend to 0 rapidly. This often leads to premature convergence and stalled training progresses. 

Root Mean Square Propagation (RMSProp) 

RMSProp [145] was proposed as a practical improvement over AdaGrad to mitigate its aggressive learning-rate decay issue. The RMSProp scheme is given by 

vk+1 = β2vk + (1 − β2)gk ⊙ gk, (2.126a) 

xk+1 = xk − αk

gk

√vk+1 + ϵ , (2.126b) where β2 is often set to 0.99 , and v1 is initialized to 0. As we can see, the weighted sum in (2.126a) mitigate the issue of accumulating squared gradient, and hence the denominator vector in (2.126b) does not increase aggressively as in Adagrad. By focusing on recent gradients, RMSProp performs well in many ap-plications and has been widely used for training recurrent neural networks. However, RMSProp seems sensitive to hyperparameter choices. 

Adaptive Momentum Estimate (Adam) 

Adam optimizer [76] combines the momentum and RMSprop techniques to provide a more balanced and efficient optimization process. The key equa-tions governing Adam are as follows: Adam combines ideas from momen-tum methods and RMSProp by maintaining exponential moving averages of both first- and second-order moments of the gradients. Specifically, in the 

kth iteration, Adam updates the first moment estimate mk and raw second moment estimate vk by 

mk+1 = β1mk + (1 − β1)gk, (2.127a) 

vk+1 = β2vk + (1 − β2)gk ⊙ gk, (2.127b) which have initials m1 = v1 = 0 . Since such initials make mk and vk

biased towards 0, Adam attempts to correct such biases using their corrected versions 

ˆmk+1 = mk+1 

1 − βk

> 1

, (2.128a) 

ˆvk+1 = vk+1 

1 − βk

> 2

, (2.128b) 2.5. STOCHASTIC OPTIMIZATION ALGORITHMS 115 where both of the correction denominators in (2.128) tend to 1 as k → ∞ 

provided β1, β 2 ∈ (0 , 1) . Then Adam updates xk by 

xk+1 = xk − αk

ˆmk+1 

> √

ˆvk+1 + ϵ . (2.129) The hyperparameters are often set the same as before: β1 = 0 .9, β2 = 0 .999 ,

ϵ = 10 −8. The step size αk can be fixed as a constant and often set to 10 −3

which generally works well, but occasionally can be as small as 10 −5.Adam is widely adopted due to its fast convergence and robustness across a broad range of architectures and datasets. However, subsequent studies have shown that Adam may generalize worse than SG with momentum in some scenarios and may fail to converge in certain theoretical settings [116]. 

Adam with Decoupled Weight Decay (AdamW) 

A typical approach to control the value of x and stabilize optimization pro-cess is simply adding a Tikhonov regularization to the objective function. Namely, one can apply an optimization algorithm, say Adam, to minimize 

f (x) + λ 

> 2

|x|2 instead of f (x). Here λ is a hyperparameter to weigh the term 

|x|2. As we can see, the Tikhonov regularization tends to reduce the mag-nitudes of the components in x. Such modification, however, changes the objective function, its first- and second-moments, and most importantly, the optimal solution. AdamW [90] is an alternative approach by simply adding a decay term when updating x. More specifically, AdamW proceeds with the same com-putations (2.127)–(2.128) as in Adam, but only changes (2.129) to 

xk+1 = xk − αkλx k − αk

ˆmk+1 

> √

ˆvk+1 + ϵ , (2.130) which differs from (2.129) by an additional term αkλx k. Here λ is referred to as the decay weight and often set to 0.01 in practical implementations. In various empirical tests, AdamW appears to improve generalization and stability, particularly in large-scale models such as transformers, when compared to Adam. As a result, AdamW has become the default optimizer in many modern deep learning frameworks. 

Momentum Orthogonalized by Newton-Schulz (Muon) 

Muon [73] is an optimization approach that harnesses the singular values of the weight parameters in deep neural networks. Recall that a majority of building blocks in deep neural networks have affine operations of form 

W x + b, where W is a weight matrix. For ease of presentation, we call W a2-dimensional (2D) weight matrix and assume its size is dout × din hereafter. 116 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

Notice that, in all optimization algorithms we discussed above, W is reshaped as a vector and concatenated with b as a part of the total parameter vector θ ∈ Rn (we use x ∈ Rn in this chapter but it refers to the parameter 

θ ∈ Rn in network training). Therefore, the matrix structure of W is ignored during the optimization process of network training. However, the weight matrices play important roles in affine transforma-tion and have strong impact to the Lipschitz constant of the network which is effectively a mapping from its inputs to the corresponding outputs. There-fore, Muon proposes to regularize the gradients with respect to the weight matrices by orthogonalization. To this end, Muon employ a few Newton– Schulz (NS) iterations to the matrix momentum μM k + gk to normalize all singular values to 1. The Muon scheme is given by 

Mk+1 = μM k + gk, (2.131a) 

Ok+1 = NS( Mk+1 ; ( a, b, c ), ϵ, K NS ), (2.131b) 

xk+1 = xk − αλx k − αO k, (2.131c) where the weight parameter μ is often set to 0.95 , λ is set to 0.1, and the step size α is set to 0.001 .Now we explain the terms in (2.131). First, we note that gk in Muon (2.131) refers to the (stochastic) gradient of the objective function with respect to each individual weight matrix in the neural network. Muon can also be applied to update 4D convolutional parameters by flattening three of the four dimensions properly such that they become 2D matrices like the weights. The gradients with respect to other parameters in the form of scalar or vector are still updated using the previously presented algorithms, such as Adam and AdamW. Hence, we assume hereafter that gk is a 2D matrix of the same size as its corresponding weight matrix W ∈ Rdout ×din .The momentum Mk is a matrix of the same size. The key step in the Muon scheme is (2.131b). Taking any matrix M

of size m × n (without loss of generality, we assume m ≥ n hereafter) as input, this step returns a matrix O of the same size as an approximation of U V ⊤, where M = U ΣV ⊤ is the singular value decomposition (SVD) of 

M . We know that, SVD can factorize any real or complex matrix M in the form of U ΣV ⊤, where U ∈ Rm×n has n orthonormal vectors in Rm (i.e., the columns of U is a subset of an orthonormal basis of Rm), V is an n × n

orthogonal matrix, and Σ is an n × n diagonal matrix with non-negative diagonal entries called the singular values of M . Consequently, the columns of U and V are called the left and right singular vectors of M , respectively. See details about SVD in [49,148]. Therefore, the effect of the step (2.131b) is removing Σ from M = U ΣV ⊤ and approximating the orthogonal version 

O = U V ⊤ of M . Namely, O is the solution to 

min

A, B ∈ Rm×n. Due to these properties of Frobenius norm, one can easily find that the minimizer of (2.132) is U V ⊤. This is called the orthogonalization in Muon. However, SVD is computationally expensive and one may want to avoid using it to solve (2.132) in practice. The NS iteration is a method that can quickly approximate the solution to (2.132) and hence is employed by Muon in (2.131b). The idea of the NS iteration is as follows. We define a quintic polynomial 

ϕ : R → R of σ (here σ refers to a positive singular value of a matrix) defined by 

ϕ(σ) := aσ + bσ 3 + cσ 5. (2.133) We also extend this definition and apply ϕ to a matrix M :

Therefore, for the NS iteration to normalize all singular values to 1 rapid, we just need to find the coefficients (a, b, c ) of (2.133) such that a few itera-tions of ϕ can map any positive number σ to 1. seems to work even better and hence is currently the default setting in prac-tical implementations in popular deep learning programming packages such as PyTorch [107]. In the Muon method, the NS iteration in (2.131b) is often terminated if the maximum iteration number KNS (usually 5 is enough) is reached or the relative change of (a, b, c ) is smaller than ϵ (usually set to

We can also incorporate the idea of Nesterov’s momentum into the Muon method (2.131). This is often implemented by replacing Bk+1 in (2.131b) with gk + μB k+1 where μ is set to 0.95 by default. There are also several modifications to the step size in practical imple-mentations. In particular, the step size α is multiplied by (max(1 , d out /d in )) 1/2

in [73] and by 0.2(max( dout , d in )) 1/2 in [88], which appear to be efficient in large language model training empirically. 

# 2.6 Remarks and References 

2.6.1 Remarks on Optimization for Network Training 

This chapter covers most of the fundamental concepts in optimization the-ory, as well as a number of optimization algorithms and techniques. The focus is on non-convex smooth optimization problems, which are prevalent in modern deep learning. Indeed, there have been a substantial amount of re-sults on deterministic optimization methods developed in the past decades, including comprehensive convergence analysis and mature software pack-ages. However, they seem to be faltered in solving modern large-scale opti-mization problems [20]. By contrast, new stochastic optimization algorithms are being continuously invented in the deep learning community to address the challenges of such problems. Despite these algorithms generally do not have complete and rigorous convergence guarantees yet, their extraordinary efficiency makes them dominate in large deep network training nowadays. Besides the comments made in [20], we have several additional remarks on this phenomenon from the practical perspective. Training large-scale neural networks is known to be extremely resource demanding. It requires a substantial number of graphical processing units (GPUs) and huge amount of electricity power. Some large networks, such as those designed for mod-ern large language models, each have several billions to hundreds of billions of parameters, and the training often takes months on clustered systems of GPUs to complete. Given the current surge of artificial intelligence, rapid developments of new deep learning techniques, and fierce competitions be-tween different entities, it is not surprising that people prefer algorithms that are faster and more efficient to obtain satisfactory results in short time at the cost of slightly reduced solution quality. By contrast, traditional ap-proaches in the field of optimization focus on crafted algorithmic design, rigorous convergence analysis, tight complexity estimates with upper/lower complexity bounds, and flexible hyperparameter settings. Figure 2.7 shows an illustrative plot to explain the situation. In Figure 2.7, the horizontal line is the number of epochs which represent the prac-tical computational cost, and the vertical line is the loss function value on training dataset. The solid curve shows the performance of an optimization algorithm with sublinear convergence rate O(1 /k ), whereas the dashed curve 2.6. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 119 # epochs loss 

> k2k1

Figure 2.7: An illustrative comparison between the loss function value de-cays using two optimization algorithms. The first algorithm (solid curve) converges at a sublinear rate O(1 /k ), where k is the number of epochs, and the second algorithm (dashed curve) converges at a linear rate O(γk) for some γ ∈ (0 , 1) . The first algorithm has a fast initial convergence and prob-ably can reach a satisfactory solution after k1 epochs, whereas the second algorithm has a faster asymptotic convergence and will surpass the first al-gorithm after k2 epochs. is of an algorithm with linear convergence rate O(γk) with some γ ∈ (0 , 1) .In theory, it is natural that we prefer the second algorithm as its linear con-vergence is superior to sublinear rate and will converge to the minimum loss function value much faster asymptotically. However, given the two decay curves in Figure 2.7, a practitioner may prefer the first algorithm with sublinear rate (solid curve) due to its fast convergence at the initial stage. This is particularly reasonable if the result provided by this algorithm at the k1th epoch is already satisfactory. Despite the algorithm with linear rate (dashed curve) will outperform the first one after k2 epochs, the improvement of solution quality may not be enough to justify the substantial investment in additional computations. For example, a few percent improvement of evaluation measure on training dataset in a recommender system or generative pre-trained transformer (GPT) may not be noticeable to human, but the training time can be a 4 months ( k2) versus 1 month ( k1) difference in practice. In this situation, most practitioners would choose to use (at least first try) the algorithm with sublinear convergence rate. Due to the large scale and complex structure of deep neural networks, numerous factors can strongly influence the performance of a deep learning model. Among these, the choice of methodology and network architecture is often the most critical. For example, a moderately trained convolutional neural network can achieve much higher image classification accuracy than a dedicately trained fully connected network. In addition, the quality of the data samples, the choice of initialization, and the preprocessing pro-120 2. NETWORK TRAINING 

cedures all have a significant impact on the empirical performance of net-work training. Consequently, practitioners tend to prioritize these aspects when designing and training deep learning models, while typically selecting a widely used optimizer (such as Adam or AdamW) that is known for its efficiency and fast convergence in practice, without paying close attention to its theoretical convergence guarantees. 

2.6.2 References of Numerical Optimization 

Comprehensive introduction to optimization theory and applications can be found in [29]. For the fundamental theoretical results in convex analysis and optimization algorithms, we refer to [11, 13, 16, 19, 21, 102, 120]. General continuous optimization theory and algorithms are given in the classic books [14, 105]. The discussions on basic optimization theory and optimality conditions in Sections 2.1 and 2.2 largely follow [29]. Section 2.3 on automatic differen-tiation follows [105], with some extra details and explanations throughout, particularly on the computations of second-order derivatives. Section 2.4 on algorithms for deterministic optimizations mostly follows [14, 29, 105]. Fun-damentals of stochastic optimization given in Section 2.5.1 follow [20]. We provide an overview of the practical stochastic optimization algorithms in Section 2.5.2 based on online discussions and short tutorials as they just emerged in recent years. 3

# Deep Optimal Control 

Optimal control provides a rigorous mathematical framework for designing sequences of decisions that steer dynamical systems toward desired objec-tives, such as maximizing reward (or equivalently, minimizing cost), subject to physical and operational constraints. It plays a central role in a broad range of real-world applications. In this chapter, we begin with a systematic study of the fundamental concepts and core results of optimal control. In particular, we present the foundational theory of optimal control, including the Pontryagin Maximum Principle and the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, together with their proofs. We then extend these classical results to the optimal control of prob-ability densities, also known as mean-field control, motivated by the fact that modern deep learning systems often involve controlling a massive number of agents. As a result, many problems of interest in these situations can naturally be formulated as control problems over probability distributions. Despite the importance and widespread use of optimal control, classical methods suffer from severe scalability limitations in high-dimensional set-tings or in the presence of complex nonlinear dynamics. To address these challenges, we introduce a deep learning-based approach, known as the neu-ral ordinary differential equation (Neural ODE) method, as a powerful tool for solving a broad class of optimal control problems. This framework can be adapted to handle both standard optimal control problems and the prob-ability control problems. Finally, we demonstrate the strong potential of combining deep learning with optimal control for tackling emerging scien-tific computing problems, using the task of learning solution operators for evolution partial differential equations as a representative example. 

# 3.1 Optimal Control Theory 

In this section, we present a concise and systematic review of the foun-dational results in optimal control theory. We begin with the calculus of 121 122 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

variations and its role in deriving the Euler–Lagrange equations. We then introduce Hamiltonian dynamics, which offer a unifying and insightful per-spective on general control systems. Finally, we present the Pontryagin Maximum Principle and the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, together with rigorous proofs. 

3.1.1 Euler–Lagrange Equations 

Optimal control is a complex optimization problem with very different na-ture compared to standard optimization discussed in Chapter 2. In an opti-mal control problem, the subject to be optimized is a function, called control ,which is often represented as a mapping from time [0 , T ] (can be extended to [0 , ∞) or R, but we restrict most of our discussions to finite time window as this has sufficient generality) to a subset U of some multi-dimensional Euclidean space Rm. This subset U is called the admissible set of control .While optimal control shares a similar goal of seeking for the solution that minimizing or maximizing certain objective function, the theory and solution methods are very different from (but still related to) those in stan-dard optimization given in Chapter 2. As the subject to be optimized is a time-dependent control function u : [0 , T ] → U ⊂ Rm, we need new theory, analysis, and computational tools to solve optimal control problems. The basic approach is called the calculus of variations , and such method is known as the variational method . We use a classical example to intro-duce calculus of variations and Lagrangian functions, which further yield the Euler–Lagrange equations. Suppose that we are given a smooth function L : Rd × Rd → R. We want to find a curve x∗ : [0 , T ] → Rd that minimizes the functional 

I[x] :=  

> ∫T
> 0

L(x(t), ˙x(t)) dt, (3.1) with constraints x(0) = x0 and x(T ) = x1 for some prescribed x0, x 1 ∈ Rd.In (3.1), ˙x is the time-derivative of x (we can think of ˙x as the control for now). We often interpret x(t) as the position (in general we refer it as the state) and ˙x(t) as the velocity at time t. Similar to optimization, our first step is to find the optimality conditions of x∗. Namely, suppose that x∗ is a minimizer of I defined in (3.1), we want to know the conditions that x∗ must satisfy. These conditions to optimal control are analogues to the Lagrange conditions and KKT conditions to constrained optimization in Chapter 2. In the literature, the integrand L in (3.1) is called the Lagrangian func-tion . Note that this should be distinguished from the Lagrangian function of equality constrained optimization problems in Chapter 2, as they have dif-ferent definitions and purposes. We often write L as a function of x, v ∈ Rd
