3.1.2 Hamiltonian Dynamics 

The Euler–Lagrange equation (3.2) provides a necessary condition of mini-mizers of (3.1), where the constraints of x are set at the endpoints of [0 , T ].The velocity ˙x can be thought of as the control of x.Next, we introduce another important concept, called Hamiltonian dy-namics, which is closely related to the Euler–Lagrange equation (3.2) but more prevalent in general optimal control problems. In contrast to (3.2) which is a system of d second-order ODEs, the Hamiltonian dynamics form a system of 2d first-order ODEs. To this end, let x : [0 , T ] → Rd be given, we define another curve p :[0 , T ] → Rd, called the generalized momentum , as follows: 

p(t) := ∂vL(x(t), ˙x(t)) (3.9) for all t ∈ [0 , T ]. Our goal is to rewrite (3.2) as a system of 2d first-order ODEs of (x, p ).Note that, the conversion from the Euler–Lagrange equations to a Hamil-tonian dynamics generally requires that 

p = ∂vL(x, v ) (3.10) can determine a unique v given (x, p ). This can be achieved by assuming that, for example, ∂2

> vv

L(x, v ) is nonsingular at every x and v, so that the Implicit Function Theorem can be applied to show this requirement is sat-isfied. In the remainder of this section, we assume that this requirement on (3.10) is satisfied and we have its solution denoted by v(x, p ). Namely, 

p = ∂vL(x, v (x, p )) .

Definition 3.1.2 (Hamiltonian) . Let x, p ∈ Rd and v(x, p ) be the solution to (3.10), then we define H : Rd × Rd → R by 

H(x, p ) = p · v(x, p ) − L(x, v (x, p )) . (3.11) We call H the Hamiltonian function. 126 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

Similar to the Lagrangian function L above, we denote the partial deriva-tives of H as 

∂xH := ( ∂x1 H, . . . , ∂ xd H) ,∂pH := ( ∂p1 H, . . . , ∂ pd H) ,

where 

∂xi H = ∂H ∂x i

and ∂pi H = ∂H ∂p i

for every i = 1 , . . . , d . Now we ready to show that the Euler–Lagrange equation (3.2) can be converted to Hamiltonian dynamics using H.

Theorem 3.1.3 (Hamiltonian dynamics) . Let x be the solution to the Euler– Lagrange equation (3.2) and define p as in (3.9) . Then the pair (x, p ) : [0 , T ] → Rd × Rd solves the Hamiltonian dynamics:  

> {

˙x(t) = ∂pH(x(t), p (t)) ,

˙p(t) = −∂xH(x(t), p (t)) . (3.12) 

Moreover, H(x(t), p (t)) is a constant function of t.Proof. By the definition of Hamiltonian H in (3.11), where v = v(x, p ) solves 

p = ∂vL(x, v ), we have 

∂xH(x, p ) = p · ∂xv(x, p ) − ∂xL(x, v (x, p )) − ∂vL(x, v (x, p )) · ∂xv(x, p )= −∂xL(x, v (x, p )) (3.13) since p = ∂vL(x, v (x, p )) . Therefore, we have 

˙p(t) = ddt 

> (

∂vL(x(t), ˙x(t)) 

> )

= ∂xL(x(t), ˙x(t)) = ∂xL(x(t), v (x(t), p (t))) = −∂xH(x(t), p (t)) ,

where the first equality is obtained by taking derivatives on both sides of (3.9) with respect to t, the second equality is due to the Euler–Lagrange equation (3.2), the third equality is because p(t) = ∂vL(x(t), ˙x(t)) which implies ˙x(t) = v(x(t), p (t)) , and the last equality is due to (3.13). Furthermore, we have from the definition of H in (3.11) that 

∂pH(x, p ) = v(x, p ) + p · ∂pv(x, p ) − ∂vL(x, v (x, p )) · ∂pv(x, p )= v(x, p )

since p = ∂vL(x, v (x, p )) . Therefore, we have 

∂pH(x(t), p (t)) = v(x(t), p (t)) 3.1. OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY 127 for every t ∈ [0 , T ]. Again, since p(t) = ∂vL(x(t), ˙x(t)) implies ˙x(t) = 

v(x(t), p (t)) , we get 

˙x(t) = ∂pH(x(t), p (t)) .

At this point, we have shown that (x(t), p (t)) satisfies the system of ODEs (3.12). To show that H(x(t), p (t)) is a constant, we take its derivative with respect to t:

ddt H(x(t), p (t)) = ∂xH(x(t), p (t)) · ˙x(t) + ∂pH(x(t), p (t)) · ˙p(t)= − ˙p(t) · ˙x(t) + ˙ x(t) · ˙p(t)= 0 ,

where the second equality is due to (3.12). This completes the proof. 

Example 3.1.4. We consider an example of classical mechanics where the Lagrangian function is given by 

L(x, v ) = 12 m|v|2 − V (x), (3.14) where x is the position, m is the mass, and v is the velocity of an object. The function V : Rd → R is the potential energy. Hence, L in (3.14) is the difference between the kinetic energy and the potential energy. We also see that 

∂xL(x, v ) = −∇ V (x) and ∂vL(x, v ) = mv. 

Plugging these into the Euler–Lagrange equation (3.2), we obtain 

m¨x(t) = −∇ V (x(t)) .

Therefore, the Euler–Lagrange equation of the function (3.14) is the second-order ODE describing the Newton’s second law of motion. Furthermore, the generalized momentum is exactly the momentum in classical mechanics: 

p = ∂vL(x, v ) = mv, 

which implies 

v = v(x, p ) = pm .

Plugging x and v(x, p ) = pm into the Hamiltonian H (3.11), we obtain 

H(x, p ) = p · pm − L

> (

x, pm

> )

= |p|2

m − 12 m |p|2

m2 + V (x)= |p|2

2m + V (x),128 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

which is the sum of the kinetic energy and the potential energy. If we substitute p(t) = mv (t) back into H above, then we obtain 

H(x(t), p (t)) = 12 m|v(t)|2 + V (x(t)) .

By Theorem 3.1.3, we know H(x(t), p (t)) remains as a constant over t. This means that the sum of the kinetic energy and the potential energy does not change over time, namely, the total energy is conserved. We also notice that the Hamiltonian dynamics are  

> {

˙x(t) = ∂pH(x(t), p (t)) = p(t) 

> m

,

˙p(t) = −∂xH(x(t), p (t)) = −∇ V (x(t)) .

If V (x) := mgx , where x is the height and g is the acceleration due to gravity (approximately 9.81 m/sec 2 on earth surface), then there is ˙p(t) = −mg .

3.1.3 Pontryagin Maximum Principle 

The discussions above reveal some fundamental connections between La-grangian mechanics and Hamiltonian mechanics. Meanwhile, the Hamilto-nian dynamics demonstrate significant importance in control theory: The necessary condition of an optimal control can be described using the Hamil-tonian dynamics. This is our goal in this subsection. To begin with, let us consider an illustrative example of optimal control problem on commodity trading. Suppose we are a wheat trader with x1(t)

cash on hand and x2(t) units of wheat in stock at time t. At the initial time 

t = 0 , we have x1(0) = x1,0 and x2(0) = x2,0 for some given x1,0, x 2,0 ≥ 0.Let c > 0 be the cost of storing a unit of wheat for a unit of time. Assume for simplicity that we know the price q(t) of a unit amount of wheat at any time t. Now we need to find the optimal u(t), which is the rate of buying or selling wheat at time t (u(t) > 0 if buying and < 0 if selling), such that the total holdings at the prescribed end time T is maximized. To form this commodity trading problem mathematically, we notice that the dynamics of x(t) = ( x1(t), x 2(t)) ∈ R2 is given by  

> {

˙x1(t) = −cx 2(t) − q(t)u(t),

˙x2(t) = u(t), (3.15) with initial values x1(0) = x1,0 and x2(0) = x2,0. The reason of (3.15) is that, the rate of spending money, ˙x1(t), is equal to the cost of storing the wheat in stock x2(t) and expense of buying (or income by selling) wheat at the rate u(t); and the change rate of wheat in stock ˙x2(t) is equal u(t). The total holdings at time T is given by 

I[u] := x1(T ) + q(T )x2(T ), (3.16) 3.1. OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY 129 which is the sum of cash on hand x1(T ) and the worth of wheat in stock 

q(T )x2(T ) at time T . Therefore, the optimal control problem is to find the rate u : [0 , T ] → R such that the total holdings I defined in (3.16) is maximized under the constraint (3.15) with the initial value of x(0) = (x1(0) , x 2(0)) = ( x1,0, x 2,0). Here we allow u(t) to be any number for sim-plicity. In practice, we often require u(t) ∈ U at every t, where U is a subset of R (or Rm if u(t) is an m-dimensional vector). In what follows, we use 

UT := {u : [0 , T ] → U ⊂ Rm} to denote the admissible set of the control u

over the entire time window [0 , T ].The commodity trading example above represents a typical formation of general optimal control problems. More precisely, these optimal control problems are formulated as 

max 

> u∈U T

I[u] :=  

> ∫T
> 0

r(x(t), u (t)) dt + g(x(T )) , (3.17a) s.t. ˙x(t) = f (x(t), u (t)) , (3.17b) 

x(0) = x0, (3.17c) where the variable to be optimized is the control trajectory u ∈ U T .By comparing the commodity trading example above with (3.17), we see that the dynamics (3.15) corresponds to the ODE constraint (3.17b), the initial value x(0) = ( x1,0, x 2,0) corresponds to (3.17c), and the total holdings x1(T )+ q(T )x2(T ) in (3.16) is g(x(T )) in (3.17a), which is called the 

terminal reward (or terminal payoff). In (3.17a), we have an additional term ∫ T 

> 0

r(x(t), u (t)) dt that did not appear in the commodity trading example (3.16). This is the integral of the so-called running reward r : Rd × Rm → R,which can be easily merged into the terminal reward g as we will show later. Note that we did not include the time variable t in the ODE defining function f in (3.17b) explicitly and make it in the form of f (t, x (t), u (t)) .This is because the time variable t can be easily merged into x(t) by intro-ducing an additional component t with constant derivative 1. More precisely, we can define 

¯x(t) := ( t, x (t)) ,

¯f (¯ x(t), u (t)) := (1 , f (t, x (t), u (t))) ,

then there is 

˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u (t)) ⇐⇒ ˙¯x(t) = ¯f (¯ x(t), u (t)) 

and the right-hand side is still of the form (3.17b). The optimal control problem (3.17) is often termed as a fixed end-time and free endpoint problem, because the terminal time T is fixed and the end-point x(T ) is not. There is another variation of optimal control, called fixed 130 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

endpoint and free end-time problem, which has similar optimality theory as (3.17). We will cover that variation in this section as well. Next, we represent one of the most significant results about optimality condition of solutions to (3.17). This result is known as the Pontryagin Maximum Principle . We first give the definition of control Hamiltonian function, which will be used in the Pontryagin Maximum Principle and its proof. 

Definition 3.1.5 (Control Hamiltonian) . Let f and r be the ODE defin-ing function and the running reward function, respectively, in (3.17). The 

control Hamiltonian function H : Rd × Rd × U → R is defined by 

H(x, p, u ) := p · f (x, u ) + r(x, u ) (3.18) for any (x, p, u ) ∈ Rd × Rd × U .Comparing with the Hamiltonian function in (3.11), we see that the con-trol Hamiltonian function in (3.18) has the control u ∈ U ⊂ Rm as another variable, the control velocity v in (3.11) is replaced with a general ODE defining function f controlled by u in (3.17b), and the negative Lagrangian 

−L (where the integral (3.1) of L is to be minimized) is replaced with the running reward function r (to be maximized) in (3.17a). Now we provide two lemmas that are crucial in the Pontryagin Maximum Principle and its proof.

In order to prove the Pontryagin Maximum Principle rigorously, we need to be inclusive about all possible variations of a control trajectory. Theoret-ically speaking, the optimal control problem does not assume the control u

to be continuous in t. Therefore, we consider the following so-called simple variation which allows discontinuous changes of a control function u.

Before we prove the Pontryagin Maximum Principle, we also introduce the concept of co-state p : [0 , T ] → Rd for the state x : [0 , T ] → Rd in a control system. It is convenient to think p(t) ∈ Rd as a row vector (notice that it has the same dimension as x(t)) at every t and behaves like the Lagrange multiplier to the constraint formed as the initial value problem (3.19). The co-state is also called the adjoint function, and it satisfies the so-called adjoint equation, as shown below. 

Definition 3.1.10 (Co-state and adjoint equation) . Let x be the state solving the initial value problem (3.19) with control u. Then p : [0 , T ] → Rd

is called the co-state if it solves the following terminal value problem  

> {

˙p(t) = −p(t)∂xf (x(t), u (t)) − ∂xr(x(t), u (t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],p(T ) = ∇g(x(T )) . (3.28) The ODE in (3.28) is called the adjoint equation .

Pontryagin Maximum Principle without Running Reward 

The Pontryagin Maximum Principle provides a necessary condition of the optimal control. Therefore, its establishment requires the assumption that the given control is optimal (just like we assume a local minimizer attains the smallest objective function value in its neighbor in optimization when we derive the necessary optimality condition). Now our objective function I[u]

is given in (3.17a). We begin with the case where I only has the terminal reward g(x(T )) .

Lemma 3.1.11. The variation of terminal reward I[u] = g(x(T )) (assum-ing there is no running reward) in (3.17a) is given by

Again, we consider the optimal control problem (3.17) without running reward in the objective function. 

Theorem 3.1.12 (Pontryagin Maximum Principle) . Let u(·) be the optimal solution to (3.17) and x the corresponding trajectory satisfying (3.17b) and 

(3.17c) . Then there exists a function p : [0 , T ] → Rn such that (x, p ) satisfies the control Hamiltonian dynamics:  

> {

˙x(t) = ∂pH (x(t), p (t), u (t)) ,

˙p(t) = −∂xH (x(t), p (t), u (t)) , (3.32) 

with initial condition of x and terminal condition of p given by 

x(0) = x0 and p(T ) = ∇g(x(T )) .

Moreover, the following identity, known as the Pontryagin Maximum Prin-ciple , holds true for every t:

H(x(t), p (t), u (t)) = max  

> w∈U

Proof. Without the running reward r, we have the control Hamiltonian (3.18) as 

H(x, p, u ) = p · f (x, u ).

Hence (3.32) is the combination of the ODE of x given in (3.17b) and the adjoint equation of p given in (3.28). The initial condition of x and terminal condition of p are given in (3.17c) and (3.28), respectively. In addition, since u(t) ∈ U , we obtain the maximum principle (3.33). Notice that the Hamiltonian dynamics with control (3.32) has the same form as the one (3.12) without control. To memorize these dynamics, just keep in mind that they are nonlinear systems of first-order ODEs of the state and co-state pair (x, p ), and the defining function is (∂pH, −∂xH).Such dynamics structure is said to be in the symplectic form .

To investigate the property of C(t), we need the following topological lemma. 

Lemma 3.1.16 (Zeros of a vector field) . Let S denote a compact (closed and bounded) and convex subset of Rn, and q be an interior point of S.Suppose ϕ : S → Rn is a continuous vector field that satisfies the following strict inequalities: 

|ϕ(x) − x| < |q − x|, ∀ x ∈ ∂S. (3.51) 

Then there exists x∗ ∈ S such that ϕ(x∗) = q.Proof. We first consider a simple case where S = ¯B1(0) := {x ∈ Rn : |x| ≤ 

1}, which is the closed unit ball with center 0 and radius 1 in Rn, and 

q = 0 ∈ Rn is an interior point of ¯B1(0) . In this case, (3.51) reduces to 

|ϕ(x) − x| < |x|, ∀ x ∈ ∂ ¯B1(0) .

Squaring both sides above, we obtain 

|ϕ(x)|2 − 2ϕ(x) · x < 0,

which implies that ϕ(x) · x > 12 |ϕ(x)|2 ≥ 0 for all x with |x| = 1 .Since ¯B1(0) and ∂ ¯B1(0) are both compact in Rn and ϕ is continuous, we know there exist M, m > 0 such that 

ϕ(x) · x ≥ m and |ϕ(x)| ≤ M140 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

for all x ∈ ¯B1(0) (including the boundary of ¯B1(0) ). Choosing α ∈ (0 , 2m/M )

and defining ψ(x) := x − αϕ (x), we obtain 

|ψ(x)|2 = |x − αϕ (x)|2 = |x|2 − 2αϕ (x) · x + α2|ϕ(x)|2

≤ | x|2 − 2mα + α2M = |x|2 − α(2 m − αM ) < |x|2 ≤ 1

for all x ∈ ¯B1(0) . By Brouwer’s fixed point theorem, there exists x∗ ∈ ¯B1(0) 

such that ψ(x∗) = x∗. Namely, x∗ − αϕ (x∗) = x∗ or ϕ(x∗) = 0 = q.In the general case, we can always assume that after a translation of S

we have its interior point q at 0, and then a radial dilation of S centered at q = 0 converts S into ¯B1(0) . The combination of these two operations is a homeomorphism (a continuous mapping from Rn to Rn with continuous inverse), and we convert the general case to the simple case with S = ¯B1(0) 

as above. Now let us consider the optimal control problem with some given fixed endpoint x1 ∈ Rd. Again let T > 0 be the first time that the trajectory x

3.1.4 Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman Equation 

In this section, we consider a different approach to solving optimal control problems. This approach is based on the idea of dynamical programming (DP), which is to embed the given problem into a larger class of problems and solve this entire class all at once. Then the solution to the entire class can directly imply the solution to the original problem. 3.1. OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY 145 Recall that the optimal control problem with fixed time T and free end-point is given by

(3.69) Since u(t) ∈ U for every t, we know the supremum in (3.68) is indeed maximum and the equality holds true, yielding the HJB equation (3.63). 3.1. > {

u∗(s) := π(x∗(s), s )

This implies that u∗ is an optimal control to (3.61). At this point, we see that solving the HJB equation (3.63) can be an alternative approach to solving the optimal control problem, and the steps have been explained as above. However, we remark that the HJB equation (3.63) is a nonlinear first-order PDE of v, and it is further complicated due to the presence of maxi-mum which requires solving an optimization problem on U at every time t.In some special cases, the maximizer as a closed form and the maximum in 148 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

(3.63) can be eliminated. However, even without the maximum, this PDE can still be infeasible or at least difficult to solve due to its high dimension (the spatial dimension is d according to x) in practice. Nevertheless, the strong connections between the HJB equation (from the DP view) and the optimal control provide significant insights and analysis tools to study and solve control problems. 

# 3.2 Optimal Probability Density Control Theory 

Deep learning problems generally involve massive amount of data points or agents. For example, in a large multi-agent control system, the state of each agent can be represented as a vector with values describing its location, orientation, velocity, and acceleration. In many cases, these agents can be thought of as samples following some specific probability distributions. Typically, the learning problems are formulated as evolving these samples in time, which is equivalent to the evolution of the probability distribution they follow, such that certain objective function can be minimized or maximized in a given time horizon [0 , T ]. Therefore, such problems can be formulated as optimal control of probability density. In this section, we extend the theoretical results of classical optimal control in Section 3.1 to the optimal probability control scenario. Let Ω be an open subset of Rd, where d is the dimension of state vector (such as the concatenation of location, orientation, and acceleration of an agent mentioned above). In the remainder of this section, we generally refer a sample to be an agent. Suppose there are N agents with states 

{xi ∈ Ω : i = 1 , . . . , N } which follow some initial distribution p on Ω, i.e., 

xi ∼ p. Under a time-evolving control vector field u : Ω × [0 , T ] → Rd over a fixed time horizon [0 , T ], the states of the agents change according to 

˙xi(t) = u(xi(t), t ), i = 1 , . . . , N. (3.73) Each agent accumulates some running rewards during [0 , T ] and receives a terminal reward at time T . Unlike classical optimal control, these rewards 

depend not only this specific agent but also the collective behaviors of all agents. We provide a few examples of running and terminal reward functions below. We use ρ(·, t ) to denote the time marginal of the states xi(t) for every t,namely, xi(t) ∼ ρ(·, t ). Then (3.73) corresponds to the continuity equation 

∂tρ(x, t ) + ∇· (ρ(x, t )u(x, t )) = 0 (3.74) in Ω × [0 , T ]. For notation simplicity, we write ρ(·, t ) and u(·, t ) as ρt and ut

respectively, and often omit (x) in the integrals hereafter unless it is needed for clarification. 3.2. OPTIMAL PROBABILITY DENSITY CONTROL THEORY 149 We define the space of probability density functions on Ω:

P =

> {

p : Ω → [0 , ∞) : 

> ∫
> Ω

p(x) dx = 1 

> }

. (3.75) Furthermore, for the given terminal time T > 0, we define the space of time-evolving probability density functions as: 

PT = {ρ : Ω × [0 , T ] → R : ρ(·, t ) ∈ P, ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ]},

We assume that the admissible set of control vector field at any fixed time is 

U = {w ∈ C( ¯Ω; Rd) : Lip( w) ≤ MU , (w · n)|∂Ω = 0 }, (3.76) for some MU > 0, namely, w is MU -Lipschitz continuous in ¯Ω and satisfies the boundary condition w(x)·n(x) = 0 , where n(x) ∈ Rd is the outer normal of ∂Ω at x for all x ∈ ∂Ω. The boundary condition of w conserves the total probability density as 1. Then we define the admissible set of time-evolving control vector fields as 

UT = {u : ¯Ω × [0 , T ] → Rd : u(·, t ) ∈ U, ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ]}. (3.77) Note that (3.77) does not require u(x, ·) to be a continuous function of t,allowing u to change direction instantaneously and yielding broader range of applications of the results obtained below. Now we have the optimal probability control problem in its general form as 

max 

> u∈UT

I[u] :=  

> ∫T
> 0

R(ρt, u t) dt + G(ρT ), (3.78a) 

s.t. ∂tρt + ∇ · (ρtut) = 0 , ∀ (t, x ) ∈ [0 , T ] × Ω, (3.78b) 

ρ0 = p, ∀ x ∈ Ω, (3.78c) where I is the total reward functional, UT is the admissible set of control vector fields, and p is a given initial probability distribution, i.e., xi(0) ∼ p.In (3.78), R : P × U → R and G : P → R are the running and terminal functionals, respectively. We assume I∗ := max u∈UT I[u] to be finite. We have a few assumptions on the probability distributions and controls as follows, which ensure the derivations later can be carried out rigorously. 

Assumption 3.2.1. We make the following assumptions: (i) For all x ∈ Ω, ρ(x, ·) is differentiable in t on [0 , T ].(ii) For all t ∈ [0 , T ], ρ(·, t )u(·, t ) is differentiable in Ω.(iii) For all x ∈ Ω, ρ(x, ·)u(x, ·) has Lebesgue points in (0 , T ) everywhere. 150 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

(iv) For all w ∈ U , R(·, w ) is Fréchet differentiable in P in the L2 sense. (v) G(·) is Fréchet differentiable in P in the L2 sense. Assumption 3.2.1 (i) and (ii) can be relaxed to weakly differentiable as long as (3.74) is valid. Assumption 3.2.1 (iii) ensures ddt   

> ∫t
> 0

ρ(x, s )u(x, s ) ds =

ρ(x, t )u(x, t ) for all t ∈ (0 , T ). This is used in a step in the proof of Lemma 3.2.7 below (we will specify it in the proof). Assumption 3.2.1 (iv) and (v) allow functional derivatives of R and G at any p ∈ P , which are needed in the establishment of control Hamiltonian dynamics later. Notice that running and terminal reward functionals in real-world applications typically satisfy Assumption 3.2.1 (iv) and (v). 

3.2.1 Maximum Principle for Probability Control 

Now we establish a maximum principle (MP) for the optimal probability control problem (3.78) with any initial value p ∈ P in this subsection. As an analogue to the Pontryagin MP for classic optimal control in Section 3.1, the MP to be established here is a time pointwise necessary condition of the optimal solution u to (3.78) defined on the infinite-dimensional spaces 

PT and UT . To develop this MP, we first introduce the definition of adjoint partial differential equation (PDE) and control Hamiltonian functional. 

Definition 3.2.2 (Adjoint PDE) . Let u ∈ UT be a control vector field and 

ρ ∈ PT the corresponding evolutionary probability function solving (3.78b) with initial (3.78c). Define an evolution PDE by 

∂tϕt + ut · ∇ ϕt = − δδρ t

R(ρt, u t) (3.79) in Ω × [0 , T ]. We call (3.79) the adjoint PDE associated to (ρ, u ). We call ϕ

the adjoint function of (ρ, u ) if ϕ : Ω × [0 , T ] → R solves (3.79) with terminal condition ϕT = δδρ T G(ρT ).Throughout this work, we assume δδρ R(·, w ), δδρ G(·), and u in the control problem (3.78) are sufficiently smooth for any w ∈ U such that the following assumption holds. 

Assumption 3.2.3. An adjoint function ϕ : Ω × [0 , T ] → R for (3.78) with any initial p ∈ P exists. Note that the adjoint function ϕ has spatial variable x and time variable 

t, and the adjoint equation is an evolution PDE. By contrast, in classical optimal control, the adjoint function is a function of t only, and the adjoint equation is an ODE. 3.2. OPTIMAL PROBABILITY DENSITY CONTROL THEORY 151 

Definition 3.2.4 (Hamiltonian functional) . Let p ∈ P , w ∈ U , and f :Ω → R be a differentiable function, we define the Hamiltonian functional by 

H(p, f, w ) = ⟨p, w · ∇ f ⟩ + R(p, w ). (3.80) where R is the running reward functional in (3.78). Now we can verify that (ρ, ϕ ) induced by any u ∈ UT satisfies the sym-plectic control Hamiltonian dynamics, as summarized in the following propo-sition. 

Proposition 3.2.5 (Control Hamiltonian dynamics) . Let u ∈ UT be a con-trol vector field, ρ ∈ PT the corresponding evolutionary probability, and ϕ

the adjoint function of (ρ, u ). Then (ρ, ϕ ) satisfies the control Hamiltonian dynamics: 

> 

∂tρt = δδϕ t

H(ρt, ϕ t, u t) = −∇ · (ρtut),∂tϕt = − δδρ t

H(ρt, ϕ t, u t) = −ut · ∇ ϕt − δδρ t

R(ρt, u t)

(3.81) 

for all t.

2( t − T − 1) . (3.103) This implies that the optimal control vector field is 

ut(x) = xt − T − 1 (3.104) for all x and t. We will continue with this example to see its value functional and HJB equation in the next subsection. 

3.2.2 HJB Equation for Probability Control 

In this subsection, we derive the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation associated with the optimal probability control problem (3.78). To this end, we need to define value functional, which is an analogue to the value function in the classic optimal control. Note that this is a time-evolving functional defined on the cross space P × [0 , T ]. The definition of this value functional is as follows. 

Definition 3.2.11 (Value functional) . For any p ∈ P and t ∈ [0 , T ], define the time-evolving functional V by 

V (p, t ) = sup  

> u∈UT
> { ∫Tt

R(ρs, u s) ds + G(ρT )

> {

∂sρs + ∇ · (usρs) = 0 , ∀ s ∈ [t, T ],ρt = p. (3.106) We call H the value functional of (3.78). Note that, for any t, V (·, t ) : P → R is a functional on the infinite-dimensional space of probability density functions on Ω. By contrast, value function in classical optimal control is function on some subset of finite-dimensional Euclidean space. Now we are ready to establish the HJB equation of the value functional

# 3.3 Neural ODE Method 

While we have seen many important theoretical results for optimal control above, it remains a question on how to solve general control problems nu-merically in practice. In this section, we introduce an approach, called the 

neural ordinary differential equation method, to tackle this problem. We call it the Neural ODE method for short hereafter. We focus on the following general optimal control problem with fixed end-time T and free endpoint given in Section 3.1: 

max 

> u∈U T

I[u] := g(x(T )) , (3.130a) s.t.  

> {

˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u (t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],x(0) = x0

(3.130b) where the terminal reward function g : Rd → R, the defining function f :[0 , T ] × Rd × U → Rd including the control as an argument, and some initial value x0 ∈ Rd are given. The reason that we focus on (3.130) is that it covers the majority of optimal control problems in real-world applications. It is also partially due 162 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL         

> x(t)f˙x(t)
> tuθ
> uθ(t)
> f(t, x (t), u θ(t))
> ˙x(t)determines the instantaneous change of x(t)

Figure 3.1: The control system solved by the Neural ODE method. The control ODE is given by ˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u (t)) , where f : [0 , T ] × Rd ×U → Rd is determined by control problem, and the control u : [0 , T ] → U 

is parameterized as a deep neural network uθ with parameter θ. In the original Neural ODE method [27], the function f : [0 , T ] × Rd → Rd is parameterized as a deep neural network fθ with parameter θ, and the ODE is ˙x(t) = fθ(t, x (t)) .to a practical issue as we will use deep neural networks to approximate the control u(t), and it is sometimes difficult for such approximation to satisfy certain constraints exactly (such as arriving at some specified state precisely in the fixed endpoint optimal control problem). We also remark that the time variable t is back to f as an explicit input in (3.130) for ease of presentation below. Meanwhile, we do not have the running reward r in (3.130) as we can simply add an auxiliary variable as in (3.36) to take the running reward into account. The idea of the Neural ODE method is to parameterize the control func-tion u as a deep neural network uθ : [0 , T ] → U ⊂ Rm, where θ ∈ Rn

represents the vector consisting of its n network parameters. Now the optimal control problem (3.130) reduces to 

max  

> θ∈Rn

J(θ) := I[uθ] = g(x(T )) , (3.131a) s.t.  

> {

˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u θ(t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],x(0) = x0. (3.131b) Note that the variable to be optimized is no longer the time-dependent control function u : [0 , T ] → U . Instead, it is the vector θ ∈ Rn to be optimized, just like in standard optimization. Once the optimal value θ is found, the neural network uθ : [0 , T ] → U is (approximately) the desired optimal solution u to the control problem (3.130). The control system with control function uθ is illustrated in Figure 3.1. Given that the variable to be optimized is a vector θ, it is natural to em-ploy some gradient-based optimization algorithm we introduced in Section 3.3. NEURAL ODE METHOD 163 2.4. The key ingredient when using such algorithm is the gradient of the objective function J with respect to θ. Therefore, our main goal is to find 

∇θJ(θ) for the function J defined in (3.131). If we can compute ∇θJ(θ) at any θ, we can supply it to any gradient-based optimization algorithm to find the (local) maximizers of J(θ), which are (sub-)optimal network parameter 

θ of uθ. The Neural ODE method provides a means to compute ∇θJ(θ).To show how the Neural ODE method works, we first introduce two trivial auxiliary functions. The first one τ : [0 , T ] → [0 , T ] is the identity function: 

τ (t) = t

for all t ∈ [0 , T ] and hence it solves the initial value problem

Now we see that, to compute ∇θJ(θ) for any θ, we can just solve the initial value problem (3.131b) with this θ for x(T ), and then solve the ter-minal value problem (3.137)–(3.138) for pσ(0) . Then we obtain (3.139). We summarize the procedure to compute ∇θJ(θ) in Algorithm 3.3.1. Note that both of the initial and terminal value problems can be solved by well-developed numerical ODE solvers, such as the midpoint method and the Runge–Kutta method [24]. As ∇θJ(θ) can be evaluated, we can employ any gradient-based optimization algorithm to find a (local) maximizer of J(θ).

Algorithm 3.3.1 Compute ∇θJ(θ) for (3.131) in the Neural ODE method 

Input: Current θ ∈ Rn. 

> 1:

Solve x(T ) from the initial value problem:  

> {

˙x(t) = f (t, x (t), u θ(t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],x(0) = x0. 

> 2:

Solve p(0) = ( px(0) , p τ (0) , p σ(0)) from the terminal value problem:  

> {

˙p(t) = −p(t)∇z h(z(t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],p(T ) = ( ∇g(x(T )) , 0, 0) ,

where the explicit form of the adjoint equation is given in (3.137). 

Output: ∇θJ(θ) = pσ(0) .

Remarks on the Neural ODE Method 

We have several remarks on the Neural ODE method in solving optimal control problems. First, the Neural ODE method parameterizes the control u : [0 , T ] → U 

as a neural network uθ, the solution control is continuous in time since we almost always design neural networks as continuous functions in practice. The issue is that we cannot find the optimal control if it is not continuous in time, but a close approximation may be possible if the network architecture is expressive enough. Meanwhile, an advantage of using deep network to parameterize the control is that we can design the network properly such that it has strong representation power while its outputs are always in U

(e.g., U is a bounded set in Rm) automatically, avoiding explicit constraint enforcement when learning the control. Some examples of neural network design are given in Section 1.5.3. Second, the Neural ODE method was originally proposed in [27] as a continuous-time version of the residual network (ResNet) architecture, which can be thought of as the Euler scheme for solving ODEs with defining func-tion f parameterized as deep networks. More specifically, the ODE in [27] 3.3. NEURAL ODE METHOD 167 has a simple yet flexible form where f is directly parameterized as a deep neural network fθ : [0 , T ] × Rd → Rd with parameter θ ∈ Rn: 

> {

˙x(t) = fθ(t, x (t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],x(0) = x0. (3.140) The adjoint equation of the co-state p can be derived similarly as we did above and is in a form simpler than (3.137): 

> 

˙px(t) = −px(t)∂xfθ(t, x (t)) ∈ R1×d,

˙pτ (t) = −px(t)∂tfθ(t, x (t)) ∈ R1×1,

˙pσ(t) = −px(t)∂θfθ(t, x (t)) ∈ R1×n,

(3.141) and the terminal value is again p(t) = ( ∇g(x(T )) , 0, 0) ∈ R1×(d+1+ n). Then we can specify a terminal reward g(x(T )) as the objective function J(θ), and the gradient ∇θJ(θ) can be computed in the same way as in Algorithm 3.3.1 with the initial value and terminal value problems replaced with (3.140) and (3.141), respectively. Fourth, we can also leverage the Neural ODE method to solve many probability control problems. The idea is that we use a sufficient number of moving particles {xi(t) ∈ Rd : i ∈ [M ]} to represent the probability density function ρ(·, t ) on Rd at any time t. The particle number M may be artificially set or determined by the data available in the problem. When we parameterize the vector field as a deep neural network fθ : [0 , T ] ×

Rd → Rd, then these particles will move according to the ODE (3.142b). 168 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

Correspondingly, the evolution of ρ(·, t ) is given by the following continuity equation: 

∂tρ(x, t ) = −∇ · (ρ(x, t )fθ(t, x )) . (3.143) Therefore, we can use the particles as the representation of ρ, and learn the optimal fθ that makes ρ(·, t ) maximize or minimize the given objective function. A concrete example of this approach is given in Section 5.4 of Chapter 5. Fifth, when we use the Neural ODE method for probability control, we sometimes need to estimate the value of ρ(x, t ). where the approximation on the right-hand side is due to Monte Carlo in-tegration (see Section A.1 for details about this integration approximation method) and xi(t) ∼ ρ(·, t ). Therefore, we need to estimate log ρ(x, t ) using the moving particles. To this end, we can employ the following ODE: 

ddt log ρ(x(t), t ) = −∇ · fθ(t, x (t)) , (3.144) where d/dt on the left-hand side is the full gradient with respect to t, i.e., the gradient with respect to t is applied to both x(t) and t in ρ(x(t), t ), and 

∇ and ∇· denote the gradient and divergence with respect to x, respectively. To see (3.144), we notice that 

ddt ρ(x(t), t ) = ∂tρ(x(t), t ) + ∇ρ(x(t), t ) · ˙x(t)= ∂tρ(x(t), t ) + ∇ρ(x(t), t ) · fθ(t, x (t)) = −∇ · (ρ(t, x )fθ(t, x )) + ∇ρ(x(t), t ) · fθ(t, x (t)) = −ρ(x(t), t )∇ · fθ(t, x (t)) ,

where the second equality is due to the ODE ˙x(t) = fθ(t, x (t)) , and the third equality is due to the continuity equation (3.143). Diving both sides by ρ(x(t), t ) (assuming ρ is positive everywhere), we obtain (3.144). Notice that (3.143) only provides a means to compute the change of 

log ρ(x(t), t ). In practice, we may not know the initial ρ(·, 0) (or the terminal 

ρ(·, T ) at some given T > 0). However, this is usually not an issue in learning θ, because the initial or terminal is often given in advance and does not depend on θ. Therefore, we can often eliminate it from the objective function during modeling without affecting the solution θ in the network training process. 3.4. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 169 

# 3.4 Remarks and References 

3.4.1 Application in PDE Solution Operator Learning 

In this subsection, we present an application of deep optimal control in learn-ing solution operators of evolution partial differential equations (PDEs). We first provide an overview of evolution PDEs and the definition of solution operators. Then we show how to learn solution operators using deep opti-mal control. We focus on the critical components and key ideas during the discussions below and briefly mention extensions to general cases. An evolution PDE refers to a PDE that describes how a system changes over time, modeling dynamic processes like heat flow, waves, fluid and etc. Suppose Ω is an open subset of Rd and F is a differential operator defining an evolution PDE. Then the evolution PDE is written as 

∂tv(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ), ∀ (x, t ) ∈ Ω × [0 , T ], (3.145) where T > 0 is some prescribed terminal time. Here are a few examples of evolution PDEs (where γ > 0 is some given constant) that appear in a broad range of real-world problems: 

• Heat equation: 

∂tv(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ) := ∆ v(x, t ). (3.146) 

• Hamilton–Jacobi equation: 

∂tv(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ) := ∆ v(x, t ) + γ

2 |∇ v(x, t )|2. (3.147) 

• Allen–Cahn equation: 

∂tv(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ) := ∆ v(x, t ) − γv (x, t )( v(x, t )2 − 1) . (3.148) In the examples above, ∇ and ∆ are respectively the gradient and Laplacian of u with respect to x as usual, and v : ¯Ω × [0 , T ] → R is called a solution to the equation if it satisfies the corresponding PDE, subject to some initial and boundary condition which we will explain in detail soon. A differential operator F refers to the mapping from v to a function determined by v and its gradient up to certain order. The differential operators F in the examples above are all second-order due to the Laplacian. We remark that the differential operator F can be nonlinear, such as in the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (3.147) and the Allen–Cahn equation (3.148). Nonlinearity often cause significant challenge in solving these PDEs because the solutions to such PDEs may become stiff and unstable as time changes. Another major challenge in solving PDEs numerically (there are very few PDEs that have analytic solutions and we almost always need some 170 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 

numerical solver to solve PDEs) is the dimensionality. If the dimension 

d is large (e.g., d ≥ 5), then the classic numerical PDE solvers, such as the finite difference method, finite element method, and spectral method, which are well studied in the literature of numerical PDEs [9], often become computationally infeasible due to the issue known as curse of dimensionality. This is because these methods typically require spatial discretization of the domain Ω ⊂ Rd (or in a different transform domain), and the number of unknown variables in the discretized versions grows exponentially fast in d,making the discretized problem intractable for large d.We also notice that, in the examples (3.146), (3.147), and (3.148) above, the differential operators are autonomous since they do not explicitly depend on t. Instead, they depend on t implicitly through v(x, t ). However, we can easily extend the method to be introduced later to tackle PDEs with non-autonomous F by using the augmented variable ¯x := ( t, x ) and converting the PDEs into autonomous ones. This is straightforward and we omit the details here. Furthermore, it is worth pointing out that we can also handle evolution PDEs with higher-order time derivatives using the method to be introduced. As an example, for the evolution PDE with second-order time derivative 

∂2

> tt

v(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ), (3.149) we can convert (3.149) to an equivalent PDE with only first-order time derivative: { ∂tv(x, t ) = w(x, t ),∂tw(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ),

and the solution (w, v ) : ¯Ω × [0 , T ] → R2 satisfying the system of two PDEs above is to be found. For simplicity, we assume that F is autonomous and Ω = Rd below. We will not consider boundary condition for simplicity in this case. Recall that evolution PDEs often require some initial condition to ensure uniqueness of solution. For instance, in (3.146) and (3.147), we see that v + C with arbitrary constant C ∈ R solves the same PDE as long as v is a solution to the evolution PDE. The initial condition is determined by some given initial value g : ¯Ω → R.We let vg : ¯Ω × [0 , T ] → R denote the solution to the initial value problem  

> {

∂tv(x, t ) = F [v]( x, t ), ∀ (x, t ) ∈ Ω × [0 , T ],v(x, 0) = g(x), x ∈ ¯Ω. (3.150) Then, we call SF : C1( ¯Ω; R) → C2,1( ¯Ω × [0 , T ]; R) defined by 

SF (g) := vg. (3.151) 3.4. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 171 the solution operator associated with F to the problem (3.150), where the range C2,1( ¯Ω×[0 , T ]; R) is the set of real-valued functions defined on ¯Ω×[0 , T ]

that are twice continuously differentiable with respect to x and continuously differentiable with respect to t in the interior points. In other words, the solution operator SF associated with F is the mapping from any initial value 

g to its corresponding solution vg of (3.150). Our goal is to develop a method that can find (an approximation of) SF for any given F .We remark that finding the solution operator SF is a problem substan-tially different from solving (3.150) with a given and fixed g. The solution operator SF can produce the solution vg at very low computational cost for any g. By contrast, solving (3.150) means that one employs a method (such as the PINN method in Example 1.5.1) with a given initial g, and needs to start from scratch when another initial g is given, which is computationally intensive if we need the solutions for many different initials. The benefit of having the solution operator SF becomes more appealing when the di-mension d is high, in which case solving (3.150) with one instance of g is computationally expensive. Now we show how deep optimal control provides an approach to ap-proximating the solution operator SF for any given F . To this end, notice that if we parameterize a function v : Rd → R as a deep neural network uθ

with parameter θ ∈ Rn, then we effectively turn an n-dimensional vector θ

as the representative of the function v which is an element of an infinite-dimensional function space. If v : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd evolves in time (as those solutions to evolution PDEs), then we can use a trajectory of parameters 

θ : [0 , T ] → Rn to approximately represent v. Namely, we use uθ(t)(·) as an approximation of v(·, t ) for any t ∈ [0 , T ] by steering (controlling) θ(t)

properly. At this point, we would like to hold on and ask the following question: Is a deep neural network uθ(t) : Rd → R with time-evolving parameter θ(t)

equally expressive as a deep neural network ˆuη : Rd × [0 , T ] → R with a fixed parameter η? By uθ(t) being equally expressive as ˆuη, we meant that 

uθ(t)(·) equals to ˆuη(·, t ) for any t. If the answer is yes, then we know uθ(t) is a function approximator of the same representation capability as ˆuη, where the latter is known to have the capability of representing a large class of functions (e.g., W k, ∞ functions on a compact subset of an Euclidean space) by the universal approximation theorem (Theorem 1.4.5). The following lemma gives a positive answer to this question. 

Lemma 3.4.1. For any differentiable feed-forward neural network architec-ture ˆuη : Rd × [0 , T ] → R defined by (x, t ) 7 → ˆuη(x, t ) with parameter η, there exists a feed-forward neural network uθ(t) : Rd → R defined by x 7 → uθ(t)(x)

with θ(t) as its parameter at time t, where θ(t) is differentiable in time, such that uθ(t)(x) = ˆ uη(x, t ) for all x ∈ Rd and t ∈ R.172 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL

where h0(x, t ) = σ(W0(t)x + b0(t)) and

for some neural network zη,ξ with input x and parameters (η, ξ ) evolving in 

t. Applying this repeatedly justifies the claim. 3.4. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 173 Now we see that it is safe to use a deep neural network uθ(t) with fixed architecture but time-evolving parameter θ(t) to approximate the solution

from (3.152), which is simple and straightforward as it is fitting a neural network to a given function g on Ω. Then we can solve for θ(t) from the following ODE starting from θ(0) :

˙θ(t) = hη(θ(t)) , ∀ t ∈ [0 , T ],

which can be solved accurately and rapidly using any numerical ODE solver. Then the network uθ(t)(·) with parameter set to θ(t) is an approximation of the solution vg(·, t ) to the evolution PDE with initial g in (3.150). The computational cost of finding θ(0) dominates this two-step process of imple-menting SF , however, it is much lower than the cost of solving the problem (3.150) from scratch. In practice, there are several tricks to improve the accuracy of the control vector field hη and accelerate its training. For example, we can increase the quality of data by choosing the set of initial values {gi : ¯Ω → R : i ∈ [M ]}

more carefully, such that they are more relevant to the initials to which the trained solution operator SF would be applied. We can also solve individual 

θi(t) for each of the training initials in Dinit and generate sample points 

θi(tj ) with multiple time points tj ∈ (0 , T ]. These sample points can be formed as a regularization term added to the ODE of ri(t) in (3.157b). We refer interested readers to [45, 47] for more details. 

3.4.2 References of Optimal Control 

Optimal control is a core discipline in applied mathematics and engineering and has been extensively studied in the past decades. There are numerous excellent books that cover the theory and computation of optimal control 3.4. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 175 in the literature [7, 39, 42, 65, 92, 112]. Note that the original versions of [39, 92, 112] were published decades before the years shown in the bibliography and the modern literature database. Historically, optimal control grew out of the calculus of variations, which dates back several centuries, but it matured into a distinct field in the mid-dle of the 20th century following advances in aerospace and computational technologies in the 1960s and 1970s. Early work connected the minimization of integral cost functionals with variations of control functions, formalizing necessary conditions for optimality that generalize classical Euler–Lagrange equations [126]. This chapter does not cover stochastic optimal control, where the ODE is replaced with a stochastic differential equation (SDE) and the reward function is in the expectation form. The main ideas are similar to deterministic optimal control, but the diffusion effect and related analysis need to be taken into account carefully. We refer interested readers to [39, 42] for more details. A foundational result in the canonical literature is the Pontryagin Maxi-mum Principle formulated in the 1950s, which provides necessary conditions for optimal controls in terms of a Hamiltonian system and an adjoint (co-state) evolution. The significance of this principle is that it reduces infinite-dimensional optimization over control functions to a pointwise condition, simplifying analysis and implementation for many problems. In parallel, the Bellman dynamic programming principle introduced an alternative frame-work focused on the value function and recursive optimality, foundational to both continuous and discrete optimal control and later to reinforcement learning (we will cover more details in the next chapter). The core ideas and results of these two foundational results have covered in Section 3.1, the large part of which follows [39] with some expansions on the proof de-tails. Extensions of these two results to optimal probability density control are presented in Section 3.2, which follows [46]. The Neural ODE method as a continuous-time variant of residual networks was proposed in [27], and it turns out to be a powerful tool to solve standard optimal control and many other general control and learning problems, as we explained in Section 3.3. We also present its application in learning PDE solution operators in Section 3.4.1 by following [45,47] and later in probability density control method for generative modeling in Section 5.4. 176 3. DEEP OPTIMAL CONTROL 4

# Deep Reinforcement Learning 

Reinforcement learning addresses sequential decision-making problems in which an agent interacts with an environment by taking actions based on observed states. The objective is to design an optimal policy that maximizes the expected cumulative reward obtained from the environment over time. This chapter focuses on two central aspects of reinforcement learning. The first concerns its mathematical foundations. We introduce the funda-mental components of reinforcement learning systems and show how they are formalized within the framework of Markov decision processes. We present a collection of core theoretical results, most notably the Bellman operators and Bellman equations, that underpin reinforcement learning. These results are established with rigorous mathematical proofs. The second aspect addresses reinforcement learning algorithms in both model-based and model-free approaches. Model-based methods are strongly grounded in the Bellman theory, and we present two canonical algorithms: policy iteration and value iteration. Recognizing the complexity of real-world reinforcement learning problems, we further discuss a series of model-free algorithms that have demonstrated impressive empirical success. During the presentations, we illustrate how deep learning techniques can be inte-grated to address large-scale and high-dimensional reinforcement learning problems. 

# 4.1 Fundamentals of Reinforcement Learning 

4.1.1 Basic Concepts in Reinforcement Learning 

In reinforcement learning, an agent usually refers to a subject which can change state, perform actions, and receive feedbacks from the environment at specified time points. Example agents include robots, automobiles, drones, 177 178 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

and game players. The state of an agent usually refers to (the combination of) its location, speed, direction and etc. An action made by the agent can be acceleration, braking, robot arm rotation, etc. The environment can be the surrounding physical world, including human, which provides rewards to the agents according to their states and actions. The environment also determines the state transitions of the agents. A reward is often formed as a numerical value based on which the agent can learn how to perform optimally and earn the maximal total rewards over time. In reinforcement learning, we usually follow a discrete time setting as the rewards and measurements of state are often taken at discrete time points. In this chapter, we use x to denote the state of the agent, u as the action 1,and r as the reward. They are represented as numbers or vectors such that numerical computations can be carried out. We let X and U denote the state space and action space , respectively. Note that X and U can be either finite sets, sets with countably many elements, or subsets of Euclidean spaces. We also have the following definition of stationary policy, which describes the action to be made by the agent at any state x.

Definition 4.1.1 (Stationary policy) . We call π a stationary policy (or just 

policy ) on X if π(·| x) is a time-independent probability distribution on the action space U at every x ∈ X , i.e., π(u|x) ≥ 0 for any u ∈ U , and 

> ∫
> U

π(u|x) du = 1 .

If U is a discrete set, such as a finite set or a set of countably many actions, then the integral should be replaced with sum over U. We use Π to denote the set of stationary policies defined on X .In practice, there are applications where the policies are non-stationary, namely, we have πt(·| x) which also depends on time t. However, it is the standard setting of using stationary policies in most reinforcement learning research and applications, and therefore we will follow this setting and only consider stationary policies in this chapter. 

4.1.2 Markov Decision Processes 

When a stationary policy π is given, the agent can start from an initial state X0 drawn from some initial state distribution p0 on the state space 

X at time t = 0 , and implement the policy π to make action U0. Then the environment provides a reward R1 based on X0 and U0, and moves the agent to a new state X1. This process will continue in time and eventually       

> 1In the reinforcement learning literature, the state and action are often denoted by s
> and a, respectively. We use xand uhere to indicate their equivalences to the state and control in optimal control discussed in Chapter 3.

4.1. FUNDAMENTALS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 179 Environment 

• The relevant physical world surrounding the agent. 

• May include non-biological and biological (such as human) subjects. 

• May provide a numerical reward value r ∈ R according to the current state x ∈ X of and action u ∈ U taken by the agent. 

• Can move the agent to a new state x′ ∈ X according to its current state x ∈ X and the action u ∈ U it takes. Here x′ ∈ U is determined by some (possibly unknown) transition probability p(·| x, u ) on U.At time t, the agent is at state xt ∈ X and takes action ut ∈ U by following policy π(·| xt).The environment observes (xt, u t).After observing (xt, u t),the environment moves the agent to xt+1 ∈ X 

according to p(·| xt, u t)

and (possibly) provides a reward rt+1 ∈ R based on (xt, u t).Agent 

• A subject that can interact with the environment. 

• Usually refers to an intelligent robot, automobile, drone, and etc. 

• Can acquire its current state x ∈ X .

• Can take an action u ∈ U based on x by following a policy π(·| x).

• If π(·| x) is deterministic, then u ∈ U is directly selected. 

• If π(·| x) is stochastic (a probability on U), then u ∼ π(·| x).

• The goal of RL is to find an optimal policy π(·| x) for every x ∈ X 

such that the agent can maximize the total reward received from the environment over time. Figure 4.1: Structure of a standard reinforcement learning (RL) system. This system consists of an agent and an environment. The functionalities of the agent and environment are explained in their corresponding boxes. The agent can interact with the environment by taking actions following a policy π. At any time t, the environment can provide a reward value rt+1 

and moves the agent to a new state xt+1 according to the current state xt

of the agent and the action ut that the agent takes. The goal of RL is to find an optimal policy π such that the agent can gain maximal total reward over time. 180 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

forms a trajectory: 

X0, U 0, R 1, X 1, U 1, R 2, · · · , X t, U t, R t+1 , X t+1 , · · · (4.1) Note that we use capital letters in (4.1) since all these values are random in general (except for cases like p0 and π are deterministic, i.e., they only map to specific values and do not have any randomness) in a reinforcement learning problem. In the setting of reinforcement learning, the trajectory (4.1) is assumed or set up as a Markov process . More precisely, for any time t, the random variables Ut, R t+1 , X t+1 , · · · depend only on the value of Xt, not any values of the past random variables X0, · · · , X t−1, U t−1, R t. In this case, (4.1) is called a Markov decision process .From the probability perspective, the Markov decision process (4.1) is determined by the following policy and transition probability distributions. For any x ∈ X , there is 

Ut ∼ π( · | Xt = x), (4.2) where the π ∈ Π is a stationary policy. For any x ∈ X and u ∈ U , the next state Xt+1 follows the transition probability 

Xt+1 ∼ p( · | Xt = x, U t = u), (4.3) Note that this transition probability p( · | x, u ) is completely determined by the environment and is not affected by any policy. When a policy π is given, we also frequently use the following state-to-state transition probability 

pπ(x′|x) := 

> ∫
> U

π(u|x)p(x′|u, x ) du (4.4) for any x′ ∈ X during our derivations in this chapter. It is worth noting that, if a policy π is given and the state-to-state transition probability pπ in (4.4) is known, then the Markov decision process (4.1) reduces to a Markov reward process :

X0, R 1, X 1, R 2, · · · , X t, R t+1 , X t+1 , · · · (4.5) since the actions will be taken automatically by the policy π, and the state transition is determined by pπ. A Markov reward process (4.5) can be ob-served, but the policy is supposed to be fixed during this process. In some reinforcement learning problems, the reward Rt+1 is also a ran-dom variable following some conditional probability pR(·| Xt = x, U t = u)

(again pR is determined by the environment and not affected by any policy). However, it is mostly the case that only the expectation of Rt+1 is needed to solve the reinforcement learning problem, and hence for simplicity we will just set Rt+1 = r(Xt, U t), where r(x, u ) : X × U → R is deterministic (and given by the environment) and can be evaluated given x and u.4.1. FUNDAMENTALS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 181 

4.1.3 Bellman Operators and Equations 

With all the concepts given above, we are ready to present the mathemat-ical formulation of a reinforcement learning problem. To this end, we first introduce an important term called the state value function (which can be thought as an analogue of the value function in optimal control). In rein-forcement learning, the state value function maps every state x ∈ X to the sum of discounted rewards received during a trajectory (4.1) started from 

X0 = x in expectation, as in the following definition. 

Definition 4.1.2 (State value function) . Let π ∈ Π be a stationary policy on the state space X and action space U, and γ ∈ (0 , 1) be a discount factor .Then the state value function (or value function for short), denoted by vπ,is a mapping from X to R defined by 

vπ(x) = E 

> [∞∑
> t=0

γtRt+1 

> ∣∣∣

X0 = x

> ]

, (4.6) where {Rt+1 : t ≥ 0} are the rewards received during the trajectory of a Markov decision process (4.1) which starts from X0 = x and follows the policy distribution (4.2) and state transition probability (4.3). We have several remarks about the discount factor γ introduced in Def-inition 4.1.2. This discount factor is often in (0 , 1) because we are usually not fully certain about the future situations. We are also not sure how long a process like (4.1) may last, and a discount factor γ ∈ (0 , 1) ensures the total reward is always bounded under mild conditions (e.g., Rt is upper bounded by some constant MR > 0 for all t). In practical applications, we use a discount factor because human and animal behaviors appear to prefer immediate rewards. In finance, a discount factor is often considered because immediate rewards may earn more interests and profits than de-layed rewards. Last but not least, a discount factor γ ∈ (0 , 1) allows us to construct strong mathematical theory and numerical algorithms more easily for reinforcement learning. It is still possible to consider reinforcement learning under the Markov decision process with γ = 1 . This is usually the case when there is a ter-mination state ¯x ∈ X , such that the agent reaches ¯x in finite time (under certain probability sense) almost surely and will stay at ¯x afterwards, i.e., 

p(¯ x|¯x, u ) = 1 for all u ∈ U . Nevertheless, we will mostly consider the stan-dard case with discount factor γ ∈ (0 , 1) in this chapter. Now we notice an important property of the state value function vπ182 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

This indicates the importance of this operation in reinforcement learning. In order to describe this operation more clearly, we first consider the set of all uniformly bounded functions on X as follows: 

B(X ) := 

{

v : X → R : sup 

> x∈X

|v(x)| < ∞

}

. (4.8) It is easy to see that B(X ) is a real linear space, i.e, a1v1 + a2v2 ∈ B(X )

for any v1, v 2 ∈ B(X ) and a1, a 2 ∈ R. Moreover, we define the ∞-norm on 

B(X ) by ∥v∥∞ := sup x∈X |v(x)| for any v ∈ B(X ). Then it is easy to check that B(X ) with this ∞-norm is a Banach space (see Appendix A.2). Now we are ready to give the formal definition of the operation on the right-hand side of (4.7). 

Definition 4.1.3 (Bellman operator associated to a given policy) . Let π ∈

Π be a stationary policy on X . Then the Bellman operator T π associated with π is a mapping from B(X ) to B(X ) given by T π : v 7 → T πv, where 

T πv(x) := EU ∼π(·| x)

[

r(x, U ) + γEX′∼p(·| x,U )[v(X′)] 

]

(4.9) for every x ∈ X .4.1. FUNDAMENTALS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 183 Note that T πv(x) should be always interpreted as (T πv)( x). Now, with the definition of Bellman operator T π in (4.9), we can write the Bellman equation (4.7) of vπ in a concise form: 

vπ = T πvπ.

This implies that vπ is a fixed point of T π. There are rich results about op-erators and their fixed points in Banach spaces. In particular, if an operator is γ-contractive for some γ ∈ (0 , 1) , then it has many important properties due to the fixed point theory (see Appendix A.2). In what follows, we will show that T π is indeed γ-contractive. 

Proposition 4.1.4 (T π is γ-contractive) . For any π ∈ Π and γ ∈ (0 , 1) ,the Bellman operator T π associated with π is a γ-contraction. Namely, for any v, ˆv ∈ B(X ), there is 

∥T πv − T π ˆv∥∞ ≤ γ∥v − ˆv∥∞. (4.10)

Since T π and T ∗ are γ-contractive, the Banach fixed point theorem (The-orem A.2.7) implies that each of them has a unique fixed point, and the corresponding fixed point iteration always converge to that fixed point from any initial. These are summarized in the following theorem. 

Theorem 4.1.8 (Bellman operator has unique fixed point) . The following two statements about Bellman operators on X with γ ∈ (0 , 1) hold true: 

• For any stationary policy π ∈ Π on X , the Bellman operator T π associ-ated with π has a unique fixed point vπ. Moreover, for any v ∈ B(X ),let {vk : k = 0 , 1, . . . } be the sequence generated by the fixed point iteration vk+1 = T πvk with v0 = v, then 

∥(T π)kv − vπ∥∞ = ∥vk − vπ∥∞ → 0 (4.15) 

as k → ∞ at a linear rate of γ.

• The Bellman operator T ∗ has a unique fixed point v∗. Moreover, for any v ∈ B(X ), let {vk : k = 0 , 1, . . . } be the sequence generated by the fixed point iteration vk+1 = T πvk with v0 = v, then 

∥(T ∗)kv − v∗∥∞ = ∥vk − v∗∥∞ → 0 (4.16) 

as k → ∞ at a linear rate of γ.Proof. Since T π and T ∗ are γ-contractive on B(X ), both the existence of a unique fixed point and the convergence of fixed point iteration immediately follow Theorem A.2.7. 186 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

Definition 4.1.9 (Partial ordering in B(X )). Let v, ˆv ∈ B(X ). We say 

v ≤ ˆv (4.17) if v(x) ≤ ˆv(x) for every x ∈ X . We say v = ˆ v if v ≤ ˆv and ˆv ≤ v.Now we have three important monotonicity properties about Bellman operators. The results will be frequently used later.

T ∗v ≤ T ∗ ˆv. (4.20)

FUNDAMENTALS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 187 Since the goal of reinforcement learning is to find the optimal policy (we have not defined it mathematically yet; it will be given soon below, in Definition 4.1.14), it is intuitive to consider those policies that are “greedy” in some sense. The following definition provides a concrete definition of greedy policies given a function in B(X ).

Definition 4.1.11 (Greedy policy) . For any v ∈ B(X ), π is called greedy with respect to v if 

T πv = T ∗v, (4.21) where T π is the Bellman operator associated with π. Note that v ∈ B(X )

may have more than one greedy policies. An important problem is that, for any v ∈ B(X ), does there always exist a stationary policy π that is greedy with respect to v? Due to the Markov property of stationary policies, the answer to this question is yes if and only if for every x ∈ X there exists u(x) ∈ U (does not need to be unique) satisfying 

r(x, u (x)) + γEX′∼p(·| x,u (x)) [ˆ v(X′)] (4.22) 

= sup 

> π∈Π

EU ∼π(·| x)

> [

r(x, U ) + γEX′∼p(·| x,U )[ˆ v(X′)] 

> ]

.

In this case, we can set up a policy π such that it maps x to u(x) at every 

x (if there are more than one u(x) satisfying (4.22), we can choose any of them). Then this π is greedy with respect to v by Definition 4.1.11. We can see that there exists such u(x) for (4.22) at every x ∈ X if and only if the supremum can be attained in the action space U. It is easy to see that this happens when (i) U is a finite set; or (ii) U ⊂ Rm is compact and the function 

r(x, ·) + γ

> ∫

p(x′|x, ·)v(x′) dx ′ : U → R (4.23) is continuous (because a continuous function defined on a compact domain in Rm has its maximizer(s) in the domain). Both (i) and (ii) are sufficient conditions but not necessary. In practical settings, we often have a policy greedy with respect to any specified v ∈ B(X ).

Theorem 4.1.12. Let v∗ be the fixed point of T ∗. If π is greedy with respect to v∗, then vπ = v∗.Proof. Since π is greedy with respect to v∗, we have by Definition 4.1.11 that 

T πv∗ = T ∗v∗. (4.24) On the other hand, we have T ∗v∗ = v∗ since v∗ is the fixed point of T ∗.Therefore, we know T πv∗ = v∗, which means that v∗ is also a fixed point of 

T π. Since vπ is the unique fixed point of T π, we know vπ = v∗.188 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

We have seen a function of state x and action u which evaluates the expected total sum of rewards provided a state value function v, such as in (4.22). This function is also useful the algorithmic developments in rein-forcement learning. We give a formal definition of this function next. 

Definition 4.1.13 (Q-function) . For any v ∈ B(X ), we define the qual-ity function , or simply the Q-function (also called the state-action value function ), associated with v by 

q(x, u ) := r(x, u ) + γEX′∼p(·| x,u )[v(X′)] (4.25) for every x ∈ X and u ∈ U . Notice that q : X × U → R.In particular, we denote the Q-function associated with vπ (the unique fixed point of T π) by 

qπ(x, u ) := r(x, u ) + γEX′∼p(·| x,u )[vπ(X′)] , (4.26) and the Q-function associated with v∗ (the unique fixed point of T ∗) by 

q∗(x, u ) := r(x, u ) + γEX′∼p(·| x,u )[v∗(X′)] (4.27) for every x ∈ X and u ∈ U .We also notice that, conversely, vπ and v∗ can be represented by their Q-functions qπ and q∗, respectively. Namely, 

vπ(x) = EU ∼π(·| x)[qπ(x, U )] 

and 

v∗(x) = sup 

> π∈Π

EU ∼π(·| x)[q∗(x, U )] (4.28) for every x ∈ X . If the supremum in (4.28) can be attained in Π for every 

x ∈ X (for example, U is a finite set), then (4.28) reduces to 

v∗(x) = max  

> u∈U

q∗(x, u ).

4.1.4 Policy Optimality and Improvement Theory 

Now we are ready to define formally the meaning of optimal policies in reinforcement learning. 

Definition 4.1.14 (Optimal policy) . We call π∗ ∈ Π an optimal policy if 

EU ∼π∗(·| x)[q∗(x, U )] = v∗(x) (4.29) at every x ∈ X , where v∗ is the unique fixed point of T ∗ defined in (4.11) and q∗ is defined in (4.27). 4.1. Theorem 4.1.16 (Policy improvement theorem) . The following statements hold true: (i) For any π0 ∈ Π, let π be greedy with respect to vπ0 (which is the unique fixed point of the Bellman operator T π0 associated with π0), namely, 

T πvπ0 = T ∗vπ0 , then 

vπ0 ≤ vπ. (4.41) 

We call π an improvement upon π0.4.1. FUNDAMENTALS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 191 

(ii) If, in addition, vπ0 (x) < T ∗vπ0 (x) for some x ∈ X , then vπ0 (x) <vπ(x). In this case, we say π strictly improves π0 at x.(iii) If T ∗vπ∗

= vπ∗

, then π∗ is an optimal policy. Proof. For (i), we have 

vπ0 = T π0 vπ0 ≤ T ∗vπ0 = T πvπ0 ,

where the inequality is due to Theorem 4.1.10(i), and the last equality is because π is greedy with respect to vπ0 . Continuing applying T π on both sides and employing Theorem 4.1.10(ii), we obtain 

vπ0 ≤ T πvπ0 ≤ · · · ≤ (T π)kvπ0

for all k. Letting k → ∞ and noticing that (T π)kvπ0 → vπ in the ∞-norm, we obtain vπ0 ≤ vπ.For (ii), we follow the same deductions as in (i) and obtain 

vπ0 (x) < T ∗vπ0 (x) ≤ (T π)kvπ0 (x)

for all k at this x. Taking k → ∞ , we get vπ0 (x) < T ∗vπ0 (x) ≤ vπ(x).For (iii), since T ∗vπ∗

= vπ∗

, we know vπ∗

= v∗, the unique fixed point of T ∗. Noticing that vπ∗

is also the fixed point of T π∗

, we have T π∗

v∗ = v∗,namely 

EU ∼π∗(·| x)[q∗(x, U )] = v∗(x)

for every x ∈ X . By Definition 4.1.14, we know π∗ is an optimal policy. Now we have a more clear picture of what an optimal policy may look like. We connect the optimality condition of a policy, its relation to ¯v, and the greediness property in the following theorem. 

Theorem 4.1.17. The following statements are equivalent: (i) π∗ is an optimal policy, namely, EU ∼π∗(·| x)[q∗(x, U )] = v∗(x).(ii) v∗ = vπ∗

= ¯ v.(iii) π∗ is greedy with respect to v∗.Proof. If (i) holds true, we have T π∗

v∗ = v∗, which means that v∗ is the unique fixed point of T π∗

. Therefore v∗ = vπ∗

. Notice that vπ∗

≤ ¯v by (4.31). Combining Lemma 4.1.15 which shows ¯v ≤ v∗, we obtain (ii). If (ii) holds true, then we have 

T π∗

v∗ = T π∗

vπ∗

= vπ∗

= v∗ = T ∗v∗,192 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

where the first and third equalities are due to (ii), the second equality is because π∗ is the fixed point of T π∗

, and the last equality is because v∗ is the fixed point of T ∗.If (iii) holds true, then 

T π∗

v∗ = T ∗v∗ = v∗,

where the first equality is because that π∗ is greedy with respect to v∗. This shows that π∗ is an optimal policy according to Definition 4.1.14. 

Corollary 4.1.18. If π∗ is an optimal policy, then π∗ is greedy with respect to ¯v.Proof. If π∗ is an optimal policy, we know from Theorem 4.1.17 that vπ∗

=¯v = v∗. Therefore, 

T π∗

= vπ∗

= v∗ = T ∗v∗ = T ∗ ¯v, 

which implies that π∗ is greedy with respect to ¯v.

Summary Now we take a brief summary of the theoretical results devel-oped in this section. Under the standard setting with infinite time horizon and a discount factor γ ∈ (0 , 1) , we know that every stationary policy π ∈ Π

yields a Bellman operator T π : B(X ) → B(X ), which has a unique fixed point vπ. Here vπ : X → R is the state value function of π, meaning that 

vπ(x) is the total future reward in expectation since the agent is at the state 

x ∈ X . The Q-function (state-action value function) qπ(x, u ) is the total future reward in expectation since a specific action u ∈ U is taken by the agent when it is at the state x ∈ X .We have a conceptually “pointwise optimal” value function ¯v : X →

R that is defined to have the supremum value over all stationary policies in Π at every x ∈ X . However, ¯v itself does not suggest whether it has a corresponding policy in Π, and consequently it is unknown whether it corresponds to any Bellman operator. Meanwhile, we also have a conceptually “optimal” Bellman operator T ∗ :

B(X ) → B(X ). It has a unique fixed point v∗ ∈ B(X ) and consequently a Q-function q∗, but it is not always true that v∗ is the state value function of some policy in Π. However, under the mild condition that for every x ∈ X ,a maximizer of the function 

r(x, ·) + γ

> ∫

p(x′|x, ·)v∗(x′) dx ′ : U → R,

can be attained in U, then v∗ has a greedy policy π∗ ∈ Π. (We mentioned that if U is finite, or U is compact and the real-valued function on U defined above is continuous, then a maximizer can be attained in U.) More impor-tantly, it turns out that the Bellman operator of π∗ is T ∗, whose unique fixed point is v∗, and ¯v is just v∗. This implies that π∗ is an optimal policy by definition and v∗ is the optimal state value function. 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 193 

# 4.2 Reinforcement Learning Algorithms 

Reinforcement learning algorithms can be categorized in many different ways. This is because the applications domains and problem settings where reinforcement learning algorithms are employed are very diverse. In this sec-tion, we present reinforcement learning algorithms into two classes following one of the most typical categorizations in this community: model-based method and model-free method. Model-based method in general consider problems where we have (approximately) accurate model to describe the the reward and state transition probability determined by the environment, whereas in model-free method we do not have such knowledge and need to use data generated by the agent (through its interactions with the environ-ment) to solve the problem. We will provide details about these two classes of algorithms in the two subsections below. 

4.2.1 Model-based Reinforcement Learning Algorithms 

Model-based reinforcement learning algorithms refer to those applied to problems where we have the following information: 

• The reward function r(x, u ), or the conditional reward probability dis-tribution pR(·| x, u ) defined on R such that we can compute the ex-pected value of reward 

ER∼pR(·| x,u )[R] = 

> ∫

rp R(r|x, u ) dr, 

for every state-action pair (x, u ) ∈ X ×U . For ease of discussion, we as-sume the function r is known when we discuss model-based algorithms below. 

• The state transition probability 

p( · | x, u )

on X , which is the probability of the next state given that the current state is x and an action u is performed, for every state-action pair 

(x, u ) ∈ X × U .Notice that, the information above is determined by the environment in the problem setting. Specifically, we know exactly how much reward can be received and what is the next state (or the probability distribution of the next state) if the current state is x and an action u is taken. This is the reason we call such reinforcement learning problems model-based .When we work on a model-based reinforcement learning problem, we often can directly solve for the optimal policy in the reinforcement learning problem, and do not need the agent to interact with the environment and 194 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

collect necessary data to estimate these quantities. In many of such cases, we can use the idea of dynamic programming or alike to find the optimal policy. In what follows, we provide details about some commonly used model-based reinforcement learning algorithms. These algorithms heavily depend on the properties of Markov decision processes and Bellman operators we discussed in Section 4.1. 

Policy Iteration 

Policy iteration is an iterative scheme that generates a sequence of con-tinuously improving policies for model-based reinforcement learning. The sequence of policies can be proved to converge to the optimal policy un-der certain conditions (to be specified later). The theory behind the policy iteration algorithm is Policy Improvement Theorem (Theorem 4.1.16). The typical procedure of a policy iteration algorithm is as follows. We choose an arbitrary initial guess v0 ∈ B(X ) (not necessarily a value function) and find the policy π0 that is greedy with respect to v0. Now at the kth iteration with any k ≥ 0, our first step is to find the value function vπk ,called the policy evaluation step as we try to find the value function vπk

corresponding to the current policy πk; and then in the second step, which is called policy improvement , we find a policy πk+1 which is greedy with respect to vπk , and can prove that πk+1 is an improvement over πk. Then we increase the iteration counter k by 1 and continue to the next iteration. We summarize the policy iteration algorithm in Algorithm 4.2.1. 

Algorithm 4.2.1 Policy Iteration for Model-based Reinforcement Learning 

Input: v0 ∈ B(X ) and π0 ∈ Π greedy with respect to v0, k = 0 . 

> 1:

repeat  

> 2:

(Policy evaluation) Find vπk such that T πk vπk = vπk . 

> 3:

(Policy improvement) Find πk+1 ∈ Π greedy with respect to vπk . 

> 4:

k ← k + 1  

> 5:

until Convergent. 

Output: πk.When we execute the policy iteration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.1), we obtain a sequence of policies 

π0, π 1, . . . , π k, . . . . (4.42) By the definition of greedy policies (Definition 4.1.11), we know that the policy improvement step results in T πk+1 vπk = T ∗vπk . Furthermore, by Policy Improvement Theorem (Theorem 4.1.16(i)), we have 

vπk ≤ vπk+1 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 195 for every k ≥ 0.If both the state space X and the action space U in are finite sets, then we know there are only finitely many greedy policies, and the iterations (4.42) must stop in finitely many iterations with πk = πk+1 for some k. In this case, we have 

vπk = T πk vπk = T πk+1 vπk = T ∗vπk

where the first equality is because that vπk is the fixed point of T πk , and the second equality is due to πk = πk+1 . Therefore, we have vπk = v∗ since 

v∗ is the unique minimizer of T ∗. From Theorem 4.1.17, we know πk is an optimal policy. Now we need to consider the practical implementation of the policy it-eration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.1). More specifically, we need to find ways to solve the policy evaluation (Line 2) and policy improvement (Line 3) sub-problems. Depending on the problem size, we need different approaches to solve these subproblems, which we detail below. 

Small problems By small problems, we mean the state space X and the action space U are small finite sets (with 10 3 elements or alike). In this case, for any π, we can compute the state reward 

rπ(x) := EU ∼π(·| x)[r(x, U )] (4.43) and the state-to-state transition probability 

pπ(x′|x) := ∑

> u∈U

π(u|x)p(x′|x, u ) (4.44) for all x, x ′ ∈ X . Let n = |X | be the number of states in X . Then both vπ and rπ on X can be represented as n-dimensional column vec-tors, and the transition probabilities can be formed as an n-by-n matrix 

P π = [ pπ(x′|x)] x,x ′∈X . Therefore, the Bellman equation of vπ

vπ(x) = T πvπ(x) = EU ∼π(·| x)
