Moderate problems In these cases, we often have state space size |X | ≈ 

10 6 or alike. It is computationally expensive to directly solve (4.45) in general. One feasible approach is to solve the policy evaluation subproblem (Line 2 in Algorithm 4.2.1) for the given policy π by applying the fixed point iteration vj+1 = T πvj for j ≥ 0 with some initial guess v0. Since T π

is γ-contractive, we know by Theorem 4.1.8 that 

∥vj − vπ∥∞ ≤ γj ∥v0 − vπ∥∞

for all j. Therefore, we can approximate vπ at a linear rate of γ for any π. In practice, we can terminate the fixed point iteration once ∥vj+1 − vj ∥∞ < ε tol 

for some prescribed tolerance (a user-defined hyperparameter) εtol > 0, and consider the policy evaluation (Line 2 in Algorithm 4.2.1) approximately solved and move on to policy improvement (Line 3 in Algorithm 4.2.1) To solve the policy improvement subproblem, we can set πk+1 to the deterministic greedy policy as in (4.48). This is computationally feasible when U is a finite set of small to moderate size. 

Large problems By large problems, we mean those where X has 10 10 

states or even much larger, or X is a continuous region in Rd with d ≥ 5

such that a moderate space discretization results in over 10 10 states (this is due to the well-known curse-of-dimensionality issue). In practice, these problems often have much larger sizes, which make storing the value function 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 197 or the policy in the memory of a modern computer infeasible. Therefore, we often use function approximators to represent the value function and the policy as an alternative. Deep neural networks are shown to be very effective function approximators in these cases. More specifically, in the policy evaluation step (Line 2 in Algorithm 4.2.1), we have the current estimated policy πk. To find its value function 

vπk : X → R, we parameterize it as a deep neural network and train it by solving the following minimization problem: 

vπk = arg min 

> v

12

> ∫

|v(x) − T πk v(x)|2 dx (4.49) where T πk is the Bellman operator associated with the policy πk as defined in (4.9). The minimization (4.49) is with respect to the network parameters of v. Since T πk is γ-contractive, it has a unique fixed point, and therefore the problem (4.49) has a unique minimizer which is the desired vπk .The policy improvement step (Line 3 in Algorithm 4.2.1) requires finding the policy πk+1 greedy with respect to vπk , as seen in (4.48). It reduces to solving the maximization problem for a greedy action u ∈ U at each state 

x ∈ X . The difficulty depends on whether such maximization problems are easy to solve or not, which can vary across different application problems. Therefore, method employed for solving this policy improvement subproblem is often case by case. Nevertheless, there are some applications where the state space X is ex-tremely large but the action space is very small. For example, in the Go board game (called Weiqi in China, Igo in Japan, and Baduk in Korea), which is an ancient Chinese strategy board game, the state space X con-tains all possible board positions and |X | ≈ 10 170 , while |U| is only a few hundreds (the number of legal actions in U is often smaller depending on the current state x). Moreover, the transition from (x, u ) to the next state 

x′ is deterministic: once an action u is performed (placing a stone on the board) at state x (the current board position), the next state x′ is known. In this case, the maximization problem in (4.48) can be easily solved by simple search using a computer for any specific x.However, it is still challenging to solve the policy improvement subprob-lem even if |U| is small: The state space X is still too large, and it is impossible to find an action u for every x ∈ X . In practice, we can pa-rameterize the policy π (which is a conditional probability on U given any state in X ) as a deep neural network (called the policy network), and train this network along with the deep neural network (called the value network) which parameterizes the value function. For example, with the current value function vπ, we can sample M states {xi : i ∈ [M ]} from X , and solve 

ui = arg max

Value Iteration 

Value iteration is another iterative scheme for solving model-based rein-forcement learning problems. The idea of value iteration is to generate a sequence of value functions which directly approximates the optimal value function v∗. When the (approximation of) value function v∗ is found, we directly obtain the optimal policy which is the one greedy with respect to v∗.By contrast, policy iteration needs to generate a sequence of continuously improving policy during its iterations. Since T ∗ is γ-contractive, we know v∗ is its unique fixed point and can be approximated by using the fixed point iteration vk+1 = T ∗vk from any initial 

v0. The value iteration algorithm is effectively implementing this fixed point iteration scheme. We summarize the value iteration algorithm in Algorithm 4.2.2. To implement the value improvement step (Line 2 of Algorithm 4.2.2), we set

However, for problems of large sizes, we need to use function approx-imators, such as deep neural networks, to approximate vk. To this end, for every given vk, we can sample M states {xi : i ∈ [m]} from X and find their corresponding ui’s by solving

4.2.2 Model-free Reinforcement Learning Algorithms 

In many real-world applications, we may not have accurate models of the reward function r (or the reward probability pR(·| x, u )) and the state tran-sition probability p(·| x, u ) due to the complexity of the environment or the problem setting. In these cases, we need to use the agent to perform ac-tions and interact with the environment. The agent can collect data during these interactions, with which we can estimate the reward and state transi-tion probability, and find the optimal policy that yields the maximal total reward. This class of methods are called the model-free reinforcement learn-ing algorithms. In this subsection, we present a series of model-free reinforcement learn-ing algorithms. These methods share lots of similarities, but they are also different due to the diversity of problem settings and data availability. Nev-ertheless, we should keep the fundamental results developed in Section 4.1 in mind which help to understand these algorithms. 

Monte Carlo Method 

The original Monte Carlo method is a model-free method and often applied to problems of small to moderate sizes. Recall that, for a given policy π ∈ Π,its value function is defined by 

vπ(x) = Eπ,p  

> [∞∑
> t=0

γtRt+1 

> ∣∣∣

X0 = x

> ]

,

for every x ∈ X , where Rt is the reward received at time t. In problems up to moderate size, X is a finite set of size 10 6 or alike, and therefore the value function vπ : X → R can be stored as a tabular (as an array of size |X | ) in computer memory and easily updated according to observation data, which we detail below. Since we do not know the reward function r and the transition proba-bility p in the model-free setting, we need to let the agent perform actions 200 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

in the environment and collect sample data to estimate these quantities. In this case, suppose we let the agent interacts with the environment by follow-ing a current estimated π, then we will observe a Markov decision process according to this π:

X0, U 0, R 1, . . . , X t, U t, R t+1 , X t+1 , U t+1 , . . . (4.51) To extend the concept of value function vπ, we define the gross reward by 

Gπt (x) :=

γτ −tRτ +1 , (4.52) which is the total reward received since time t with Xt = x, assuming the Markov decision process started from time t instead of 0. We also define the expected gross reward by 

gπt (x) := Eπ,p

> ∣∣∣

. (4.53) Then gπt (x) with any t can be used to estimate vπ(x) with proper scaling factor γt. In the Monte Carlo method, we need to use the samples of gπt (x),namely the samples of Gπt (x), to approximate the value function vπ.To be more specific, we let the agent follow the current policy π and perform actions in the environment. Then we observe a sample trajectory, called an episode , of the Markov reward process. An episode is just a part of the sampled Markov decision process (4.51) without actions: 

x0, r 1, x 1, r 2, . . . , x t, r t+1 , x t+1 , . . . , x T , r T +1 , (4.54) which terminates at some finite time T in practice. If we observe xt = x for at time t, then we take T∑ 

> τ=t

γτ −trτ +1 (4.55) as one sample of Gπt (x). Notice that, we may obtain zero, one, or multiple samples like (4.55) for x ∈ X in one episode, depending on the number of times that the state x appears in the episode. In practice, we can also run multiple episodes and take the average of samples like (4.55). With a slight abuse of notation, we denote this average as Gπt (x) (t is to imply that the gross reward is received since we reached the state x at time t; but the actual value of t does not matter because Gπt (x) is an estimate of vπ(x) for any t). In this case, the Monte Carlo method updates vπ(x) by 

vπ(x) ← vπ(x) + α(Gπt (x) − vπ(x)) , (4.56) where α > 0 is the step size. The Monte Carlo method is known to yield unbiased estimate of the value function vπ if the episode is infinitely long, but the variance of the estimate 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 201 is often large in general. After vπ is estimated, we can find the policy π′

greedy with respect to vπ, and repeat the process of the Monte Carlo method using the updated policy π′ and estimate the new value function vπ′

, and so on. As a result, the Monte Carlo method generates a sequence of improving policies as in the value iteration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.2), but without knowledge of the reward function r and the transition probability p.

Temporal Difference Method 

The Temporal difference (TD) is another model-free method closely related to the Monte Carlo method. The difference is that, for the current policy 

π, the tabular of vπ in the TD method is continuously updated when going through the sampled Markov reward process (an episode) of form (4.54). By contrast, this tabular is only updated at the completion of every episode in the Monte Carlo method. More specifically, suppose xt = x, and the agent receives a reward rt+1 

and move to state xt+1 , then we can update vπ(x) by 

vπ(x) ← vπ(x) + α   

> (TD target
> ︷︸︸ ︷

rt+1 + γv π(xt+1 ) −vπ(x)  

> ︸︷︷ ︸
> TD error
> )

(4.57) where α > 0 is the step size. As we can see, the TD method (4.57) uses the so-called TD target rt+1 + γv π(xt+1 ) to replace Gπt (x) in the Monte Carlo method (4.56). The goal is to make the TD error in (4.57) become zero, yielding the Bellman equation of vπ in (4.7). Note that the TD method uses part of the current estimate, i.e., vπ(xt+1 ), to estimate the value vπ(x). This strategy is called bootstrapping . Comparing to the Monte Carlo method, the estimate of vπ obtained in the TD method is biased, but the variance is often smaller than that in the Monte Carlo method. The TD method discussed above is often called in short as the TD(0) method. It can be generalized by using the n-step gross rewards for n =1, 2, . . . as the TD target. To this end, we denote 

Gπt, 1(x) := Rt+1 + γv π(Xt+1 ),Gπt, 2(x) := Rt+1 + γR t+2 + γ2vπ(Xt+2 ),

...

Gπt,n (x) := Rt+1 + γR t+2 + γ2Rt+3 + · · · + γn−1Rt+n + γnvπ(Xt+n),

...

Gπt, ∞(x) := Gπt , (Assuming Rt = 0 when t > T + 1 ).

Then for any λ ∈ [0 , 1] , we define 

Gπ,λ t (x) := 

> ∞∑
> n=1

(1 − λ)λn−1Gπt,n (x). (4.58) 202 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

Notice that (1 − λ)λn−1 plays the role of weight in the sum (4.58), and the total weight is ∑∞

> n=1

(1 − λ)λn−1 = 1 . Then the TD (λ) method, as an extension of the TD(0) method, updates vπ(x) by 

vπ(x) ← vπ(x) + α(Gπ,λ t (x) − vπ(x)) . (4.59) In fact, the TD (λ) method is just an interpolation of the TD(0) method and the Monte Carlo method: When λ = 0 , we have 

Gπ, 0 

> t

(x) = Gπt, 0(x) = Rt+1 + γv π(Xt+1 )

and hence TD (λ) is exactly the TD(0) method; when λ → 1, all the weights in (4.58) approximate 1, and the TD (λ) becomes the Monte Carlo method asymptotically. For any policy π, the value vπ(x) updated by (4.57) iteratively is provably convergent to the true value function of π provided proper assignments of step sizes and infinite amount of episode data. We omit the proofs here. Same as the Monte Carlo method, after the value function vπ is updated, we need to find a policy π′ greedy with respect to vπ, and then repeat the process of the TD update (4.57) using π′ to estimate the new function vπ′

,and so on. Therefore, the TD method is similar to the Monte Carlo method, but considered more efficient in using data than the latter in practice. 

The SARSA Algorithm 

The SARSA algorithm is also a model-free method that has a lot of similarity as the TD method and applicable to problems of small to moderate sizes. The difference is the SARSA algorithm learns the quality function (the state-action value function) qπ instead of the state value function vπ in the TD method. Given a policy π, the agent can perform actions and generate a Markov decision process 

X0, U 0, R 1, . . . , X t, U t, R t+1 , X t+1 , U t+1 , . . . (4.60) To learn qπ, the SARSA algorithm also keeps a tabular of qπ on X × U in computer memory. Then by sampling an episode of (4.60), we observe a sample trajectory of the Markov decision process 

x0, u 0, r 1, . . . , x t, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 , u t+1 , . . . 

and updates qπ(x, u ) by 

qπ(xt, u t) ← qπ(xt, u t) + α(rt+1 + γq π(xt+1 , u t+1 ) − qπ(xt, u t)) . (4.61) We can see a lot of similarities between (4.57) and (4.61) (we directly use 

qπ(xt, u t) instead of qπ(x, u ) in (4.61) but the idea is the same as vπ(xt)4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 203 in (4.57)). The goal of (4.61) is to make rt+1 + γq π(xt+1 , u t+1 ) equal to 

qπ(xt, u t) in expectation provided xt+1 is a sample following p(·| xt, u t), yield-ing (4.26). As each of such updates requires a tuple (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 , u t+1 )

extracted from the sample trajectory, the SARSA algorithm got its name as a brevity of (State, Action, Reward, State, Action). For any policy π, the function qπ updated by the SARSA scheme (4.61) iteratively can also be proved to converge to the true Q-function of π. Then we can update the policy to the one greedy with respect to π, namely, 

π′(u|x) =  

> {

1, if u = arg max u′∈U qπ(x, u ′),

0, otherwise . (4.62) The greedy policy above is chosen this way because 

T π′

vπ(x) = T ∗vπ(x) = max  

> ˆπ∈Π

EU ′∼ˆπ(·| x)[qπ(x, U ′)] = max  

> u′∈U

qπ(x, u ′),

provided a maximizer can be attained in U for every x ∈ X . Once we have π′, we can generate new sample trajectories following π′, and apply the SARSA scheme (4.61) on the trajectories to obtain qπ′

, and so on. This process generates a sequence of policies π1, π 2, . . . , π k, . . . , where πk+1 is greedy with respect to vπk for every k. By Policy Improvement Theorem (Theorem 4.1.16), we know πk converges to π∗.In practice, the SARSA algorithm does not need a very long episode to update q, because it can be updated continuously during an episode as in (4.61) and terminated any time. A main drawback of the SARSA algorithm is that it requires tuple 

(xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 , u t+1 ) to implement the update (4.61). If some state-action pair (xt+1 , u t+1 ) is not sampled, which often happens when the current esti-mate of q suggests that certain action ut+1 is unlikely valuable at the state 

xt+1 , then we may never be able to update q(xt+1 , u t+1 ).This drawback is a common issue in model-free algorithms, and the key behind is about the tradeoff between exploitation and exploration in reinforcement learning. Exploitation means that the algorithm always takes the best action according to the previously obtained state-action value q,whereas exploration reserves some probability to try other actions and may discover even better actions. A real-life analogue is that by exploitation we always go to our current favorite restaurant and try all items on its menu, versus by exploration we occasionally try other restaurants randomly and see if they have better dishes. In many model-free reinforcement learning algorithms, exploration is implemented by the ϵ-greedy policy 

π(u|x) =  

> 
> ϵ
> |U|

+ 1 − ϵ, if u = arg max u′∈U qπ(x, u ′), 

> ϵ
> |U|

, otherwise . (4.63) 204 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

for some small ϵ ∈ (0 , 1) , where |U| < ∞, e.g., U is a finite set or a continuous compact subset in an Euclidean space. (We can use probabilities other than uniform distribution for cases where U is unbounded.) To implement this, we can first draw a Bernoulli sample with two possible outcomes: Head with probability 1 − ϵ versus Tail with probability ϵ, then we take the maximizer 

u when we get the Head and randomly choose one from U with uniform distribution when we get the Tail. Similar to the n-step temporal difference method, the SARSA algorithm may also use a longer tuple 

(xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 , u t+1 , . . . , r t+n, x t+n, u t+n)

in a sample trajectory of the Markov decision process. In this case, we have the variant of the SARSA algorithm, called the n-step SARSA algorithm which updates q by 

qπ(xt, u t) ← qπ(xt, u t)+ α

> (

rt+1 + · · · + γn−1rt+n + γnqπ(xt+n, u t+n) − qπ(xt, u t)

> )

.

The Q-learning Algorithm 

The Q-learning algorithm is a variant of the SARSA algorithm by changing the goal of learning the Q-function qπ of the current estimate of policy π

to learning the optimal Q-function q∗ directly. This is similar to the value iteration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.2) which learns the optimal state value function v∗). Recall from (4.27) that the optimal state-action value function 

q∗ satisfies the Bellman equation 

q∗(x, u ) = r(x, u ) + EX′∼p(·| x,u )[v∗(X′)] = r(x, u ) + EX′∼p(·| x,u )[max  

> u′∈U

q∗(X′, u ′)] . (4.64) The transition probability p(·| x, u ) is determined by the environment, not any policy. Therefore, if we observe a transition triple (xt, u t, x t+1 ) in an episode, we can take v∗(xt+1 ) as an unbiased estimate of EX′∼p(·| xt,u t)[v∗(X′)] .Similar to the update rule in the SARSA algorithm, the update of q in the Q-learning algorithm is 

q(xt, u t) ← q(xt, u t) + α

> (

rt+1 + γ max  

> u′∈U

q(xt+1 , u ′) − q(xt, u t)

> )

. (4.65) The convergence of q updated above to q∗ can also be verified under certain conditions. The rest part of the Q-learning algorithm is the same as the SARSA algorithm. Once q∗ is learned by iterating (4.65), we can find a policy greedy with respect to q∗ as in (4.62) and it will be an optimal policy. Compared to the SARSA algorithm, the major advantage of the Q-learning algorithm is that only tuples of form (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 ) are used as samples. They do not include ut+1 and hence is less data demanding than the SARSA algorithm. 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 205 

Summary At this point, we can take a quick summary about the model-free methods we discussed so far, including the Monte Carlo method, the temporal difference (TD) method, the SARSA and Q-learning algorithms. More specifically, given a policy π, the Monte Carlo method uses complete episodes to estimate the value function vπ, the TD(0) method uses the current estimation vπ to itself (a bootstrapping strategy), and the TD (λ)

method is an interpolation between the TD(0) method and the Monte Carlo method. For both Monte Carlo and TD methods, a new policy greedy with respect to vπ is obtained and the process is repeated. They can be regarded as model-free versions of the policy iteration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.1). By contrast, the SARSA algorithm learns the Q-function qπ for the cur-rent policy π, and then update π to the policy greedy with respect to qπ.Then the sequence of policies generated by the SARSA algorithm is expected to converge to the optimal policy π∗. Therefore, the SARSA algorithm can also be thought of as a model-free version of the policy iteration algorithm, but it learns qπ instead of vπ in each iteration. The Q-learning algorithm directly learns the optimal Q-function q∗ by updating q in the rule (4.65). Upon convergence, (an approximation of) q∗

is obtained and the optimal policy is set to the one greedy with respect to 

q∗. Therefore, the Q-learning algorithm can be considered as a model-free version of the value iteration algorithm (Algorithm 4.2.2), except that it is approximating q∗ instead of v∗.All these methods can be applied to problems of small to moderate sizes, as they need to store either vπ or qπ as a tabular in computer memory. We consider their variants that can be used for large problems next. 

Deep Q-Network (DQN) Algorithm 

We consider a variant of the Q-learning algorithm, called the deep Q-network (DQN) algorithm, that can solve model-free reinforcement problems with large state space X and action space U. The key in the DQN algorithm is using a function approximator to approximate the optimal Q-function q∗.We use deep neural networks as such approximators. Recall that the Bellman equation of q∗ is given by (4.64). Therefore, it is natural to consider the loss function for training q∗ as 

12

> ∣∣∣

q∗(xt, u t) −

> (

rt+1 + γ max  

> u′∈U

q∗(xt+1 , u ′)

> )∣ ∣∣2

(4.66) for a sample tuple (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 ). By running many episodes, we can get many sample tuples of form (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 ), and take the average of them as the loss function of the Q-network. In each iteration, we can take an ϵ-greedy policy π with respect to the current estimate of q∗, and run episodes again to update q∗.206 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

In practice, the DQN algorithm uses an experience replay buffer to store previously obtained (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 ). Note that, once (xt, u t) is deter-mined, rt+1 = r(xt, u t) or rt+1 ∼ pR(·| xt, u t), and xt+1 ∼ p(·| xt, u t) are independent of policies, so the data obtained from a policy earlier can be stored in the experience replay buffer, and reused in the training of q∗ for a new policy. This mitigates the issue of sampling dependency if we only use samples generated by following the current policy, because the same samples can be reused by different policies and the overall sampling complexity can be improved. Notice that an issue with the DQN setting above is that q∗ appears at both places in the loss (4.66). This often causes the training unstable because once the current estimate q∗ prefers a specific state-action sequel, then it will be further encouraged during the later training and cause larger estimation error. To mitigate this issue, we can set two DQNs, denoted by q

and ˆq, which are called the training DQN and the target DQN, respectively. When we update q, we keep ˆq fixed and solve 

min

12

Double Deep Q-Networks Algorithm 

Notice that, in the DQN algorithm above, we fix ˆq when we update q.Namely, the target used to update q is given by the following function:

Policy Gradient Algorithm 

The Monte Carlo algorithm, the temporal difference algorithm, and the SARSA algorithm all aim at learning the state value function vπ or the state-action value function qπ for some given policy π. On the other hand, the Q-learning algorithm, the DQN method and their variants attempt to learn q∗. Only when the optimal v∗ is q∗ is learned, these methods set the optimal policy to be the one greedy to these value function. The policy gradient algorithm takes a different approach by learning the optimal policy π∗ directly. In the policy gradient algorithm, we also parametrize the policy as a deep neural network. This network is either a mapping from the state space X to the action space U, or a probability distribution on U for every x ∈ X . However, we need a scalar-valued objec-tive function to train this policy π∗. Specifically, we need to compute the gradient of the objective function with respect to the policy (more precisely, the network parameters of this policy network). Suppose we currently have an estimated policy π, then we can generate a sample episode of arbitrary length t:

zt = ( x0, u 0, x 1, u 1, . . . , x t, u t), (4.69) where x0 ∼ p0 for some initial state distribution (which is given beforehand and independent on any specific policy), and ut ∼ π(·| xt) determined by the policy π and xt+1 ∼ p(·| xt, u t) by the environment at any time t. and approximate the optimal policy π∗.

The Actor-Critic Algorithm 

Notice that the algorithms we discussed above either learn the state (or state-action) value function (such as the DQN and double DQN algorithms) or the policy (such as the policy gradient algorithm). The actor-critic (AC) algorithm is a new one that learns both of the state value function vπ and the actor π jointly. In the AC algorithm, both π and vπ are parameterized as deep neural networks, and they are called the actor network and the critic network, respectively. The goal of the critic network is to evaluate the value function vπ of the current policy determined by the actor network π,whereas the goal of the actor network is to improve π based on the feedback from vπ given by the critic network. More specifically, recall that 

∇πJ(π) = EZT ∼ρπT

to replace ∑Ts=t γsRs+1 with

> s=t

γs−tRs+1 = γt(rt+1 ) + γv π(xt+1 ),

where vπ is the critic network. Once the actor network π is updated by the gradient ascent algorithm or alike on (4.81), we can turn to update the critic network vπ by minimizing 

12 |rt+1 + γv π(xt+1 ) − vπ(xt)|2. (4.82) In practical implementations, rt+1 +γv π(xt+1 ) above is set as the target state value function and the gradient of (4.82) with respect to the parameter of 4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 211 

vπ is only applied to the second vπ. Namley, the approximate gradient of (4.82) is set to −(rt+1 + γv π(xt+1 ) − vπ(xt)) · ∇ vπ vπ(xt) empirically in the AC algorithm. 

Trust-Region Policy Optimization (TRPO) Algorithm 

While the actor-critic (AC) algorithm establish a new framework of alter-nately updating the policy (the actor) and its value function (the critic), its empirical computations often behave unstably. One of the major reasons is that the gradients with respect to the network parameters usually appear overly aggressive and it may need better restrains to mitigate the stability issue. We recall that the policy gradient algorithm considered the gradient of the following function 

J(π) = EZT ∼ρπT [R(ZT )] 

with respect to the current policy π, where we also assume here that the sampled episode is infinitely long and the total reward is given by R(zT ) =  

> ∑∞
> t=0

γtr(xt, u t) for ease of discussion below. We also notice that this func-tion can be written as 

J(π) = EX∼p0 [vπ(X)] = EX∼p0

> [

EU ∼π(·| X)[qπ(X, U )] 

> ]

. (4.83) As we can see, the unstable computation of the policy gradient step is mainly because the estimation error in vπ or qπ when they are parameterized deep neural networks. In this case, it can be expensive to use π to generate new episodes as many risky sample actions may occur, raising the practical cost of data generations. To overcome this issue, the trust-region policy optimization (TRPO) algorithm modifies the objective function in (4.83) by introducing a pre-viously estimated policy πk (assuming it behaved well during the sample episode generation processes): 

J(π) = EX∼p0

> [

> ]]

. (4.84) It is easy to verify that the new objective function (4.84) is identical to the one in (4.83). As the TRPO algorithm requires that the gradient with respect to the network parameters of the policy π should not be overly aggressive, it pro-poses to constrain the difference between the previous πk(·| x) and the new 

π(·| x) at every x ∈ X , and assumes that the unknown Q-function qπ can be well approximated by the previously obtained qπk . More precisely, the 212 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

TRPO algorithm proposes the following scheme to update the policy π:

k ≥ 1}. The advantage is that the adjacent policies are required to be close in the sense of KL divergence (4.85b) in order to avoid unstable updates of the network parameters of πk.One can design many variants of the TRPO algorithms based on the idea presented above. For the original TRPO algorithm (4.85), the objective function in (4.85a) can be approximated by an affine function of π, and (4.85b) can be approximated as an inequality constraint on a quadratic function of the network parameters of π. With these approximations, the computational cost of (4.85) can be considerably lower, but is still a bit expensive compared to some other variants we will introduce later. As a final remark related to the TRPO algorithm and its variants, we note that an empirically promising approach to further stabilize the numer-ical computation is to introduce the so-called baseline into the objective function. The reason is that, while the objective function such as (4.84) appears to be an unbiased estimate of the true objective function (4.83), the sample variances are often large and can cause undesired convergence issues. The baseline function is designed to be another unbiased estimator of the true objective function, and when it is subtracted from (4.84), the estimate remains unbiased. Meanwhile, the goal of this baseline function is to reduce the sample variance and improve the empirical convergence stability of the TRPO algorithm and its variants. There are multiple choices of designing the baseline function. One typi-cal choice is to implement the policy network π(·| x) as a normal distribution given any x ∈ X , so that the KL divergence in (4.85b) has closed form ex-pression and is easier to handle. Meanwhile, we design the baseline function to be subtracted from (4.85a) as

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 213 function (4.85a) is replaced with qπk (X, U )−vπk (X). This is shown to reduce the computation instability issue in many numerical experiments. 

Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) Algorithm 

The proximal policy optimization (PPO) algorithm is a followup variant of the TRPO algorithm above. With some moderate changes to the TRPO, the PPO algorithm often presents stronger learning performance and faster numerical computations. The idea of the PPO algorithm is to use a relaxed version of the Lagrangian method to handle the constraint (4.85b). By relaxed version it means that the Lagrangian multiplier is updated in a heuristic manner. More specifically, the penalty version of the PPO algorithm modifies the scheme (4.85) in the TRPO algorithm as an unconstrained problem: 

˜J(π) := EX∼p0

where aπk (x, u ) := qπk (x, u )−vπk (x) is called the advantage function (notice that it can be either positive or negative, depending whether the action u

in qπk brings over-average value compared to its expected value vπk ), which is commonly used in many model-free reinforcement learning algorithms, λ

plays the role of the Lagrange (KKT) multiplier. In the PPO algorithm, λ

is simply updated to λ/ 2 if the KL divergence is smaller than 2δ/ 3; to 2λ if the KL is larger than 3δ/ 2; and remains unchanged otherwise, where δ > 0

is again a user-chosen hyperparameter. Another simple yet effective version of the PPO algorithm is called the PPO-Clip. In PPO-Clip, the objective function is changed to the following one:

This PPO-Clip version is simpler to implement than the relaxed Lagrangian approach and appears to be more very effective in many empirical tests. 214 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DPG) Algorithm 

The deep deterministic policy gradient (DPG) algorithm is an approach that avoids sampling actions in the learning process. By assuming the policy to be greedy, the DPG algorithm can directly merge the policy network (the actor network) into the value network (the critic network) in the actor-critic algorithm. Since greedy policies are all deterministic, the actor in the DPG algo-rithm can be set to a deep neural network w : X → U . In other words, the deterministic policy π reduces to the following one-hot probability: 

π(u|x) :=  

> {

1, at w(x) where w(x) = arg max u∈U q(x, u ),

0, at every u ∈ U \ { w(x)}.

If there are multiple maximizers, then w(x) can be set to any one of them. Since the actor network w always outputs a maximizer in U, the critic in the DPG algorithm can be set to the optimal Q-function q∗, which is also parameterized as a deep neural network defined on X × U . Assuming the critic network q∗ is differentiable with respect to the argument u ∈ U , we have 

∂uq∗(x, w (x)) ∇ww(x),

where ∇ww(x) should be interpreted as the gradient of the actor network w

with respect to its network parameters. Notice that the use of the greedy policy w(x) results in 

q∗(x, w (x)) = max  

> π∈Π

EU ∼π(·| x)[q∗(x, U )] = v∗(x).

Therefore, we can define an objective function J of w to update the param-eters of w as follows: 

J(w) := EX∼p0 [v∗(X)] = EX∼p0 [q∗(X, w (X))] .

We see that the gradient of J(w) with respect to w can be evaluated by 

∇wJ(w) = ∇wEX∼p0 [q∗(X, w (X))] = EX∼p0 [∂uq∗(X, w (X)) ∇ww(X)] .

The update of the optimal Q-function q∗ in the DPG algorithm is similar to the Q-learning algorithm. More specifically, we need to sample tuples of form (xt, u t, r t+1 , x t+1 ) by letting the agent behave in the environment following the current estimated policy π, namely, w. Then for each sample tuple, we would like to minimize the error 

12 |rt+1 + γq ∗(xt+1 , w (xt+1 )) − q∗(xt, u t)|2.4.2. REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS 215 In the training process, the DPG algorithm approximates the gradient of the quantity above with respect to the network parameters of q∗ as 

−

(

rt+1 + γq ∗(xt+1 , w (xt+1 )) − q∗(xt, u t)

)

· ∇ q∗ q∗(xt, u t),

where ∇q∗ q∗(xt, u t) should be interpreted as the gradient of q∗ with respect to its network parameters at the given sample pair (xt, u t). As we can see, the term rt+1 + γq ∗(xt+1 , w (xt+1 )) is considered as the target and the gradient ∇q∗ is not applied to the q∗ in this term. In practice, the gradient above is averaged over all sampled tuples to update q∗.There are also many variants of the DPG algorithms. For example, we can parameterize π as some typical probability distribution, such as the normal distribution when U is a continuous subset of some Euclidean space. This improves the the exploration ability of the learning process and may discover better actions compared to learning greedy actions only. 

The Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) Algorithm 

The deep deterministic policy gradient (DPG) algorithm sometimes demon-strates problematic convergence behaviors since the gradients with respect to the network parameters of q∗ are often not accurate. One resolution to tackle this issue is adding a regularization to the current estimated policy 

π. To this end, recall that the objective function of π can be defined by 

J(π) := EX∼p0

[

In either case, we can evaluate ∇πJ(π), the gradient of J with respect to the network parameters of π, provided the current estimated Q-function qπ.

# 4.3 Remarks and References 

4.3.1 Reinforcement Learning and Optimal Control 

Reinforcement learning and optimal control are two closely related frame-works for sequential decision making. Both frameworks study how an agent should act over time in order to optimize specified cumulative and/or ter-minal performance objectives, and the methodologies are also extended to multi-agent cases. Historically, optimal control originates from control the-ory and applied mathematics, while reinforcement learning emerged from artificial intelligence and psychology. Despite their shared foundations, the two fields differ in modeling assumptions, solution methodologies, and areas of application. In the standard optimal control setting, the control systems are typically modeled as controlled dynamical system described by an ODE. There are two major classes of optimal control problems: One is with fixed end-time T

but free endpoint x(T ), while the other one is fixed endpoint x(T ) but free end-time T , as we have shown in Section 3.1. In either case, the formulation 4.3. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 217 of the ODE as well as the running and/or terminal rewards (or equivalently the costs) are known, the optimality conditions of the optimal control can be characterized by the Pontryagin Maximum Principle and the Hamilton– Jacobi–Bellman equation in continuous time [12, 23]. Reinforcement learning, on the other hand, typically models decision making as a Markov decision process as we shown in Section 4.1. In rein-forcement learning problems, the transition dynamics p(x′|x, u ) and the re-ward function can be unknown, and they need to learned during the solution process in finding the optimal control (called optimal policy in reinforcement learning). For these problems, a class of different methods, typically model-free algorithms, are widely used. We have shown some representative and influential algorithms in Section 4.2. It is known that the theory of reinforcement learning and optimal con-trol are strongly connected. For instance, when the system dynamics are known and the state and action spaces are continuous, reinforcement learn-ing reduces to stochastic optimal control. In this case, dynamic program-ming provides the unifying framework for both fields and Bellman theory underpins the optimality conditions and algorithmic developments in both fields [17]. Moreover, the value function in reinforcement learning is di-rectly analogous to its counterpart in optimal control, and the policy gra-dient methods have strong connections to optimal control via Pontryagin Maximum Principle and trajectory optimization. The literature has shown equivalences between certain reinforcement learning objectives and stochas-tic control formulations, including entropy-regularized control and path in-tegral control [74, 146]. Despite the connections above, important differences between reinforce-ment learning and optimal control remain. For instance, as we mentioned, optimal control often assumes known dynamics, while reinforcement learning emphasizes learning from data with minimal prior knowledge. As a result, solution quality of optimal control problems heavily rely on accurate mod-els, whereas reinforcement learning algorithms often need to trade off be-tween computation efficiency and data (both provided and generated) qual-ity. Moreover, exploration is central to reinforcement learning but largely absent in the standard optimal control where uncertainty is handled via robust or stochastic formulations. Nevertheless, the boundary between reinforcement learning and optimal control has started to blur after recent research advances [115]. In particular, deep learning has become a powerful tool to tackle large-scale and high-dimensional problems in both fields. The common issues raised by using deep neural networks, such as stability, robustness, and generalizability, may be addressed by further theoretical and algorithmic developments. To that end, understanding their relationship can provide valuable insights for designing efficient and reliable decision-making systems. 218 4. DEEP REINFORCEMENT LEARNING 

4.3.2 References of Reinforcement Learning 

A comprehensive introduction to reinforcement learning is presented in [140]. The work [142] provides a systematic review of classic reinforcement learn-ing algorithms. Section 4.1 largely follows the line of theory part presented in [142], with significant expansions on the proof details and new main re-sults through Lemma 4.1.15 and Theorem 4.1.17 which clarify the relations between ¯v and the fixed point v∗ of the optimal Bellman operator T ∗. Re-inforcement learning and optimal control as well as their connections are studied in depth in [15]. Deep reinforcement learning has emerged in the past decade. Recent surveys can be found in [6, 44, 152]. Classic reinforcement algorithms, such as policy iteration, value itera-tion, and temporal difference method, are discussed in detail in [140, 142]. The Q-learning method was developed in [155] and showed to be convergent to a fixed point of the Bellman operator in [156]. The connection between dynamic programming and reinforcement learning was first shown in [155]. The SARSA algorithm was developed by [124]. Early work on Monte Carlo methods was presented in [134]. The original policy gradient method [141] and actor-critic method [80] were proposed much earlier than deep learning, but they have substantial influences to the developments of modern rein-forcement learning algorithms. The deep Q-learning algorithm pioneering deep reinforcement learning was shown to achieve human-level performance across a benchmark of Atari games [99]. Another breakthrough demon-strated by deep reinforcement learning around the same time is the AlphaGo algorithm [132], which beat the world champion at Go, a classic board game considered to be impossible for computer to win human masters before deep learning emerged. Deep reinforcement learning was also employed to faster ways to multiply matrices using fewer multiplication operations [41]. There are substantial amount of works conducted for improved deep re-inforcement learning algorithms in the past years. The dueling Q-network architecture was proposed in [153], where two heads of the same network predict the state value function and the advantage function. The standard Q-learning shows systematic overestimation of the state values due to the max operation. The double Q-network architecture was proposed to remedy this overestimation issue [149]. In [43], the noisy deep Q-network is intro-duced to add noise for stochasticity and encourage exploration, and the network can learn to decrease the noise over time as it converges. Distri-butional deep Q-network architecture was proposed in [10, 31] aims to learn more complete information about the distribution of reward than just the expectation. A deep network architecture combining several aforementioned Q-network features was developed to improve both the training speed and the final performance on the ATARI benchmark [61]. The prioritized expe-rience replay method to improve training stability and speed was developed in [127]. Extensions to advantage functions were also developed in [129]. 4.3. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 219 These methods are variants of integrating deep learning into approximation of value functions, including state value functions, Q-functions (state-action value functions) and advantage functions. Deep neural networks were also employed to learn optimal policies of re-inforcement learning problems. The trust region policy optimization (TRPO) algorithm was developed in [128]. The sequential work called the proximal policy optimization (PPO) algorithm was proposed in [130]. In [133], the deep deterministic policy gradient (DDPG) algorithm was developed. An actor-critic model-free algorithm based on the DDPG that can operate over continuous action spaces was introduced in [84]. Soft actor-critic algorithms were developed in [58, 59]. This field is undergoing very rapid research ad-vancements. More powerful, stable, and robust deep reinforcement learning algorithms are expected to developed in near future. 220 4. # Generative Models 

This chapter introduces generative models , an important class of deep learn-ing methods that have emerged over the past decade for modeling com-plex data distributions and synthesizing new samples. They have vast real-world applications and can be used to generate realistic texts, music, images, videos and so on, called as artificial intelligence generated content (AIGC). The prototype mathematical problem underlying generative modeling can be formulated as follows. Suppose we are given a dataset D = {xi ∈

Rd : i ∈ [M ]} consisting of M samples independently drawn from some unknown probability distribution pdata on Rd. The objective of generative modeling is to learn how to generate additional samples that follow the same distribution pdata .This task is challenging for several reasons. First, only the finite dataset 

D is available, while the analytical form of pdata is unknown. Second, the dimension d can be extremely large. For example, each xi may represent an image, in which case d often reaches 10 4 or higher. The problem becomes even more involved in the setting of conditional generation, where the goal is to generate samples from an unknown con-ditional distribution pdata (· | y) for any user-specified conditioning variable (often called a prompt ) y. The purpose is to generate samples with proper-ties aligned with the need or preference of the user. In this chapter, we focus primarily on unconditional generative models, as their theoretical foundations and methodological developments are more mature. Moreover, many existing conditional generative models build di-rectly upon the theoretical insights and computational techniques originally developed for the unconditional setting. Generative modeling has undergone rapid advances in recent years, yet there is still no unified framework that fully resolves the problem. This chap-ter presents several of the most influential generative modeling paradigms in the literature and examines their constructions and properties in detail. We begin with the classical variational autoencoder framework and the closely 221 222 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

related generative adversarial networks approach, which emerged around the same time. We then discuss more recent developments, including diffu-sion models, probability density control, and flow matching methods, which leverage stochastic or deterministic differential equations for sample gener-ation. Finally, we address practical and theoretical issues associated with these models and briefly discuss extensions and modifications for conditional generation. 

# 5.1 Variational Autoencoder 

Variational autoencoder (VAE) is a network architecture that can generate new samples of pdata by using a dataset D consisting of finitely many of its independently drawn samples. The VAE model consists of two functions, called the encoder and the decoder. The encoder and decoder are typically parameterized as deep neural networks, denoted by gη and fθ with network parameters η and θ, respectively. The encoder gη learns to map every data sample x ∈ D to a latent variable z, which lies in the latent space Rm. Instead of directly determining 

z with a given input x, the output gη(x) is often set to be the parameters of some probability distribution. The typical choice is that gη(x) is a pair of a vector μη(x) ∈ Rm and a positive scalar ση(x) > 0 (or a positive vector in Rm

as the diagonal of a diagonal covariance matrix, or even directly an m-by-m

symmetric positive definite matrix as a covariance matrix, but we will only consider the simple case with scalar-valued ση given that the generalizations are straightforward). Then μη(x) and ση(x) are set to be the mean and standard deviation, respectively, of a Gaussian distribution, from which the latent variable z is drawn. More precisely, z is a sample drawn from the conditional probability distribution qη( · | x) := N ( · ; μη(x), σ η(x)2Im). This can be easily implemented as z = μη(x) + ση(x)ϵ with ϵ ∼ N (0 , I m) in practice. The decoder fθ aims at mapping any z ∈ Rm to a new sample x′ ∈ Rd.Again, instead of directly determining x′ based on z, the output fθ(z) for each input z is set to be a pair of a vector μθ(z) ∈ Rd and a positive scalar 

σθ(z) > 0. Then the new sample x′ is set to be a sample drawn from the conditional probability distribution pθ(·| z) := N ( · ; μθ(z), σ θ(z)2Id). This is implemented as x′ = μθ(z) + σθ(z)ϵ with ϵ ∼ N (0 , I d) in practice. One additional requirement on the encoder gη is that the marginal dis-tribution 

qη(z) := 

> ∫

qη(z|x)pdata (x) dx 

is approximately the standard Gaussian N (0 , I m), such that, after gη and 

fθ are trained, we can easily draw samples from this marginal qη and apply the decoder fθ on them to obtain new samples following pdata .5.1. VARIATIONAL AUTOENCODER 223 Original data space Rd

We first simulate z ∼N (0 , I m) and ϵ ∼ N (0 , I d), and set x = μθ(z) + σθ(z)ϵ, then x is a new sample following the probability pdata . We can simulate any number of pairs (z, ϵ ) independently and simultantenously as such and compute the corresponding x’s, which are all new samples following the probability dis-tribution pdata .

# 5.2 Generative Adversarial Networks 

Generative adversarial networks (GANs) is a deep network architecture de-veloped around the same time as the variational autoencoder framework. The idea of GANs is straightforward and simple. Let D = {xi ∈ Rd : i ∈

[M ]} be a given dataset consisting of M samples independently drawn from some unknown probability distribution pdata on Rd as before. The idea of GANs is to construct a generator function gθ : Rd → Rd, parameterized as a 5.2. GENERATIVE ADVERSARIAL NETWORKS 229 

Dreal =

> {

(xi, 1) : xi ∈ D , i ∈ [M ]

> }

Dfake =

> {

(gθ(z), 0) : z ∼ N (0 , I d)

> }

Classifier aη

Figure 5.2: A schematic view of the generative adversarial networks (GANs). The dataset Dreal consists of the M given real samples in D with label 1. The dataset Dfake consists of generated samples gθ(z)’s with label 0, where 

gθ : Rd → Rd is the generator network with parameter θ, and z ∼ N (0 , I d).The classifier aη : Rd → (0 , 1) is an adversarial network with parameter 

η and assigns every input sample with a probability value in (0 , 1) that it believes the sample is real. deep neural network with parameter θ, such that gθ(z) is a sample following the probability pdata for any z ∼ N (0 , I d).Now the question is how to determine the parameter θ such that gθ(z)

indeed follows the unknown pdata . To this end, we notice that we have the real samples from D and generated samples gθ(z) with z ∼ N (0 , I d).Therefore, we can assign binary labels to these samples, i.e., we assign 1 to every sample from D and 0 to every generated (fake) sample gθ(z). More specifically, we denote 

Dreal := 

> {

(xi, 1) : xi ∈ D , i ∈ [M ]

> }

(5.19) and

> {

(gθ(zj ), 0) : zj ∼ N (0 , I d), j ∈ [N ]

> }

, (5.20) where 1 and 0 represent the labels of the samples. In (5.20), we assumed an arbitrary number N of generated samples. With the two datasets Dreal in (5.19) and Dfake , GANs further introduce a classifier, also called a discriminator , which is parameterized as an adver-sarial network aη : Rd → (0 , 1) with parameter η. This adversarial network 

aη is to be trained in order to distinguish between the generated samples from Dfake in (5.20) and the real ones from Dreal in (5.19). A schematic view of the GANs is provided in Figure 5.2. The functionality of aη is based on the Bernoulli probability distribution. Recall that for any p ∈ (0 , 1) and X ∼ Bernoulli (p), the probability of X = 1 

is p and the probability of X = 0 is 1 − p. Therefore, the probability mass function of X can be written as Prob (X = x) = px(1 − p)1−x for x = 0 

and 1. In light of this formulation, we can interpret aη(x) ∈ (0 , 1) as the probability of x being a real sample. Specifically, this probability mass function is aη(xi) for any xi from Dreal , and is aη(gθ(zj )) for any gθ(zj ) from 230 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

Dfake . Since all samples from Dreal and Dfake are independently drawn and the labels are already known to be 1 and 0 respectively, we can form their joint probability distribution as

> i=1

is fixed, then the classifier aη should be trained to assign 1 (or very close to 1) to all xi’s and 0 (or very close to 0) to all gθ(zj )’s. This is equivalent to maximizing E(θ, η ) with respect to η when θ is held fixed. By this means, the classifier aη can excel its discrimination ability and tell correctly whether a sample is real or generated. Conversely, if the classifier aη is fixed, then the generator gθ should be trained in order to confuse the classifier, i.e., attaining a parameter θ

such that aη(xi) = aη(gθ(zj )) = 12 for all xi and zj . This is equivalent to minimizing E(θ, η ) with respect to θ when η is held fixed. By this means, the generator gθ can excel its generation ability so that gθ(zj ) looks no different from a real sample to the classifier aη, and aη can only assign probability  

> 12

to whatever it receives, i.e., aη cannot tell whether the sample is real or generated at all. Based on the interpretation above, it is natural to set the problem of training θ and η as the following saddle point (minimax) problem:

The convergence can be unstable but there are some remedies to mitigate this issue in practical implementations. We can dive further into the saddle point problem (5.23) and see its meaning. Π( p, q ) :=

Wasserstein distance have many important mathematical properties, in-cluding being a well-defined metric in the space of probability distributions on Rd. It is one of the key concepts in the area of optimal transport, which has been extensively studied in the past decades. For more details about Wasserstein distances, see Appendix A.3. However, the main issue with Wasserstein distances is that they are complicated for numerical computa-tions due to their constrained structures and dimensionality. To solve the minimization (5.27), we can convert the Wasserstein-1 dis-tance to its dual form, which is relatively easier to handle. The dual form of the Wasserstein-1 distance W1(p, q ) is given by 

W1(p, q ) = max  

> f∈Lip 1(Rd)
> ∫

f (x)p(x) dx −

> ∫

f (x)q(x) dx, (5.29) where Lip 1(Rd) is the set of all 1-Lipschitz continuous functions defined on 

Rd, i.e., f ∈ Lip 1(Rd) if |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ | x − y| for all x, y ∈ Rd.With the dual of Wasserstein-1 distance in (5.29), we can rewrite the min-imization problem (5.27) in an equivalent form, which is the minimization problem in the so-called Wasserstein GAN model for learning the optimal parameter θ of the generator network gθ:

min

max

due to change of variables (5.24). In practical implementations, the integrals in (5.30) are replaced with Monte Carlo integrations, and f : Rd → R is parameterized as a deep neural network with proper constraints on its parameters such that f approximately satisfies f ∈ Lip 1(Rd). In this sense, f plays a similar role as the adversarial network aη in the original work of GANs. Interested readers may refer to the relevant literature listed at the end of this chapter for more details. After the generator network gθ is trained, we can first simulate as many samples as we want from N (0 , I d), and then apply gθ to these standard Gaussian samples to obtain new samples, which are new samples following the probability distribution pdata .

# 5.3 Diffusion Models 

Diffusion models is a new class of generative models. The goal is again learning how to generate new samples following an unknown probability distribution pdata on Rd, provided only a dataset D = {xi ∈ Rd : i ∈ [M ]}5.3. DIFFUSION MODELS 233 consisting of M samples independently drawn from pdata . Same as before, we do not know the analytic form of pdata .Diffusion models take a novel approach of sample generation by con-structing a stochastic differential equation (SDE) defined in Rd over the time interval [0 , T ], where T > 0 is arbitrarily chosen: 

dZ t = μt(Zt)dt + (Σ t(Zt)) 1/2dW t, (5.31) where 

μ : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd

is called the drift and 

Σ : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd×d

is called the diffusion coefficient. Both μ and Σ are time-dependent. The property of this SDE is that, from a simple initialization probability pinit 

on Rd which is often set to N (0 , I d) or alike, this SDE (5.31) can drive any initial value Z0 drawn from pinit at time t = 0 forward in time to ZT at 

t = T , such that ZT follows the probability pdata , i.e., ZT is a sample drawn from pdata .Note that, since the goal is to generate any number of new samples ZT ’s following pdata , we need the ability of easily drawing initials Z0’s following 

pinit . We also need the conditional probability distribution to have a closed form in order to implement the training loss function conveniently, which we will detail later. Due to these reasons, the initialization probability pinit 

is often chosen to be a simple one so that it is easy to draw samples from it. As mentioned above, a typical choice of pinit is the standard Gaussian distribution N (0 , I d) on Rd, or some trivial variant of it with shifted mean or scaled variance. For implementation simplicity and computation efficiency, Σ is often set to a state-independent scalar multiple of the identity matrix Id, namely, σI d,where σ : [0 , T ] → [0 , ∞) depends on time t only. This simplification does not affect the core of diffusion models and can be generalized easily. Now the goal of diffusion models is summarized as follows: Find a drift 

μ : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd and a diffusion coefficient σ : [0 , T ] → [0 , ∞), such that for any trajectory Zt with initial value Z0 ∼ pinit = N (0 , I d) and following the SDE (for notation simplicity, we denote μt(z) := μ(z, t ) ∈ Rd

and σt := σ(t) ∈ R hereafter): 

dZ t = μt(Zt)dt + σtdW t (5.32) over [0 , T ], there is ZT ∼ pdata . Once such μ and σ are found, we can draw 

Z0 ∼ pinit as many as we want, and numerically solve the SDE (5.32) (using numerical SDE solvers such as the Euler-Maruyama method) to obtain a new sample ZT ∼ pdata from each Z0.234 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

As we can see, the main task now is to find such μt and σt for (5.32). To this end, diffusion models first try to determine another SDE 

dX t = ft(Xt)dt + gtdW t (5.33) with some manually designed f : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd and diffusion coefficient 

g : [0 , T ] → [0 , ∞), so that (5.33) can do exactly the opposite of (5.32): Namely, for any initial X0 chosen to be one of the given samples following 

pdata , we can numerically solve (5.33) and get XT ∼ pinit . In this sense, the SDE (5.33) can transform pdata to pinit . The purpose of designing this new SDE (5.33) is to simulate its trajectories and learn the corresponding time-evolving probability. By doing so, we can learn the desired μt and σt,as will be shown later in this section. Before moving on, we remark that finding the proper f and g for (5.33) such that XT ∼ pinit from X0 ∼ pdata is highly non-trivial in general. We will discuss this issue further in this section. Nevertheless, it is easy to find 

f and g such that XT can approximately follow pinit when T is sufficiently large. For example, a common choice is using the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) process with a diffusion coefficient compatible with the drift: 

dX t = −aX tdt + √2a dW t. (5.34) That is, 

ft(x) = −ax and gt ≡ √2a (5.35) for all t and x, where a > 0 is a user-chosen constant. For this SDE (5.34), it is known that the conditional probability of Xt given its initial value 

X0 = x0 ∼ pdata is a Gaussian: 

N (x0e−at , (1 − e−2at )Id)

for any t ≥ 0. Therefore, we have 

XT |(X0 = x0) ∼ N (x0e−aT , (1 − e−2aT )Id) ≈ pinit := N (0 , I d)

for sufficiently large T with any x0, regardless of what pdata is. For now, we will accept the OU process (5.34) as our choice (5.33) and that X0 ∼ pdata 

and XT ∼ pinit = N (0 , I d).Now the questions are how these simple f and g in (5.35) relate to the desired μ and σ in (5.32), and how to use f and g to construct μ and σ.To answer these two questions, we notice that the Fokker–Planck equa-tion of the SDE (5.33) is given by 

∂tρt = −∇ · (ftρt) + 12 g2

∆ρt (5.36) 5.3. DIFFUSION MODELS 235 where ρ : Rd × [0 , T ] → [0 , ∞) is the probability density of Xt for every 

t ∈ [0 , T ], and ρ0 = pdata and ρT = pinit . On the other hand, we also have the Fokker–Planck equation of the SDE (5.32): 

∂tπt = −∇ · (μtπt) + 12 σ2

Sample generation using trained score function Once we have the approximate score function st, we can put it in the place of the true score function ∇ log ρt in (5.43). Notice that for the OU process, we have f and g

given in the simple forms in (5.35), i.e., ft = −ax and gt = √2a. Then the SDE (5.43) for generation is 

dZ t = ( −aZ t + 2 as t(Zt)) dt + √2a dW t. (5.55) The actual sample generation can be proceeded by following the Euler-Maruyama scheme: Partition [0 , T ] into intervals of equal length h > 0,and compute 

Zt+h = Zt + ( −aZ t + 2 as t(Zt)) h + √2ahζ t (5.56) for t = 0 , h, 2h, . . . , T , where the initial Z0 and the perturbations ζt’s are sampled from N (0 , I d) independently. Then ZT is a newly generated sample and follows the probability pdata .There is an obvious drawback of (5.56): At each time point t, we need to sample one ζt from the standard Gaussian. This can be very expensive when there are many time steps and we need to simulate many Gaussian samples. We can overcome this drawback by converting the SDE (5.55) into an ODE, such that the solution follow the same probability distribution πt at every t. To this end, we notice that ∆πt = ∇· (πt∇ log πt) in (5.42). Further-more, we know πt = ρT −t for all t ∈ [0 , T ] from Theorem 5.3.1. Therefore, 242 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

the Fokker–Planck equation (5.42) can be rewritten as a continuity equation of π:

∂tπt = −∇ ·

(−fT −t + 12 g2

)

, (5.57) and the corresponding ODE is

> T−t

∇ log ρT −t(zt). (5.58) where ˙zt is the time derivative of zt. In practice, we can substitute ∇ log ρT −t

with the trained score function sT −t, and solve this ODE (5.58) for zT

from any initial z0 drawn from N (0 , I d). The generated zT also follows the distribution πT = ρ0 = pdata as desired. There are many numerical ODE solvers, such as the midpoint method and the fourth-order Runge-Kutta method [24], that can solve the ODE (5.58) much faster than solving the SDE (5.55). The numerical error caused by these solvers is usually very small compared to the approximation error of the learned score function st to the true score function ∇ log ρt.

Other SDEs to generate sample trajectories for training There are actually many alternatives of the OU process (5.34) that we can use to design (5.33). For example, the following class of variance-preserving (VP) SDEs is a slight generalization of the OU process and often used in practical implementations: 

dX t = −γtdt + √2γtdW t, (5.59) where γt is chosen between [γmin , γ max ] for every t. Here the bounds γmin 

and γmax are some user-chosen positive numbers, and γmin is often set as a small positive number, such as 0.01, to prevent γt from being too close to 

0 and cause numerical instability in computation. On the other hand, the choice of γmax is rather flexible, such as 20. The VP SDE (5.59) also has solution following Gaussian conditioned on the initial value X0 = x0 like the OU process. More precisely, the conditional probability distribution of Xt given X0 = x0 is 

ρt(xt|x0) = N (xt; αtx0, β 2

Discussions on the choice of the SDE (5.33) As the sample data distribution pdata can vary in different applications, we would hope to find some “universal” drift f and diffusion coefficient g such that the forward-time SDE (5.33) can drive X0 ∼ ρ0 = pdata to XT ∼ ρT = pinit = N (0 , I d).By universal we meant that f and g are chosen such that the SDE (5.33), or equivalently the Fokker–Planck equation (5.36), can transfer any given 

pdata to N (0 , I d) in finite time T . In other words, the choice of f and g

should not depend on pdata . However, this is not always easy because pdata 

is an unknown and usually complicated probability distribution in practice. In fact, we can claim that such universal f and g do not exist, as shown in the next proposition. 

Proposition 5.3.4. If there exist f and g such that the Fokker–Planck equation (5.36) transfers ρ0 = pdata to ρT = N (0 , I d), then f and g must depend on pdata .Proof. We use a proof by contradiction. Assume that f and g do not depend on pdata . We know that there exists π as described in Proposition 5.3.1 such that πt = ρT −t almost everywhere in Rd for every t, where ρt satisfies (5.36) with such f and g and terminal values ρ0 = pdata and ρT = N (0 , I d). This implies that the Fokker–Planck equation (5.42) of π can be written as 

∂tπt = −∇ · 

> (

(−fT −t + g2 

> T−t

∇ log πt)πt

> )

+ 12 g2 

> T−t

∆πt.

with initial π0 = ρT = N (0 , I d). However, none of π0, f , and g includes any information about pdata . This is a contradiction to πT = ρ0 = pdata .As we see now, transferring from pdata to pinit in a finite time interval 

[0 , T ] is itself a non-trivial problem, perhaps not much simpler than trans-ferring from pinit to pdata . While we used the OU process (5.34) such that the probability distribution ρT is approximately pinit , we know it is not pos-sible to reach pinit = N (0 , I d) exactly within a finite time T , as justified in Proposition 5.3.4. Theoretically speaking, we should use the actual ρT

solved from the Fokker–Planck equation (5.36) or the SDE (5.33) as the initial distribution π0 in sample generation. However, this is not practically feasible because it is also difficult to evaluate or sample from ρT .244 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

It is natural to think whether there exists some proper drift f (and diffusion coefficient g) such that the solution ρ of the Fokker–Planck equation (5.36) satisfies ρ0 = pdata and ρT = N (0 , I d) (or the other way around, which is the original target generation problem). The answer to this question is positive provided certain mild conditions on pdata .In fact, this is about the feasibility of the so-called Schrödinger bridge problem (SBP): given two probability distributions p0 and p1 in Rd, the SBP can be written as 

min 

> f

12 

> ∫T
> 0
> ∫

|ft(x)|2ρt(x) dxdt, 

s.t. ∂tρt = −∇ · (ftρt) + σ2

ρ0 = p0, ρT = p1,

where σ > 0 is a given constant diffusion coefficient. If we set p0 = pdata and 

p1 = N (0 , I d), then the SBP does not only require transferring from pdata 

to N (0 , I d), which immediately answers the feasibility of f (or μ) we had earlier, but also attain the optimality regarding the objective function in (5.62). Nevertheless, finding either a feasible solution or an optimal solution to (5.62) is difficult in general. Despite the approximation issue mentioned above, diffusion models still perform well in practice. The discussions above indicate the limitation of diffusion models and suggest the necessity of learning the score function 

∇ log ρt to build the generating drift μt in (5.32). 

# 5.4 Probability Density Control 

In the derivation of diffusion models in Section 5.3, we have two state-independent scalar functions gt and σt. They are the diffusion coefficients in the Fokker–Planck equations (5.36) and (5.42), respectively, and satisfy 

σt = gT −t for all t, However, they do not play any critical role in the learning and generation process, but only determine the magnitude of fluctuations of the corresponding SDEs (5.33) and (5.43). In light of Theorem 5.3.1, we can simply choose gt = 0 and σt = 0 for all t, then the Fokker–Planck equations (5.36) and (5.42) reduces to the following two continuity equations 

∂tρt = −∇ · (ftρt) (5.63) and 

∂tπt = −∇ · (−fT −tπt) (5.64) respectively, defined on Rd × [0 , T ]. Moreover, ρ and π still satisfy

# 5.5 Flow Matching 

Flow matching is an alternative generative model framework that aims at directly learning the vector field to transfer N (0 , I d) to pdata . Same as before, we are only given a dataset D = {xi ∈ Rd : i ∈ [M ]} consisting of M samples independently drawn from pdata . In flow matching, our goal is to learn a vector field u : Rd × [0 , T ] → Rd using these samples, such that the solution 

xT to the ODE 

˙xt = ut(xt) (5.74) with any initial x0 ∼ N (0 , I d) satisfies xT ∼ pdata . As the value of T does not matter in the flow matching method, we set T = 1 in the remainder of this section. We again parameterize the target time-dependent vector field u as a deep neural network. Then the key of flow matching is to design a practi-cally implementable training problem of u. To this end, we adopt a similar approach used in diffusion models shown in Section 5.3 and consider some “conditional” vector fields first. Notice that transferring N (0 , I d) to pdata is difficult, but transferring 

N (0 , I d) to any specific sample z from pdata is easy. For example, for any fixed z ∼ pdata (i.e., z is a given data sample following pdata ), the easiest way of moving from any point ϵ ∈ Rd to z in the time window [0 , 1] is to follow the straight line from ϵ to z with a constant speed. Namely, we can move by following xt defined by 

xt = (1 − t)ϵ + tz. (5.75) It is clear that x0 = ϵ and x1 = z. We also notice two consequences of (5.75): 

˙xt = z − ϵ (5.76) and 

ϵ = xt − tz

˙xt = z − ϵ = z − xt

# 5.6 Remarks and References 

5.6.1 Sampling Noises in Generative Models 

In the variational autoencoder framework, diffusion model and flow match-ing methods discussed in Sections 5.1, 5.3 and 5.5, we often end up with a loss function in the form of 

> ∫∫

ϕ(x, ϵ )pdata (x)N (ϵ; 0 , I d) dϵdx (5.95) with some scalar-valued function ϕ of (x, ϵ ). Examples include (5.17), (5.53) and (5.93). The time variable t does not need to be taken into consideration here: (5.95) is supposed to be minimized at every t, and we just artificially sample t on [0 , T ] for numerical implementation in practice. Therefore t has a nature different from (x, ϵ ).We always use Monte Carlo integration to approximate the loss function of form (5.95). More specifically, we have a set of m (we used M in the previous sections of this chapter, but change to m here as we will consider sampling n Gaussian noises for each given data sample) given samples {xi ∈

Rd : i ∈ [m]}, which are assumed to be samples independently drawn from an unknown data distribution pdata . Meanwhile, we need to sample ϵ ∼N (0 , I d), the d-dimensional standard Gaussian distribution, independently. There are two ways to sample ϵ. The first way is to only sample one 

ϵi ∼ N (0 , I d) for each xi, and the second way is to sample many independent 

ϵ’s for each xi. In theory, the first way is correct in Monte Carlo integration because it uses pdata (x)N (ϵ; 0 , I d) as the joint probability distribution of 

(x, ϵ ) in (5.95). However, the second way is tempting since we can easily draw as many samples as we want from N (0 , I d), unlike the samples xi’s which are the only ones available. But is there any issue if we do the second way? For ease of discussion, we consider the case where we sample n indepen-dent ϵ’s for each xi. More precisely, suppose the samples we used to form the Monte Carlo integration for (5.95) are given by 

(xi, ϵ ij ), i ∈ [m], j ∈ [n], (5.96) 254 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

where xi’s and ϵij ’s are all independent. Then we approximate (5.95) by

ϕ(xi, ϵ ij ). (5.97) Therefore, the difference between the two ways we mentioned above is just that n = 1 in the first way and n > 1 in the second way. It is straightforward to see that the expectation of (5.97) is (5.95) re-gardless of the value of n. In other words, (5.97) is always an unbiased estimate of (5.95). Now we want to check the variances of (5.97), and see if the variance can be smaller when n > 1. To this end, we first derive a general result for similar cases.

and

Therefore, we need to check which of v and c is larger. In general, ϕ(x, ϵ ) is a complicated function, such as (5.53) and (5.93). We can see that ϕ contains some deep neural networks to be trained in these cases. Therefore, we cannot make a conclusion on which of v and c is larger in the general case. However, we notice that the benefit (the value of the gap (5.98)) may not be significant even if v − c is positive (and fixed): (i) Increasing n makes n−1 

> n

→ 1 and therefore the gap (5.98) becomes saturated for larger n but only the computational cost of (5.97) increases; and (ii) The larger sample size m (this is determined by the data we have and cannot be changed in the computation of (5.97)), the smaller the gap (5.98) is, which suggests that it may not be worth using large n if we already have a large amount of samples. Nevertheless, we can still consider a simple case where ϕ(x, ϵ ) = x + ϵ

where x and ϵ are both scalar-valued. Then we have Xi ∼ pdata in R and 

Eij , E ij ′ ∼ N (0 , 1) which are all independent. In this case, we have 

v = Var (Xi,E ij )[Xi + Eij ] = Var[ Xi] + 1 

and 

c = Cov (Xi,E ij ,E ij ′ )[Xi + Eij , X i + Eij ′ ] = Var[ Xi].

Therefore, we indeed find v > c in this simple case. Moreover, we notice that Xi + Eij and Xi + Eij ′ are dependent since their covariance is positive. This simple example also gives a warning that we need to be cautious when trying to create more “training samples” by adding noises or alike to the given ones, as these artificially created samples are statistically depen-dent (there covariances are nonzero). As a consequence, some assumptions in the theoretical derivations of the methods we discussed may be invalid. 

5.6.2 Conditional Generations 

The generative models discussed in this chapter aim at generating new sam-ples that resemble observed data following an unknown probability distri-bution pdata . In real-world scenarios, we often want to generate data condi-tional on some input information y ∈ Rk (represented as a vector here for ease of discussions below, while it can be a tensor of some specified shape in different methods) provided by users, such as class labels, attributes, text descriptions, or other modalities. This leads to the problem of conditional generation , the goal of which is to generate samples from conditional distri-bution pdata (x|y), enabling controllable, targeted, and interpretable genera-tion. Existing conditional generation frameworks differ mainly in how they represent, learn, and generate samples following pdata (x|y), but they all 5.6. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 257 strongly based on their corresponding unconditional generation prototypes like those given earlier in this chapter. Nevertheless, the common principle they share is injecting the conditioning variable y into the generative pro-cess, either explicitly through probabilistic modeling or implicitly through neural network architectures. Below we briefly introduce several existing approaches of conditional generation. Notice that we also call the process 

guided generation and y a guidance instead of condition which are common terms in the generative model community, as they sometimes help to avoid confusions with the various conditional probability distributions in deriva-tions. The conditional GANs is an approach that extends GANs by condition-ing both the generator gθ and discriminator aη on y. The generator gθ is parameterized as a neural network with parameter θ and learns the mapping from a naive sample z (often drawn from N (0 , I d) on Rd as in GANs) and a guidance y ∈ Rk to data space. As a result, the generator gθ can be used to generate samples following the induced conditional probability pθ(x|y).Meanwhile, the discriminator is parameterized as an adversarial network 

aη(·| y) that distinguishes real from fake samples given y. The training of parameters (θ, η ) is implemented by solving the saddle point problem 

min  

> θ

max 

> η
> { ∫∫

pdata (x, y ) log aη(x|y) dxdy 

+

> ∫∫

p(y)pθ(x|y) log(1 − aη(x|y)) dxdy 

> }

, (5.99) where in practical implementations the first integral in (5.99) is approxi-mated by Monte Carlo integration on the given dataset consisting of inde-pendent sample pairs (x, y ) drawn from pdata , and y is the guidance corre-sponding to x. The probability p(y) in (5.99) refers to the probability distri-bution of classes of guidances and is implemented by sampling all guidance of interest during the training process. Conditional GANs are particularly effective for sharp sample generation and are widely used in class-conditional synthesis and image-to-image translation. We can also apply the idea of sampling the conditional probability pdata (·| y)

in the flow matching framework. More specifically, we can replace the flow probability ρt(x) in the flow matching method (Section 5.5) with the con-ditional counterpart ρt(x|y) with guidance y. To this end, we first notice that 

ρt(x|y) = 

> ∫

ρt(x|y, z )pdata (z|y) dz =

> ∫

ρzt (x)pdata (z|y) dz (5.100) where ρzt is the probability flow with a given target z in (5.81), and pdata (z|y)

will again be replaced with given samples z labeled by guidance variable y

in the approximation of (5.100) by Monte Carlo integration. Notice that we assumed in (5.100) that ρt(x|y, z ) = ρt(x|z) = ρzt (x) as the path from 258 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

x to any specified z ∼ pdata is determined by x and z, not y, because the information about y is fully contained in z. Similar to the derivations in Section 5.5, we can show that the marginal distributions at the start time 

t = 0 and end time t = 1 are 

ρ0(·| y) = N (0 , I d) and ρ1(·| y) = pdata (·| y),

and the guided vector field ut(·|· ) : Rd × Rk → Rd is 

ut(x|y) = 

∫

> c∈Rd

EZ∼ρt(·| x,y )

,
