min 

> u

∫ 10

E(X,Y )∼pdata EZ∼ρt(·| X,Y )

[

|ut(X|Y ) − vZt (X)|2]

dt. (5.101) Notice that the minimization is again with respect to network parameters of u. The literature shows that such conditional generation is most effective in applications where each guidance y has a large amount of samples x in the given dataset, such as in class guidance [103]. However, this can be a challenge in general since many guidance signal y’s are non-repeating and complex, such as image captions [86]. Diffusion models can also be modified into conditional variants. There have been classifier guidance [32, 139] and classifier-free guidance [64] meth-ods developed to construct such variants. Specifically, by Bayes rule, we know 

ρt(x|y) = ρt(y|x)ρt(x)

p(y)

where p(y) is the distribution of guidance as above, and ρt(x) and ρt(x|y) are the unconditional and conditional probability distributions as above. Taking logarithm and then gradient with respect to x on both sides, we obtain 

∇x log ρt(x|y)

︸ ︷︷ ︸

> conditional score

= ∇x log ρt(y|x)

︸ ︷︷ ︸

> classifier score

+ ∇x log ρt(x)

︸ ︷︷ ︸

> unconditional score

. (5.102) Therefore, as long as we have the classifier score, we know the desired con-ditional score and can train network st(x|y) to approximate it following the derivations in diffusion models. Based on this relation, classifier guidance methods train a time-dependent function ϕt(y|x), parameterized as a deep neural network, to approximate the classifier score ∇xρt(y|x). We remark that this function ϕ is trained separately from the unconditional score in diffusion models, and more im-portantly, it is time-dependent and capable of approximating the classifier 5.6. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 259 score when x ∼ ρt(·) is a sample in the intermediate of the diffusion process. It is shown in [86] that the guided vector field is 

ut(x|y) = ut(x) + 1 − tt ϕt(y|x) (5.103) when using the line interpolation as in (5.75) in the diffusion model, while providing a formula table of customizable interpolation coefficients. This approach appears to approximate the guided vector field better than (5.101) for both class and text conditioning [104]. Since the classifier score and the unconditional score are learned sepa-rately, it is often necessary to calibrate the weight on the classifier score with a scalar γ ∈ R on ϕt in the second term on the right-hand side of (5.103). This scalar is typically chosen to be γ > 1 [32]. An alternative approach called classifier-free guidance (CFG) method, which does not need to train a separate classifier score function, was pro-posed in [64]. From (5.102), we see that the classifier score is the difference between the conditional score and the unconditional score. Therefore, the CFG method only trains a single network to approximate the conditional score ∇xρt(x|y), and takes a fixed vector ynull as the place-holder when the input sample z does not have a guidance signal (i.e., z ∼ pdata (·)). This is shown to result in the following guided vector field [86]: 

ut(x|y) = (1 − γ)ut(x|ynull ) + γu t(x|y). (5.104) The loss function for training u includes another layer of data selection: one first draw either ynull or a real guidance y based on Bernoulli( p) with a user-chosen p ∈ (0 , 1) , and then sample z without guidance if ynull is drawn, or with the drawn guidance y otherwise. The following steps are the same as in diffusion models [64]. The exact distribution which the CFG method samples from is unknown. Some followup works proposed different intuitive or theoretical justifications for CFG sampling, see, e.g., [22, 28, 57]. 

5.6.3 References of Generative Models 

Early work on generative modeling was dominated by classical probabilis-tic approaches, such as Gaussian mixture models, hidden Markov models, and other latent variable models with manually designed structures. While these methods are mathematically well-founded and interpretable, they of-ten struggle to scale to high-dimensional data such as images, audio signals, and natural language. The integration of deep neural networks as flexible function approximators marked a major shift, enabling generative models to capture complex, multimodal data distributions more effectively. The concept of autoencoder was presented in early works [62,123], whereas the goal was to establish a low-dimensional representations using latent vari-ables to capture the essence of given high-dimensional data. VAE was first 260 5. GENERATIVE MODELS 

introduced in [78]. A comprehensive introduction of VAE is given in [79]. VAE employs an encoder-decoder structure that maps data into a contin-uous latent space and reconstructs samples through a generative decoder. This formulation enables efficient sampling, structured representation learn-ing, and stable optimization. However, VAEs are often observed to produce overly smooth or blurry samples, reflecting a trade-off between likelihood-based objectives and perceptual fidelity. A substantial amount of improvements and extensions of VAE have been proposed. For example, conditional VAE was developed and showed that input conditions can be represented in different manners in latent spaces after encoding [136]. In [137], ladder VAEs are constructed to recursively correct the generative distribution with a data-dependent approximate like-lihood term. An autoregressive model over the output variables was pro-posed in [56]. The quality of generated samples is shown to improve by using an adversarial loss in [81]. There are also many different forms of the variational approximation to the posterior investigated in the past years, including normalizing flows [77, 117], graphical models [91], and models for temporal data [52, 53]. Generative adversarial networks (GANs) was originally introduced in [50]. Reviews of GANs and variants are provided in [54,93,154]. The original GANs are known to be unstable in training [40, 72], and a large amount of variants and improvements were proposed since then [5, 55, 94, 95, 113, 160]. In particular, Wasserstein GANs was developed in [5] and several variants were proposed [1, 157]. Early work illustrating the fundamental idea of diffusion models was presented in [135], which introduced the concept of progressively diffusing data into noise via a Markov chain and learning a reverse-time process to recover the data distribution. The practical success of diffusion models was significantly advanced by the work of Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Mod-els (DDPMs) developed in [63]. In [103], DDPMs were improved through better noise schedules and architectural refinements, leading to substantial gains in sample fidelity. Furthermore, the work [75] analyzed design choices in diffusion models and proposed principled parameterizations that improve training stability and efficiency. These advances have contributed to diffu-sion models surpassing GANs in many benchmark tasks, especially in terms of mode coverage and training robustness. The connection between DDPMs and score-based generative modeling represented by SDEs was established in [139]. Deterministic versions represented by ODEs were given [138]. Be-yond image generation, diffusion models have been successfully extended to conditional generation, super-resolution, text-to-image synthesis, and scien-tific applications. Notable examples include classifier-guided diffusion [32] and large-scale text-conditioned models such as latent diffusion models [121], which reduce computational cost by operating in a learned latent space. Flow matching is a simplified and improved framework for generative 5.6. REMARKS AND REFERENCES 261 modeling [2, 85, 89] compared to diffusion models and has seen various ap-plications of large-scale generation in speech [82], images [36], videos [111], robotics [18], and proteins [71]. It was shown that the linear version of Flow Matching can be seen as a certain limiting case of bridge matching in [87,131]. The idea of using vector field in flow matching is related to prob-ability density matching in [27, 51] which are based on likelihood. Recent improvements and extensions are presented in [60, 101, 147]. Flow matching has also been considered in the discrete-state scenarios in [25, 48] and on Riemann manifolds [26]. The flow matching framework was shown to be applicable to general continuous-time Markov processes [66]. Overall, the generative models including all the aforementioned vari-ants represent a flexible and theoretically grounded paradigm for generative modeling, with ongoing research aimed at accelerating sampling, improving scalability, and extending their applicability to broader data modalities. 262 5. GENERATIVE MODELS Appendix A

# A.1 Monte Carlo Integration 

Monte Carlo integration is a computationally feasible approach to approx-imate integrals in subsets of high-dimensional Euclidean spaces. For such integrals, classical numerical integration methods do not apply due to the curse of dimensionality issue arising form spatial discretization of the integral domains. Monte Carlo integration, on the other hand, does not need explicit spatial discretization. Instead, it draws sample points from the integration domain by following certain probability distribution, and evaluate the func-tion to be integrated at these sample points. Then a proper averaging of the function values at these points often yields reasonable approximation to the desired integral. In this section, we provide some details about the Monte Carlo integration methods and related mathematical properties. The basic idea of Monte Carlo integration is as follows. If Ω ⊂ Rd is bounded and h : Ω → R is integrable on Ω, then we can approximate its integral by ∫

h(x) dx ≈ |Ω|

h(x(i)), (A.1) where {x(i) ∈ Ω : i ∈ [N ]} are N samples drawn independently from the uniform distribution in Ω, and |Ω| is the volume of Ω in Rd. Next, we consider a slight generalization of this approach, and show the bias and variance of such Monte Carlo integral approximations. The generalization is known as importance sampling . Let Ω be a possibly unbounded subset of Rd and p a probability density function defined on Ω

with p(x) > 0 for all x ∈ Ω. Let h : Ω → R be square integrable in Ω, and

However, it is in general not possible to have p ∝ h, and the idea of importance sampling is to use a relatively simple probability distribution, such as the mixture of Gaussians, as p and make it as close to h as possible. The reason of setting p as a mixture of Gaussians is that one can easily sample points X(i)’s from p and evaluate h(X(i))/p (X(i)) to compute JNh in (A.2). This can be done by setting the means of these Gaussians to the points where h attains peak values, the standard deviations according to the landscape of h, and the mixing weights such that p is near proportional to h.Overall, we can see that by increasing the sample number N and setting 

p properly, the approximation error of JNh to the true value Ih can be made A.2. BANACH SPACE AND FIXED POINT THEORY 265 smaller. Meanwhile, the rate O(1 /N ) does not depend on the dimension d of the integral and hence such approximated integrals are potential to mitigate the curse of dimensionality issue in the cases where spatial discretization is utilized. We remark that the real-world situations of using Monte Carlo integra-tions can be more complicated. Specifically, we often need to use Monte Carlo integration to approximate the gradient of some objective functions of form 

Ih(θ) := 12

for some h : Rd × Rm → Rn, where θ ∈ Rm represents the neural network parameter to be optimized. As most network training algorithms are first-order optimization methods, we need to approximate

> ∫
> Ω

∇θh(x, θ )h(x, θ ) dx, 

which is a vector of dimension m. When we use Monte Carlo integration, the variances at different components of ∇θIh(θ) may vary and we need to be careful about choosing a suitable probability distribution p for importance sampling. The variances of the stochastic gradient approximating ∇θIh(θ)

play important roles in the convergence of the stochastic gradient method and alike, as shown in Chapter 2. 

# A.2 Banach Space and Fixed Point Theory 

In this section, we present a crush review of Banach spaces and fixed point theory. The results given in this section are fundamental to the theory of Bellman operators and Markov decision processes in Section 4.1. We first provide the definition of Banach spaces, which are complete normed linear spaces. We give the definitions of these terms and then the formal definition of Banach spaces afterwards. Then we present the Banach fixed point theorem. Finally, we show that B(X ) considered in 4.1 is a Banach space, on which Banach fixed point theorem can be applied. 

Definition A.2.1 (Linear space) . Let X be a set. We call X a linear space (also called vector space ) if it has summation and scalar-multiplication defined (i.e., x + y ∈ X and ax ∈ X for all x, y ∈ X and a ∈ R), such that for any x, y, z ∈ X and a, b ∈ R, the following statements hold. 1. x + y = y + x.2. (x + y) + z = x + ( y + z).3. There exists 0 ∈ X such that x + 0 = 0 + x for all x ∈ X.266 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

4. For any x ∈ X, there exists x′ ∈ X, called −x, such that x + x′ = 0 .5. a(bx ) = ( ab )x.6. 1 · x = x where 1 ∈ R.7. (a + b)x = ax + bx .8. a(x + y) = ax + ay .

Definition A.2.2 (Norm) . The function ∥ · ∥ : X → R is called a norm if for any x, y, z ∈ X and a ∈ R the following statements hold: 1. (Positive definite) ∥x∥ ≥ 0; and ∥x∥ = 0 iff x = 0 .2. (Homogeneous) ∥ax ∥ = |a|∥ x∥.3. (Triangle inequality) ∥x + y∥ ≤ ∥ x∥ + ∥y∥.If the linear space X has a norm, then we call (X, ∥ · ∥ ) a normed linear space .

Definition A.2.3 (Distance) . Let X be a set. We call d : X × X → R a

distance (or metric ) if for any x, y, z ∈ X, there are 1. d(x, y ) ≥ 0; and d(x, y ) = 0 iff x = y;2. d(x, y ) = d(y, x ); and 3. d(x, z ) ≤ d(x, y ) + d(y, z ) .If X is a linear space with norm ∥ · ∥ , then the function d : X × X → R

defined by d(x, y ) := ∥x − y∥ is a distance (this can be easily verified by checking the conditions in Definition A.2.3). In what follows, we always assume the distance d on a normed linear space (X, ∥ · ∥ ) is defined this way. 

Definition A.2.4 (Cauchy sequence) . A sequence {xk} in a normed linear space (X, ∥ · ∥ ) is called a Cauchy sequence if for any ϵ > 0, there exists 

K ∈ N, such that ∥xk − xj ∥ < ϵ for all k, j ≥ K.

Definition A.2.5 (Banach space) . We call a normed linear space (X, ∥ · ∥ )

complete if every Cauchy sequence in X converge to some point in X. Acomplete normed linear space (X, ∥ · ∥ ) is called a Banach space .We will simply write (X, ∥ · ∥ ) as X provided the norm ∥ · ∥ is clear from the context. Now we consider operations on Banach spaces. A.2. BANACH SPACE AND FIXED POINT THEORY 267 

Definition A.2.6 (Lipschitz, Contractive, and Fixed Point) . Let X be a Banach space. A mapping (operation) T : X → X is called Lipschitz with constant γ > 0 on X if 

∥T x − T ˆx∥ ≤ γ∥x − ˆx∥

for any x, ˆx ∈ X. If γ = 1 , we call T non-expansive . If γ ∈ (0 , 1) , we call T

a γ-contraction (or T is γ-contractive ). If x ∈ X satisfies T x = x, then x is called a fixed point of T .

Theorem A.2.7 (Banach fixed point theorem) . Let X be a Banach space and T : X → X a γ-contraction with some γ ∈ (0 , 1) . Then the following statements hold true: (i) T has a unique fixed point x∗ in X.(ii) For any x ∈ X, define sequentially xk+1 = T x k with x0 = x (this scheme of generating a sequence is called the fixed point iteration ). Then for any k ∈ N, there are 

∥xk+1 − xk∥ ≤ γ∥xk − xk−1∥, (A.4) 

and 

∥xk − x∗∥ ≤ γk∥x − x∗∥. (A.5) 

We call xk converges to x∗ at a linear rate of γ given (A.5) .Proof. We prove the two statements together. Let x ∈ X be arbitrary and 

x0 = x. Let {xk} be the sequence generated by the fixed point iteration using the γ-contractive T . Then (A.4) is obvious due to T . Furthermore, for any k ∈ N, there is 

∥xk+1 − xk∥ = ∥T x k − T x k−1∥≤ γ∥xk − xk−1∥≤ · · · ≤ γk∥x1 − x0∥.

Then, for any j ∈ N, we have 

∥xk+j − xk∥ ≤ ∥ xk+j − xk+j−1∥ + · · · + ∥xk+1 − xk∥≤ (γk+j−1 + · · · + γk)∥x1 − x0∥≤ γk

1 − γ ∥x1 − x0∥.

Hence, ∥xk+j − xk∥ can be arbitrarily small as long as k is sufficiently large despite of j. Therefore {xk} is a Cauchy sequence in X. Since X is complete, 268 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

we know xk converges to some x∗ ∈ X. Moreover, we know lim k→∞ ∥T x k −

T x ∗∥ = lim k→∞ ∥xk+1 − x∗∥ = 0 , and hence ∥T x ∗ − x∗∥ = lim k→∞ ∥T x k −

xk∥ = 0 , which implies that x∗ = T x ∗, i.e., x∗ is a fixed point of T .Now we have 

∥xk − x∗∥ = ∥T x k−1 − T x ∗∥≤ γ∥xk−1 − x∗∥≤ · · · ≤ γk∥x0 − x∗∥

= γk∥x − x∗∥,

since x0 = x, which proves (A.5). If x∗ and ˆx are both fixed points of T but different, then ∥x∗ − ˆx∥ > 0.However 

∥x∗ − ˆx∥ = ∥T x ∗ − T ˆx∥ ≤ γ∥x∗ − ˆx∥ < ∥x∗ − ˆx∥,

which is a contradiction. Hence x∗ is the unique fixed point of T .Now we consider the space B(X ) in Section 4.1. Recall that X is a set, which can be a set of finitely many or countably many elements, or a subset of some Euclidean space. Then we define 

B(X ) :=

. (A.6) Namely, B(X ) is the set of all uniformly bounded real-valued functions defined on X . We also define the ∞-norm on B(X ):

> x∈X

|v(x)|. (A.7) Then it is clear that (B(X ), ∥ · ∥ ∞) is a normed linear space. Next, we want to show that (B(X ), ∥ ˙∥∞) is complete. To this end, let {vk} be a Cauchy sequence in (B(X ), ∥ · ∥ ∞). That is, for any ϵ > 0, there exists K ∈ N, such that ∥vk − vj ∥∞ < ϵ for all k, j ≥ K.Therefore, for any x ∈ X , we have 

|vk(x) − vj (x)| ≤ ∥ vk − vj ∥∞ < ϵ (A.8) for all k, j ≥ K. This implies that {vk(x)} is a Cauchy sequence of numbers in R. Hence {vk(x)} converges and has a limit, denoted by v(x), in R. Define 

v : X → R as such limit at every x ∈ X . Then, for every x ∈ X , we notice that by letting j → ∞ in (A.8), we have

ϵ + ∥vk∥∞ < ∞, which implies that v ∈ B(X ). To conclude, we have that 

{vk} is convergent in ∞-norm and the limit v is in B(X ). Therefore, B(X )

is complete. By Definition A.2.5, B(X ) is a Banach space. 

# A.3 Basics of Information Theory 

In this section, we provide a crush review of the basics of information theory and its relations to probability. 

Definition A.3.1 (Entropy) . Let X be a random variable following a prob-ability p on Rn. Then the entropy of X is given by 

H(X) := EX∼p[− log p(X)] = −

> ∫

p(x) log p(x) dx. (A.10) If X is a discrete random variable, i.e., the set X of all possible values of X

is finite or countably infinite, then the integral in (A.10) is replaced with the sum over X . We use integral throughout this section unless a formula only applies to summations. Notice that we always use the convention 0 log 0 = 0 

given that lim z→0+ z log z = 0 .The entropy of a random variable (or equivalently its probability distri-bution) quantifies the average level of uncertainty. The entropy is invariant to the mean (expectation) of the random variable, namely, if the mean of the random variable shifts, the entropy does not change. The variance and dimensionality of the random variables are main factors to determine the entropy, but there are also other factors that affect the value of entropy for general random variables. If X is a discrete random variable, then H(X) ≥ 0. To see this, we notice that p(x) log p(x) ∈ [−e−1, 0] at every x ∈ X since p(x) ∈ [0 , 1] .If X is a continuous random variable, then H(X) can be negative. Aspecial and important example is the entropy of d-dimensional Gaussian random variable X ∼ N (μ, Σ) . One can check that the entropy of X is 

H(X) = d

2 (1 + log 2 π) + 12 log det(Σ) .

As we can see, H(X) → −∞ if Σ = σ2In and σ → 0.270 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

Definition A.3.2 (Conditional entropy) . Let X and Y be two random variables with joint probability p on Rn × Rm. Then the conditional entropy 

of Y given X is 

H(Y |X) := −

> ∫∫

p(x, y ) log p(x, y )

pX (x) dxdy, (A.11) where pX is the marginal probability of X.Let H(X, Y ) be the joint entropy of (X, Y ), namely, 

H(X, Y ) = −

> ∫∫

p(x, y ) log p(x, y )dxdy. 

Then there is 

H(Y |X) = H(X, Y ) − H(X).

It is easy to verify that H(Y |X) = 0 if and only if Y is completely determined by X (i.e., Y is a function of X). We also have H(Y |X) = H(Y ) if and only if X and Y are independent. 

Definition A.3.3 (Bregman divergence) . Let F be a convex functional with first variation δF . Then the Bregman divergence associated with F is defined by 

DF (g, f ) := F (g) − F (f ) −

> ∫

δF (f ) · (g − f ),

for any two functions f and g.Bregman divergences are mostly used in finite dimensional cases in prac-tice. In these cases, F : Ω → R is a convex differentiable function and 

Ω ⊂ Rn is a convex set. Then the Bregman divergence associated with F is defined by 

DF (y, x ) := F (y) − F (x) − ∇ F (x) · (y − x),

for any x, y ∈ Ω. An illustration of the Bregman divergence DF (y, x ) in the finite dimensional case is given in Figure A.1. Bregman divergences can be defined when F is not differentiable, in which case ∇F (x) can be replaced with any specified subgradient of F at x.This subgradient should be clearly specified in the definition of the Bregman divergence. We will not consider this extension here. A simple example of Bregman divergence is F (x) = 12 |x|2 for x ∈ Rn. In this case, one can verify that 

DF (y, x ) = 12 |y − x|2.

It is easy to verify the following properties of Bregman divergence as-sociated with F , where F is either a functional or function. We use the notations with F being a function below. A.3. BASICS OF INFORMATION THEORY 271

> F(x) + ∇F(x)·(y−x)

Figure A.1: An illustration of the Bregman divergence associated with F

(the convex curve). The tangent line with gradient ∇F (x) passing through 

(x, F (x)) is shown by the dashed line. At y, the function F and the tangent line take values F (y) and F (x)+ ∇F (x)·(y−x), respectively. Their difference (the length of the vertical solid line above the point y) is the Bregman divergence DF (y, x ) = F (y) − F (x) − ∇ F (x) · (y − x). Due to convexity, F

is on or above the tangent line everywhere, and hence DF (y, x ) ≥ 0 for all 

x, y .1. Non-negativity: DF (y, x ) ≥ 0 for all x, y because F is convex. If F is strictly convex, then DF (y, x ) = 0 if and only if y = x.2. Convexity in its first argument: DF (·, x ) is (strictly) convex for every 

x if F is (strictly) convex. 3. Convexity in the first argument: for any x, y, z and λ ∈ [0 , 1] , there is 

DF ((1 − λ)y + λz, x ) ≤ (1 − λ)DF (y, x ) + λD F (z, x ).

However, Bregman divergences are not standard distance: They are not symmetric and do not satisfy the triangle inequality. 

Definition A.3.4 (Kullback–Leibler divergence) . The Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between two probability distributions p and q is defined as the Bregman divergence associated with the entropy functional 

F (ρ) := −

> ∫

ρ(x) log ρ(x)dx 

for any ρ ∈ P := {ρ : Rn → R : ρ ≥ 0, ∫ ρ = 1 }. More precisely, KL (p, q ) := DF (p, q ) = 

> ∫

p(x) log p(x)

q(x) dx 

If F is the entropy of discrete random variables defined over X , then KL (p, q ) := DF (p, q ) = ∑

> x∈X

p(x) log p(x)

q(x)

for any probability distributions p and q on X .272 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

Since the KL divergence is a special case of the Bregman divergence, it follows all properties of the latter. In particular, we should be always aware that the KL divergence is non-negative and KL (p, q ) = 0 if and only if p = q

everywhere (because when F is chosen as the entropy of p, we know F is strictly convex). Moreover, the KL divergence is not a distance as it is not symmetric, i.e., KL (p, q ) is not equal to KL (q, p ) even if they both exist, and the KL divergence does not satisfy the triangle inequality. Next, we take a closer look of the KL divergence and check more of its properties, as the KL divergence is prevalent in deep learning research and applications due to its ease of use when measuring the discrepancy between two probability distributions. Assume that p and q are two probability distributions on Rn. The KL divergence between them does not always exist due to the presence of loga-rithm, particularly when p and q do not have the same support. If p(x) > 0 and q(x) = 0 at some x ∈ Rn, then 

p(x) log p(x)

q(x) = + ∞.

If the set of such points has positive measure in Rn, then KL (p, q ) is not defined because by its definition the integral does not exist. In this case, we cannot use the KL divergence to measure the difference between p and q,nor take operations such as gradient of KL (p, q ) either. This happens when 

p(x) > 0 occurs outside of the support of q.If p(x) = 0 and q(x) > 0 at any x ∈ Rn, then there is no such evaluation issue since 

p(x) log p(x)

q(x) = 0 .

In this case, the KL divergence is defined because the integral in the KL is finite. In summary, the use of KL (p, q ) is restricted to the cases where the support of p is contained in the support of q. Otherwise, we may use KL (q, p )

or other distances or divergences between p and q to measure their difference. We need to be particularly cautious about the aforementioned matter when we use KL divergence in deep learning models. More specificially, recall that in several generative models in Chapter 5, we use KL divergence to measure the distance between a given data probability distribution pdata 

(usually in the form of a dataset D = {xi ∈ Rd : i ∈ [M ]}, where xi’s are samples drawn independently from pdata ) and a generated or pushforward probability pθ, where θ refers to the parameter of some deep neural network to be trained and pθ is the probability induced by this network. In this case, we often want to find the optimal parameter θ by solving 

min 

> θ
> {

KL (pdata , p θ) = −H(pdata ) −

> ∫

pdata (x) log pθ(x) dx 

> }

.A.3. BASICS OF INFORMATION THEORY 273 Since pdata is unknown and fixed, H(pdata ) is independent of θ, and the problem above reduces to 

max 

> θ
> ∫

pdata (x) log pθ(x) dx ≈ max 

> θ

1

M

> M∑
> i=1

log pθ(xi),

where the approximation is obtained by Monte Carlo integration using the fact xi ∼ pdata . We can see that this requires pθ(xi) > 0 for all xi ∈ D .Meanwhile, pθ often presents some continuity or smoothness property due to the use of neural networks. Therefore, it is very likely to have pθ(x) > 0

at x outside of the support of pdata . This will not cause computation issue, however, it may infer or generate samples that are not from pdata . This can cause inaccurate estimation of θ and even incorrect solution to the target problem. In such cases, proper modifications of the method or alternative approaches may be needed. When the involved probabilities are of special types, KL divergence can be used without danger and often have closed forms. We present two exam-ples of such cases. 

Example A.3.5 (KL divergence between two Gaussians) . Let p1 and p2 be two Gaussian probability distributions on Rn. Specifically, 

p1 = N (μ1, Σ1), p2 = N (μ2, Σ2),

where μ1, μ 2 ∈ Rn, and Σ1, Σ2 ∈ Rn×n are symmetric positive definite. The KL divergence from p1 to p2 is defined as KL (p1, p 2) =

It is easy to verify that KL (p1, p 2) = 0 if and only if λ1 = λ2.There are several variants of KL divergences that have symmetry and become well defined when p and q do not share the same support. The most notable one is the Jensen–Shannon divergence given as follows. 

Definition A.3.7 (Jensen–Shannon divergence) . The Jensen–Shannon (JS) divergence between two probability distributions p and q is JS (p, q ) := 12

> (

KL (p, r ) + KL (q, r )

> )

(A.18) where r = ( p + q)/2 is the pointwise average of p and q.Note that r is also a probability distribution and its support contains both supports of p and q. Therefore, JS (p, q ) is always well defined and takes a finite value. Indeed, one can check that JS (p, q ) ∈ [0 , log 2] for any p and 

q. The maximum value 2 log 2 occurs when the supports of p and q do not overlap. Note that, however, this is not necessarily an advantage because the generalized gradient of JS (p, q ) with respect to p or q becomes zero and we cannot update any of them (more precisely, the parameters of the neural network used to generate or pushforward them) using gradient-based optimization algorithms in practice. Nevertheless, it is easy to check that JS (p, q ) ≥ 0 and JS (p, q ) = 0 if and only if p = q. Moreover, unlike the KL divergence, the JS divergence is symmetric, namely, JS (p, q ) = JS (q, p ) for any probability distributions p

and q on Rn.Despite the simple variation of the JS divergence from the KL divergence and the few new properties, JS divergence is more difficult to use as it lacks a closed form for many typical probability distributions. For example, there is no closed form of the JS divergence between two Gaussian distributions like the KL divergence in (A.16), because r in (A.18) becomes a mixture of two Gaussian distributions and the KL divergence from p (or q) to r does not have a closed form. There exists a distance to measure the difference between two probability distributions with mathematical elegance and overcome the issues of KL and JS divergences above. It is called the Wasserstein distance, which we define below. 

Definition A.3.8 (Wasserstein distance) . Let P := {p : Rn → R : p(x) ≥

0, ∀ x ∈ Rn, ∫ p(x)dx = 1 } be the space of probability distributions on Rn.276 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

For s ∈ [1 , ∞), the Wasserstein-s distance between p, q ∈ P is defined by 

Ws(p, q ) := inf 

> π∈Π( p,q )
> ( ∫

|x − y|sπ(x, y ) dx 

> )1/s

, (A.19) where Π( p, q ) is the set of joint probability distributions on Rn × Rn with p

and q as the marginal distributions, namely, 

Π( p, q ) := 

> {

π : Rd × Rd → [0 , ∞) : p(x) = ∫ π(x, y ) dy, q(y) = ∫ π(x, y ) dx, ∀ x, y ∈ Rd}

.

For s = ∞, the Wasserstein-∞ distance between p, q ∈ P is defined by 

W∞(p, q ) := lim  

> s→∞

Ws(p, q ).

The optimal solution π∗ to (A.19) is called an optimal transport from p to 

q.As an example, the Wasserstein-2 distance between two Gaussian distri-butions p1 = N (μ1, Σ1) and p2 = N (μ2, Σ2) on Rd is shown to be 

W2(p1, p 2) = 

> (

|μ1 − μ2|2 + tr 

> (

Σ1 + Σ 2 − 2(Σ 1/21 Σ2Σ1/21 )1/2)) 1/2

.

Wasserstein distance exhibits many important mathematical properties. A basic one is that Ws is a distance (see Definition A.2.3) in rigorous math-ematical sense. Namely, Ws satisfies the following properties: 

• Positive definiteness: Ws(p, q ) ≥ 0 for all p, q ∈ P ; and Ws(p, q ) = 0 if and only if p = q almost everywhere on Rn.

• Symmetry: Ws(p, q ) = Ws(q, p ) for any p, q ∈ P .

• Triangle inequality: Ws(p, q ) ≤ Ws(p, r ) + Ws(r, q ) for any p, q, r ∈ P .In addition, Ws(p, q ) is well defined even if the supports of p and q are different. This is a significant advantage over KL divergence. Moreover, Wasserstein distances represent many useful physical interpretations. For example, the Wasserstein-1 distance, which is also called the earth mover’s distance , measures the optimal cost of moving probability mass p to q where the cost of moving a unit mass from location x to y is proportional to their distance |x − y|.Wasserstein distances is a fundamental concept in the field of optimal transport, the theory of which has been extensively studied in the past decades. Optimal transport theory connects to many branches of mathe-matics, including geometric analysis, partial differential equations, proba-bility theory, and optimal control. Many important results are presented in [3, 125, 151]. A.3. BASICS OF INFORMATION THEORY 277 However, the main issue with Wasserstein distances is that they are dif-ficult to compute numerically. This is due to the constraints involved in the definition of Wasserstein distances and high dimensionality of spaces they are used in practice. In simple cases where p and q are probability distri-butions on small finite set X , they are effectively normalized histograms, and (A.19) reduces to a linear programming problem that can be solved by existing optimization algorithms. The computation can be significantly accelerated by adding the entropy regularization of the joint probability distribution to the objective function in (A.19), and the solution can be ob-tained solving the dual form of the problem using matrix re-scaling method called the Sinkhorn algorithm. However, this technique does not apply to high-dimensional problems or problems defined in contnuous spaces such as 

Rn due to computation infeasibility. In the past years, there are also deep learning methods developed to compute Wasserstein distances. For exam-ple, the problem (A.19) can be converted to its dual form, then the dual variables are parameterized as deep neural networks and trained to solve the dual problem, yielding the solution to (A.19). Some of these results are included in [108]. However, there is yet a computationally effective means to approximate Wasserstein distances accurately for deep learning models in general. This has hindered the extensive use of Wasserstein distances in deep learning. The last concept to be introduced in this section is a classic one called mutual information. Unlike any of the divergences and distances above, mutual information exploits the joint probability distribution between ran-dom variables to measure their dependency. The formal definition of mutual information is given as follows. 

Definition A.3.9 (Mutual information) . Let X and Y be two random variables with joint probability distribution p(x, y ). The muutal information 

between X and Y is defined by MI (X, Y ) = KL (p, p X pY ) = 

> ∫∫

p(x, y ) p(x, y )

pX (x)pY (y) dxdy, (A.20) where pX and pY are the marginal probability distributions of X and Y ,respectively. Notice that, the mutual information is non-negative as it is defined as the KL divergence from the joint probability distribution p to the prod-uct of the marginal probability distributions pX pY . Moreover, we can see MI (X, Y ) = 0 if and only if p = pX pY , namely, X and Y are independent. Mutual information is also symmetric. However, we remark that mutual information is not a distance in any sense. Nevertheless, large MI (X, Y ) is an indication that X and Y are strongly dependent. Indeed, we have the following relation between the mutual information of two random variables 278 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

and their (conditional) entropy H(X|Y ):MI (X, Y ) = 

> ∫

p(x, y ) log p(x, y )

pX (x)pY (y) dxdy 

=

> ∫

p(x, y ) log pX|Y (x|y)

pX (x) dxdy 

=

> ∫

p(x, y ) log pX|Y (x|y) dxdy −

> ∫

pX (x) log pX (x) dx 

= H(X) − H(X|Y ).

This again implies that MI (X, Y ) = 0 if X and Y are independent in which case H(X|Y ) = H(X). The other extreme is that X is completely deter-mined by Y (namely, X is a function of Y ), which implies H(X|Y ) = 0 and MI (X, Y ) = H(X).

# A.4 Basics of Stochastic Differential Equations 

Stochastic differential equations (SDEs) have become more prevalent in problem formulation, modeling and derivations in modern deep learning research and applications. For example, we have seen some extensive uses of SDEs in diffusion models in Section 5.3. In this section, we provide a brief review of some basics about SDEs. In particular, we cover the definitions of Wiener processes, stochastic integrals and differentials, the Itô formula, and the Fokker–Planck equations. Consider a particle (as a random walker) moving on the real line R. At time t = 0 , it is at the origin. Then it moves with step size ∆x in every unit time ∆t. Namely, it moves to its left (location changes by −∆x) or its right (location changes by ∆x), with probability 12 in either case, in every ∆t.Let X(t) denote the location of the particle at time t = n∆t after moving 

n steps. To study the probability distribution of X(t), we consider Xi ∼

Bernoulli ( 12 ) which are independent for i = 1 , 2, . . . , and hence Xi = 1 or 0

with probability 12 in either case. It is clear that E[Xi] = 12 and Var[ Xi] = 14 .Let Sn := ∑ni=1 Xi, then Sn is the number of times the particle moved right in the first n steps, and n − Sn is the number of times it moved left. We see that E[Sn] = n 

> 2

and Var[ Sn] = n 

> 4

X(t) = Sn∆x − (n − Sn)∆ x = (2 Sn − n)∆ x. 

Therefore, E[X(t)] = 0 and 

Var[ X(t)] = (∆ x)2 Var[2 Sn − n] = (∆ x)24 Var[ Sn] = (∆ x)2n = (∆ x)2

∆t t. A.4. BASICS OF STOCHASTIC DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS 279 Without loss of generality, we assume (∆ x)2

which implies that X(t) ∼ N (0 , t ). In other words, if the particle is moving in the sense of continuous time, then its location X(t) ∼ N (0 , t ). This yields the definition of the Wiener process , also commonly called the Brownian motion , as follows. 

Definition A.4.1 (Wiener process) . A real-valued stochastic process W :[0 , ∞) → R is called a Wiener process if 1. Start from the origin: W (0) = 0 almost surely; 2. Independent increment: for any 0 < t 1 < t 2 < · · · < t k, the random variables 

W (t1), W (t2) − W (t1), . . . , W (tk) − W (tk−1)

are independent; 3. Normal distribution of increments: for any t > s ≥ 0, there is W (t) −

W (s) ∼ N (0 , t − s); and 4. Continuous path: t 7 → W (t) is continuous in t.Notice that E[W (t)] = 0 and Var[ W (t)] = E[W (t)2] = t for any t as 

W (t) ∼ N (0 , t ).To rigorously define SDEs, we need a series of mathematical concepts from measure theory. We only go through them in brief below and refer interested to [38, 93, 106] for more details. Let (Ω , Γ, μ ) be a probability space, where Ω is taken as Rd here, Γ is a 

σ-algebra on Ω, and μ is a probability measure such that all elements of Γ

are measurable under μ. Here a σ-algebra of Ω is defined as a set of subsets of Ω, and it contains the empty set ∅ and is closed under complement and 280 APPENDIX A. SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIALS 

countable union of its elements in Ω. Let A be a collection of subsets of 

Ω, we call Γ( A) the σ-algebra generated by A if Γ( A) is the intersection of all σ-algebras of Ω containing A. It can be shown that Γ( A) is indeed a 

σ-algebra as well. As random variables can be considered equivalent to μ-measurable functions, we can also define Γ( {Xα : α ∈ A} ) as the σ-algebra generated by the random variables {Xα : α ∈ A} in Ω, where A is an index set which can be infinite, and Xα here refers to the set of all pre-images of Lebesgue measurable sets of R under the mapping Xα.We denote W(t) := Γ( {W (s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ t}) the σ-algebra generated by the Wiener process up to time t. We also call W(t) the history of the Wiener process W up to time t. We let the generated σ-algebra W+(t) := Γ( {W (s)−W (t) : s ≥ t}) denote the future of the Wiener process W beyond time t. We call the family of σ-algebras, denoted by {F (t) ⊂ Γ : t ≥ 0},non-anticipating (also called adapted) with respect to W if (i) F(s) ⊂ F (t)

for all 0 ≤ s < t ; (ii) W(t) ⊂ F (t) for all t ≥ 0; and (iii) F(t) is independent of W+(t) for all t ≥ 0. We also call F(t) a filtration up to time t. We can informally interpret F(t) as the σ-algebra that “contains all information up to time t”, in the sense that all random variables generated by the Wiener process up to time t and some initial random variables independent of the Wiener process are measurable under F(t) for any t.We call a real-valued stochastic process G non-anticipating with respect to F if G(t) is F(t)-measurable for any t ≥ 0. In addition, we denote by 

L2(0 , T ) the space of all real-valued non-anticipating stochastic process G

with E[∫ T 

> 0

|G(t)|2dt ] < ∞. Similarly, we denote by L1(0 , T ) the space of all real-valued non-anticipating stochastic process F with E[∫ T 

> 0

The equation (A.30) has appeared several times in this book, such as in the Neural ODE method in Section 3.3 and the flow matching method in Section 5.5. As a side note, continuity equations generally refer to the class of equations that follow certain conservation laws, and (A.30) is a particular example that conserves probability. Bibliography
